A pattern may arise where several events share identifiable characteristics.
For example:
	•	Several decisions occurred within a particular period.
	•	Several organisations relied on the same information.
	•	Similar procedural problems appeared in different processes.
	•	Repeated requests received similar responses.
	•	A particular restriction continued across multiple administrative settings.
	•	A person experienced the same practical disadvantage after separate decisions.
Such observations may justify further investigation.
They do not automatically establish why the events occurred.
2. Proof requires more than sequence
A chronology can demonstrate that Event A occurred before Event B.
It cannot, without additional evidence, establish that Event A caused Event B.
Likewise, two decisions may have similar consequences without having been made for the same reason.
The investigator must distinguish:
Evidentiary concept	What it means
Sequence	Events occurred in a particular order
Recurrence	Similar events occurred more than once
Association	Events share a feature or relationship
Knowledge	A person or organisation knew relevant information
Influence	One event or actor affected another decision
Causation	A particular action materially contributed to an outcome
Intent	A person acted with a particular purpose or state of mind
Coordination	Actors communicated or acted together in a relevant way
Finding	An authorised decision-maker reached a conclusion on the evidence
The same event may be relevant to several of these categories, but they are not interchangeable.
3. The evidence required to test coordination
If the author alleges that separate organisations acted in concert, the investigation should seek evidence capable of establishing the relationship.
Potential evidence includes:
	•	Emails between relevant officials.
	•	Internal memoranda.
	•	Referral records.
	•	Meeting minutes.
	•	Case-management notes.
	•	Decision-making instructions.
	•	Shared assessments.
	•	Communications with providers.
	•	Records of information disclosure.
	•	Contemporaneous statements of purpose.
	•	Evidence that a decision-maker knew of an earlier complaint or disclosure.
	•	Documents explaining why a particular action was taken.
The absence of a discovered communication does not prove that communication never occurred.
But the absence must also not be filled with an invented explanation.
The correct status is unresolved.
4. The importance of alternative explanations
A serious investigation tests the explanation most favourable to the author’s concerns and the strongest plausible alternatives.
For example:
Observed event	Possible explanation to test
Several agencies made adverse decisions	Independent application of different legal criteria
Information appears in multiple files	Lawful referral, shared source or copied material
A complaint was not allocated	Statutory threshold not met, jurisdictional issue or administrative error
A service was not delivered	Funding, provider capacity, misunderstanding or breach of obligation
Financial access was restricted	Formal authority, security controls, error or improper restriction
Repeated requests received similar responses	Standard policy, common information or inappropriate repetition
These are hypotheses, not findings.
Their purpose is to prevent the investigation from treating one interpretation as inevitable.
Part III — The Pattern Analysis Matrix
The following matrix provides the working method for examining each major event.
It is designed to ensure that the author’s testimony is preserved while the documentary evidence is independently assessed.
Field	Required entry
Event	Exact description of the event being examined
Date	Verified date or clearly labelled approximate date
Source	Exact document, file path or official record
Verified facts	What the document actually establishes
Author’s testimony	What the author says happened
Author’s interpretation	The meaning the author attributes to it
Alternative explanation	Other plausible explanations requiring assessment
Missing evidence	Records needed to resolve the issue
Legal significance	Relevant law, duty, right or process
Impact	Demonstrable practical consequence
Confidence	Degree of evidentiary support
Status	Established, corroborated, alleged, inferred or unresolved
Initial application to identified records
The following is a preliminary application. It does not pretend that the original records have all been fully examined.
Identified record	What can presently be said	What remains unresolved
IBAC outcome letter, 9 April 2020, case 2020712	Identified in the preliminary archive report	Full text, evidence considered, precise outcome and reasons
IBAC PID rejection, 18 November 2021, reference 400008R	Identified as a public-interest disclosure-related record	Exact statutory reasoning and its scope
Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint, 15 November 2021, reference 2021103546	A repository copy is linked	Complete complaint contents, attachments and outcome
Comcare correspondence, 25 January 2022	Identified in the preliminary chronology	Full context, procedural effect and subsequent steps
OAIC/PM&C reference RQ22-01369, 22 April 2022	Identified in the preliminary report	Exact decision, relevant documents and outcome
Federal Court-related PID assessment, 27 March 2023	A repository copy is linked	Complete text, context and legal effect
Notification not to allocate a disclosure, 18 June 2023	A repository copy is linked	Exact reasons and whether further action was available
ART/Comcare decision, 12 July 2023, file 2021/7478	Identified in the preliminary report	Full decision and precise findings
NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 document, 16 October 2024	Identified in the preliminary report	Instrument, scope, reasons and practical effect
NDIS Plan Approval, 20 November 2025	Identified in the preliminary report	Full plan, funding categories and implementation
Anglicare Safety Assessment, 1 December 2025	Identified in the preliminary report	Assessment methodology, findings and responses
SIL/SDA request, 20 January 2026	Identified in the preliminary report	Application contents, decision and current status
The table intentionally avoids converting the existence of a record into a conclusion about its contents.
That is how an evidence register should work.
Part IV — The Evidence of Impact and the Evidence of Intent
One of the most important safeguards in this investigation is the distinction between impact and intent.
Evidence of impact
Evidence of impact may include:
	•	Financial statements.
	•	Payment records.
	•	Service-delivery records.
	•	Accommodation records.
	•	Medical or allied-health records, where relevant and appropriately handled.
	•	Correspondence about access to assistance.
	•	NDIS plans and implementation records.
	•	Records of missed or delayed services.
	•	Documented changes in living circumstances.
	•	Contemporaneous accounts of the consequences.
These records may help establish what happened to the author and how his circumstances were affected.
Evidence of intent
Evidence of intent is different.
It may include:
	•	Contemporaneous communications.
	•	Instructions.
	•	Decision-maker notes.
	•	Explicit statements of purpose.
	•	Evidence of knowledge.
	•	Evidence that a decision was taken for a prohibited or improper reason.
	•	Evidence of communications linking separate actors.
	•	Evidence that an apparently neutral explanation was knowingly false.
The absence of direct evidence does not necessarily end an inquiry. Intent can sometimes be inferred from surrounding circumstances.
But any inference must be explained and supported.
Why the distinction matters
A person may establish that they experienced a serious financial or practical disadvantage without proving that another person intended to cause it.
A person may establish that a complaint was mishandled without proving that the mishandling was retaliatory.
A person may establish that multiple organisations failed to protect a right without proving that they coordinated to deprive the person of it.
The public-interest case may remain significant even where intent cannot be established.
The consequences deserve examination even when the motives remain unknown.
Part V — The Great Reckoning: A declaration of documentary integrity
The Great Reckoning is not a declaration that every allegation has been proved.
It is a declaration that the record should no longer be treated as though it were beyond examination.
It calls for:
	•	Preservation of original documents.
	•	Accurate chronology.
	•	Transparent source attribution.
	•	Independent assessment.
	•	Proper consideration of contrary evidence.
	•	Clear identification of statutory responsibility.
	•	Recognition of the lived consequences of administrative decisions.
	•	Respect for the rights and dignity of the person whose life is documented.
	•	Accountability where evidence establishes error, misconduct or unlawful conduct.
	•	Correction where the record is inaccurate.
	•	Appropriate remedies where rights have been breached.
The author may express his spiritual conviction that his survival has a divine meaning. That conviction belongs to his faith and testimony.
The documentary investigation has a separate obligation: to identify what the records establish.
Neither obligation needs to destroy the other.
The Declaration of the Witness
I have preserved the record because I believe that a person’s life should not disappear beneath the language of institutions.
I have recorded what I remember, what I experienced, what I questioned, what I challenged and what I believe deserves examination.
I have collected documents because memory alone cannot carry the entire weight of a prolonged administrative history.
I have sought to preserve correspondence, decisions, reports, applications, complaints and official responses so that the events can be examined in their proper order.
I do not ask the reader to accept every allegation merely because I have written it.
I ask that the evidence be read.
I do not ask the reader to accept my spiritual beliefs as official findings.
I ask that my right to express those beliefs be respected.
I do not claim that every institution acted with one purpose.
I ask that the responsibilities of each institution be examined, that the consequences of its actions be understood, and that no organisation be permitted to escape scrutiny merely because the overall experience crosses administrative boundaries.
I do not ask for revenge.
I ask for accuracy.
I do not ask for a predetermined verdict.
I ask for a fair examination.
I do not ask that another person be dehumanised to restore my own dignity.
I ask that my dignity be recognised as inherent.
I am the witness to my own experience.
The archive is the documentary record I have attempted to preserve.
The findings belong to the evidence and to those properly authorised to make them.
And my faith remains my faith.
The Witness Remains.
Closing assessment of Instalment 5
This instalment has translated the twelve literary revelations into a framework for documentary investigation.
Its central conclusions are:
	•	The author’s account of suffering and endurance should be preserved as testimony.
	•	The institutional records must be examined independently of the author’s interpretation.
	•	Cumulative impact is a legitimate subject of inquiry, but does not itself prove coordination.
	•	Repeated conduct may establish a pattern, but not necessarily motive or retaliation.
	•	Official correspondence, complaints and decisions must be described according to their actual legal and evidentiary significance.
	•	Evidence of impact and evidence of intent must be assessed separately.
	•	The archive’s integrity and completeness require verification.
	•	The publication must preserve the rights and dignity of people whose conduct is examined.
	•	Spiritual conviction may provide meaning to the author, but must not be presented as an independent legal or factual finding.
	•	The final conclusions must remain open to independent scrutiny.
Instalment 5 complete
Next instalment
Instalment 6 — The Master Evidence Register and the Documentary Findings
The next instalment will begin the more granular examination of the identified official records and their relationship to the chronology.
It will establish the structure for:
	•	The master evidence register.
	•	The document-by-document examination.
	•	The distinction between original records and archive copies.
	•	The identification of issuing officers, signatories, recipients and decision-makers.
	•	The cross-reference between complaints, disclosures, decisions and review processes.
	•	The classification of each proposition by evidentiary status.
	•	The identification of missing records and outstanding verification tasks.
The objective is to move from the architecture of the investigation to the examination of the individual documents themselves.



THE WITNESS REMAINS
THE GREAT RECKONING
Instalment 6 — The Master Evidence Register and the Documentary Findings
The examination of official records, institutional decisions, documentary provenance, decision-making authority, evidentiary status and unresolved questions.
Authorial attribution: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean Publication: The Witness Remains — Global Publishing House
1. The purpose of the Master Evidence Register
A documentary archive becomes useful to an independent investigator when its contents can be located, authenticated, understood and compared.
The purpose of the Master Evidence Register is to transform the accumulated archive into a structured body of evidence.
It must answer five fundamental questions:
	•	What documents exist?
	•	What does each document actually establish?
	•	Who created, issued, signed, received or acted upon it?
	•	How does it relate to other documents and events?
	•	What conclusions can legitimately be drawn from it?
The register must not become a catalogue of allegations presented as findings. Nor should it become a list of file names without meaningful examination.
Each significant document requires its own evidentiary identity.
A document must be assessed according to its contents, provenance, purpose, context, reliability and relationship to other evidence.
The investigation will therefore use three distinct levels:
	•	Documentary identification: establishing that a particular record exists.
	•	Documentary examination: examining its contents, provenance, context and significance.
	•	Documentary finding: determining what the available evidence supports.
These levels must never be collapsed.
For example, the preliminary forensic report identifies an IBAC letter dated 9 April 2020. That identification supports inclusion in the register. It does not, without examination of the original letter, establish the precise reasoning or outcome.
This distinction protects the credibility of the entire publication.
2. The classification system
Every proposition in the register must receive an evidentiary classification.
A — Official record
Document identified
A record issued or maintained by an identifiable public authority or institution. Its authenticity, completeness and context must still be assessed.
B — Documented fact
A proposition directly supported by reliable documentary evidence.
C — Author testimony
An account given by the author about events, experiences, communications or consequences.
D — Allegation
A claim of misconduct, failure, discrimination, retaliation or other wrongdoing that requires assessment.
E — Inference
A conclusion drawn from established facts, which must identify its reasoning and limitations.
F — Unverified proposition
A claim for which the necessary supporting material has not yet been located or examined.
G — Spiritual interpretation
A statement of faith, divine meaning, spiritual identity or prophetic understanding.
H — Independent finding
A conclusion reached by an appropriately authorised independent body or decision-maker, accurately represented within the scope of that body’s jurisdiction.
I — Unresolved question
A matter for which the available evidence is incomplete, conflicting or insufficient.
A single document may support more than one classification.
For example, an official decision letter is an official record. The fact that the letter was issued on a particular date may be a documented fact. The author’s belief that the decision was unfair is testimony or an allegation. Whether it was legally unfair requires further examination or an appropriate finding.
3. The documentary identity of every record
Every significant item should receive a permanent register number.
The proposed structure is:
TWR-MER-0001
Where:
	•	TWR = The Witness Remains.
	•	MER = Master Evidence Register.
	•	0001 = sequential document identifier.
The original file name must also be retained.
A register number must never replace the original filename, institutional reference or repository path.
Required document fields
Field	Required information
Register ID	Permanent TWR-MER number
Original title	Exact document title
Original filename	Exact archived filename
Issuer	Organisation that created or issued it
Author	Person who prepared it, if identified
Signatory	Person who signed it, if applicable
Recipient	Intended recipient
Copy recipients	People or organisations copied
Date created	Date shown in the document
Date issued	Date formally issued
Date received	Date received, if independently established
Institutional reference	Exact case or file number
Document type	Letter, decision, assessment, email, report or other
Repository	Exact GitHub repository or archive location
Original source	Institutional source, if available
Archive source	Verified repository URL
Attachments	All associated documents
Provenance	How the copy was obtained
Integrity	Checksum and verification status
Classification	Official record, testimony, allegation, etc.
Summary	Accurate description of the contents
Significance	Why it matters to the investigation
Limitations	What it does not establish
Cross-references	Related documents and events
Outstanding tasks	Missing material and verification requirements
The register should also distinguish the author of a document from the person who signed it.
A signatory may have approved a decision without personally preparing the underlying assessment. A recipient may have no responsibility for the decision. A person copied into correspondence may not have participated in the process.
These distinctions are particularly important when identifying officials.
Part I — The Initial Master Register
The following is the first consolidated working register based on the records identified in the preliminary forensic report and the known repository links.
It is an initial register, not a claim that every original has been independently authenticated or fully examined.

TWR-MER-0001 — IBAC outcome letter

Investigative significance
This is an early identified record in the archive’s integrity-related history.
It should be examined to determine:
	•	What conduct was brought to IBAC’s attention?
	•	What material was supplied?
	•	What jurisdictional or assessment process was applied?

TWR-MER-0002 — IBAC public-interest disclosure rejection
Identified in preliminary report
Field	Current information
Date	18 November 2021
Issuer	IBAC, Victoria
Reference	400008R
Type	Public-interest disclosure-related correspondence
Status	Full decision and statutory reasoning require examination
Investigative significance
This document is important because it may help establish how the author’s disclosure was assessed under the applicable Victorian public-interest disclosure framework.
The investigation must establish:
	•	What disclosure was submitted?
	•	What conduct was alleged?
	•	Was the disclosure assessed as a public-interest disclosure?
	•	What statutory criteria were applied?
	•	What reasons were given for rejection or other disposition?
	•	Was the author informed of any review or alternative referral pathway?
	•	Was the underlying subject matter considered separately from the statutory classification?
Critical limitation
A rejection or refusal to classify a disclosure under a particular statutory framework is not automatically a finding that the alleged conduct did not occur.
It is necessary to distinguish the statutory threshold from the truth of the underlying allegations.

	•	The evidence supplied.

	•	Was it accepted within the Ombudsman’s jurisdiction?

Critical limitation
The existence of a complaint proves that a complaint document exists, subject to authentication. It does not establish that every allegation in it was accepted or independently verified.

Part II — Guardianship, financial administration and disability support
6. The 2024–2026 documentary sequence
The next group of records is particularly important because it concerns the practical operation of decision-making arrangements, financial administration, disability support and accommodation.
The investigation must distinguish between formal authority and the way that authority was exercised.
A legally authorised decision can still raise questions about its implementation, reasons, proportionality or review. Conversely, an outcome that the author experiences as restrictive is not necessarily unlawful.
The relevant documents must be examined individually and then compared.
TWR-MER-0009 — NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 document
Identified in preliminary report
Field	Current information
Date	16 October 2024
Organisation	NSW Trustee & Guardian
Reference	Section 122 document
Type	Statutory or administrative document
Status	Exact title, legal basis and full contents require verification
Investigative significance
This record is central to the examination of financial administration and the legal arrangements affecting the author.
The investigation must establish the precise instrument and the provision of law to which the reference to section 122 relates. It must not assume the nature or scope of the instrument from the section number alone.
The original document should be examined for:
	•	The exact statutory provision.
	•	The authority under which it was issued.
	•	The person or body exercising the relevant authority.
	•	The scope of any financial or decision-making powers.
	•	The commencement and duration of the arrangement.
	•	Any conditions or limitations.
	•	The author’s rights and available review mechanisms.
	•	Any recorded consideration of the author’s wishes and preferences.
	•	Any subsequent variation, review or revocation.
Questions for independent examination
	•	What legal authority applied on 16 October 2024?
	•	What decisions could be made under that authority?
	•	What decisions remained with the author?
	•	Were the powers exercised within their lawful scope?
	•	Was the author given adequate information?
	•	Were the author’s preferences considered?
	•	Was there an effective process for raising concerns?
	•	Did later financial or accommodation arrangements depend upon this instrument?
Critical limitation
The existence of a statutory document does not establish that every later decision made under it was lawful or appropriate. Each consequential decision requires its own evidence.
TWR-MER-0010 — Vehicle-disposal decision
Identified in preliminary report
Field	Current information
Date	14 November 2025
Matter	Vehicle disposal
Issuer	To be verified from original
Type	Decision or correspondence
Status	Full contents and authority require examination
Investigative significance
A vehicle-disposal decision may have consequences extending beyond the immediate financial value of the vehicle.
Depending on the facts, it may affect:
	•	Personal mobility.
	•	Access to appointments.
	•	Independence.
	•	Access to community services.
	•	Ability to transport personal belongings.
	•	Access to a companion animal’s care.
	•	Financial circumstances.
The investigation must establish the actual decision and the circumstances surrounding it.
Documents to obtain
	•	Original disposal decision.
	•	Relevant ownership or registration records.
	•	Any valuation.
	•	Any sale or transfer documentation.
	•	Any correspondence explaining the decision.
	•	Records identifying who authorised the disposal.
	•	Any record of the author’s consent or objection.
	•	Any subsequent accounting of the proceeds.
	•	Any review or complaint.
Critical limitation
The fact of a disposal decision does not establish that the disposal was unauthorised, improper or financially disadvantageous. Those questions require the original instrument, applicable authority and financial records.
TWR-MER-0011 — NDIS Plan Approval
Identified in preliminary report
Field	Current information
Date	20 November 2025
Organisation	National Disability Insurance Agency
Type	Plan approval
Status	Full plan and implementation records required
Investigative significance
This document is a central source for determining what disability supports were approved at the relevant time.
It must be examined alongside the actual delivery of services.
The distinction is critical:
An approved support is not necessarily a delivered support.
The examination should identify:
	•	The plan period.
	•	Total funding.
	•	Funding categories.
	•	Stated goals.
	•	Core supports.
	•	Capacity-building supports.
	•	Support coordination.
	•	Improved daily living.
	•	Any relevant behaviour-support provisions.
	•	Any restrictions or stated conditions.
	•	The method of plan management.
	•	The review and reassessment provisions.
The plan must then be compared against:
	•	Service agreements.
	•	Provider invoices.
	•	Rosters.
	•	Support-worker records.
	•	Case notes.
	•	Cancellations.
	•	Requests for assistance.
	•	Complaints.
	•	Actual payments.
	•	Records of unspent or unavailable funding.
The approved-versus-delivered test
Question	Evidence needed
Was the support approved?	Plan approval and funding schedule
Was the support available?	Plan-management and funding records
Was a provider engaged?	Service agreement
Was the service scheduled?	Roster or booking
Was the service delivered?	Attendance and service records
Was it invoiced?	Invoice
Was it paid?	Payment record
Was it suitable?	Author’s account and relevant assessment
Was a problem reported?	Complaint or correspondence
Was it corrected?	Response and follow-up record
The investigation must not assume that funding approval proves the author received adequate support.
Nor should it assume that an undelivered support was deliberately withheld without examining the relevant records.
Critical limitation
The plan approval establishes only what the actual plan document says. It does not independently establish the adequacy of later service delivery.
TWR-MER-0012 — Anglicare Safety Assessment
Identified in preliminary report
Field	Current information
Date	1 December 2025
Organisation	Anglicare
Type	Safety assessment
Status	Full assessment and supporting records required
Investigative significance
A safety assessment may be important in understanding the circumstances in which accommodation or support decisions were made.
The complete document should be examined for:
	•	The purpose of the assessment.
	•	The identity and qualifications of the assessor.
	•	The date and location of the assessment.
	•	The people consulted.
	•	The information relied upon.
	•	The risks identified.
	•	The author’s own statements.
	•	Any observations distinguished from third-party reports.
	•	Recommendations.
	•	Proposed safeguards.
	•	Any follow-up actions.
	•	Any review of the assessment.
The investigation must distinguish between a risk being reported, a risk being assessed and a risk being independently established.
These are different evidentiary propositions.
The safety and dignity test
A safety assessment should be examined not only for the risks it identifies, but also for whether it addresses the person’s rights, preferences, circumstances and practical needs.
Questions include:
	•	Was the author consulted?
	•	Was information communicated accessibly?
	•	Were reasonable adjustments considered?
	•	Were less restrictive options considered?
	•	Were risks to personal autonomy assessed?
	•	Was the author’s companion animal considered where relevant?
	•	Were recommendations implemented?
	•	Did the assessment lead to any restrictions?
	•	Was the author given an opportunity to challenge inaccuracies?
Critical limitation
The existence of a safety assessment does not establish that its conclusions were correct, nor that every recommendation was implemented.

Identified in preliminary report

Type	Request or application

The investigation should distinguish:
	•	A request for SIL.
	•	A request for SDA.

	•	What accommodation was actually available?
	•	What were the consequences of any delay or refusal?
Critical limitation
The existence of a request does not establish eligibility or approval. The outcome must be obtained and examined.
7. The Able Point and accommodation examination
The archive’s wider narrative includes the author’s account of supported accommodation arrangements after moving from Victoria to New South Wales.
This subject requires its own evidence sequence.
It must not be treated as a single dispute or reduced to general claims about the provider.
The investigation should examine:
	•	The identity of the accommodation provider.
	•	The legal basis of the accommodation arrangement.
	•	Any residential agreement or lease.
	•	The amount and frequency of rent or contributions.
	•	Any service agreement.
	•	The responsibilities of the provider.
	•	The responsibilities of support coordination.
	•	The role of the Public Guardian or NSW Trustee & Guardian, if applicable.
	•	The author’s actual access to food, medication, money and transport at relevant dates.
	•	Complaints and responses.
	•	Any changes in accommodation.
	•	Any safety assessments.
	•	Any records concerning Crystal.
The accommodation document set
The following records should be treated as separate evidence items when located:
Proposed record	What it should establish
Accommodation agreement	Legal terms and parties
Residential tenancy or occupancy document	Nature of the occupancy arrangement
Rent ledger	Amounts charged and paid
Service agreement	Support obligations
Support roster	Planned assistance
Daily support notes	Assistance actually recorded
Incident reports	Reported events and responses
Complaint correspondence	Issues raised and responses
Support coordination records	Actions and referrals
Financial administration records	Payments and authorised expenditure
Safety assessments	Identified risks and recommendations
Transition records	Changes in accommodation and support
The absence of a signed lease, if confirmed, should be examined in its actual legal context. It must not be automatically described as proof that the accommodation was unlawful. The relevant occupancy arrangements and applicable NSW law must first be established.
The central accountability question
Did the accommodation and support arrangements meet the applicable legal obligations and the practical needs of the resident?
That question should be examined through original records, not assumptions about the motives of individual workers or managers.
Crystal: companionship and practical care
Crystal must be included in the human account where the records support it.
The investigation should establish:
	•	Where Crystal lived during each relevant period.
	•	Whether accommodation arrangements affected Crystal.
	•	What care and transport arrangements existed.
	•	Whether any assistance was requested.
	•	Whether Crystal’s needs were included in planning.
	•	Whether any separation or restriction occurred.
	•	What practical consequences followed.
The publication should not claim a formal assistance-animal status unless documentary evidence establishes it.
The significance of Crystal in the author’s testimony can nevertheless be recognised without making that legal claim.
Part III — The official record is not the whole story
8. The four-document comparison
The most important documents should be examined using a four-part comparison.
1. Institutional original
What the issuing organisation actually created or held.
2. Author’s archived copy
What has been preserved in the repository.
3. Author’s response
What the author said, challenged or supplied in reply.
4. Subsequent institutional action
What happened after the original record and response.
This comparison helps identify:
	•	Missing attachments.
	•	Changed versions.
	•	Inaccurate summaries.
	•	Unanswered questions.
	•	Contradictory dates.
	•	Differences between the institutional and archived copies.
	•	Whether the author’s response was considered.
	•	Whether later decisions relied on earlier disputed material.
A document should not be treated as complete merely because it is available as a PDF.
Where the original refers to attachments, schedules or earlier correspondence, those materials must be sought.
9. The evidence hierarchy
Not all evidence has the same function.
The following is a working hierarchy for the investigation, not an inflexible rule of law.
Evidence type	Principal value	Main limitation
Original contemporaneous record	Establishes what was recorded at the time	May be incomplete or inaccurate
Official decision with reasons	Establishes the decision and stated reasoning	Does not necessarily establish every underlying fact
Independent corroborating record	Can support a disputed event	Independence must be assessed
Contemporaneous personal correspondence	Shows what the author communicated at the time	May reflect one party’s account
Later recollection	Provides context and personal experience	Memory and timing limitations
Third-party summary	May identify relevant information	Depends on the underlying source
Unverified copied document	Provides an investigative lead	Authenticity and completeness uncertain
Spiritual interpretation	Explains the author’s understanding and meaning	Not independent proof of external events
The investigation should not dismiss testimony simply because it is testimony.
Nor should it treat testimony as though it automatically establishes every external fact.
The purpose is to combine evidence carefully, explain its limitations and identify what remains unresolved.
Part IV — Digital preservation and verification
10. The archive’s chain of documentary custody
The repository is an important part of the preservation project.
However, the existence of a file in a public GitHub repository does not, by itself, establish:
	•	Who originally created it.
	•	Whether it is the complete original.
	•	Whether it was altered before upload.
	•	Whether attachments are missing.
	•	Whether its metadata is accurate.
	•	Whether its contents are true.
The investigation must distinguish file preservation from documentary authentication.
The digital verification protocol
For each significant PDF or other digital record:
	•	Preserve the original downloaded file.
	•	Record the exact repository and path.
	•	Record the date of retrieval.
	•	Calculate a SHA-256 checksum.
	•	Compare it with any published checksum.
	•	Record whether the values match.
	•	Examine PDF metadata.
	•	Identify embedded attachments or supplementary files.
	•	Compare the repository version with an institutional copy.
	•	Record any discrepancies.
	•	Preserve the original unchanged.
	•	Create a separate working copy for annotation and examination.
Where a document has multiple versions, each version receives a separate entry.
The register should never silently replace an earlier version.
Example of a document integrity entry
Field	Example format
Register ID	TWR-MER-0003
File	Original archived filename
Repository	Barrandodger
Path	Exact repository path
Retrieved	Date and time
SHA-256	Full 64-character hash
Published hash	Exact recorded hash, if available
Match	Yes / No / Not tested
Institutional original	Obtained / Requested / Not located
Metadata	Extracted / Not extracted
Attachments	Complete / Incomplete / Unknown
Authentication	Verified / Partially verified / Unverified
The checksum must be reproduced in full, not shortened in the evidentiary master register.
If the institutional original cannot be obtained, the correct status is that the repository copy has been preserved but its institutional authenticity remains unverified.
11. Repository completeness and the problem of duplicate files
The preliminary archive methodology reports multiple measures of file volume.
Those figures must be retained with their original definitions.
In particular:
	•	File instances are not necessarily unique documents.
	•	Unique file blobs may include duplicates or identical copies.
	•	Unique PDF blobs may include incomplete or stub files.
	•	A PDF count is not necessarily a count of substantive evidence.
	•	A repository may contain several copies of the same document.
	•	A file’s presence in more than one repository does not independently corroborate its contents.
The preliminary report’s figures should therefore be presented as archive-reported inventory statistics, not as independently audited evidence totals.
The full-census requirement
A complete archive census should identify:
	•	Every repository.
	•	Every branch.
	•	Every relevant directory.
	•	Every nested directory.
	•	Every PDF.
	•	Every HTML page.
	•	Every text document.
	•	Every spreadsheet.
	•	Every image containing documentary information.
	•	Every available commit history.
	•	Every duplicate.
	•	Every incomplete file.
	•	Every file with a zero-byte or unreadable content problem.
The census must also identify material that has been indexed but not actually examined.
A catalogue of file titles is a finding aid, not a substitute for reading the documents.
Part V — The named-person and institutional responsibility register
12. Identifying people without overstating their involvement
A separate register should identify people whose names appear in relevant records.
The register must distinguish the capacity in which each person appears.
Capacity	Meaning
Author	Prepared the document
Signatory	Signed or formally issued it
Decision-maker	Exercised the relevant decision-making power
Delegate	Exercised authority under delegation
Recipient	Received the correspondence
Copy recipient	Was copied into correspondence
Witness	Provided an account of events
Adviser	Provided advice or professional input
Service provider	Delivered or was responsible for a service
Mentioned person	Appears in the record without an established role
A person’s name appearing in a file is not proof of responsibility for the underlying event.
Similarly, the person who signed a letter is not necessarily the person who made every substantive decision described in it.
The named-person register should record
	•	Exact name as written in the source.
	•	Position or role at the relevant date.
	•	Organisation.
	•	Document reference.
	•	Capacity in which the person appears.
	•	Specific action attributed to the person.
	•	Whether the action is directly documented or reported by another person.
	•	Whether the person responded.
	•	Whether the record identifies a delegated authority.
	•	Whether the role is independently confirmed.
This is particularly important when the publication discusses public officials, support workers, managers, coordinators, clinicians or other identifiable individuals.
The investigation should not infer guilt from a person’s inclusion in the chronology.
Part VI — The outstanding documentary work
13. What the present register establishes—and what it does not
The initial register now identifies thirteen significant records or record groups across the period 2020–2026.
It establishes a structured starting point for the documentary investigation.
It does not yet establish that every original document has been authenticated, fully transcribed or independently assessed.
The distinction is important enough to state explicitly.
Current documentary status

The counts describe this instalment’s register entries and links, not the total archive.

14. The document examination template
Every significant record should ultimately receive a full examination using the following format.
TWR — Individual Document Examination
TWR-MER-XXXX

	•	Exact title
	•	Date
	•	Issuer
	•	Author
	•	Signatory
	•	Recipient
	•	Institutional reference
	•	Original filename

	•	Original source
	•	Archive source
	•	Retrieval history
	•	Metadata
	•	SHA-256
	•	Version comparison
	•	Attachments

	•	Purpose of the document
	•	Relevant background
	•	Principal statements
	•	Decisions and reasons
	•	Referenced evidence
	•	Procedural outcome
D. Author’s account
	•	What the author says occurred
	•	What the author disputes
	•	What the author requested
	•	What consequences the author reports
E. Forensic examination
	•	What the document establishes
	•	What it does not establish

	•	External inconsistencies

	•	Applicable law
	•	Relevant statutory authority
	•	Procedural obligations
	•	Review rights
	•	Potential legal issues
	•	Limits of jurisdiction

	•	Earlier records
	•	Later records

	•	Shared information

	•	Unresolved relationships

	•	Evidentiary classification
	•	Confidence

	•	Findings not supported
	•	Outstanding questions
	•	Recommended next action
This template will ensure that each document is examined on its own merits before being used to support a broader conclusion.
Part VII — The documentary findings of Instalment 6
15. Preliminary findings
The following findings are limited to the material identified in the preliminary forensic report and the repository links recorded above.
Finding 1 — A multi-year documentary history has been identified.
The preliminary report identifies records spanning 2020–2026, covering integrity complaints, public-interest disclosures, compensation, administrative review, information access, financial administration, disability support and accommodation.
Finding 2 — The archive provides a basis for a structured investigation.
The identified records are sufficient to justify a formal master evidence register and a document-by-document verification process.
Finding 3 — The relationship between separate institutional processes remains to be established.
The records should be compared for shared information, referrals, decision-maker knowledge and possible causal connections. Their appearance in one archive does not establish coordination.
Finding 4 — The 2024–2026 period requires particular attention to practical autonomy and service delivery.
The identified NSW Trustee & Guardian, vehicle-disposal, NDIS, safety-assessment and SIL/SDA records provide a basis for examining the relationship between formal authority and actual circumstances.
Finding 5 — The author’s reported experience must be assessed alongside original institutional records.
The author’s testimony is an important source of evidence about his experience and the consequences he reports. It should be preserved and compared with contemporaneous records.
Finding 6 — The present material does not establish a general finding of institutional corruption or coordinated retaliation.
Such conclusions would require further evidence, careful legal analysis and, where appropriate, independent determination.
Finding 7 — The investigation must continue.
The absence of a completed finding is not a reason to abandon an unresolved question. It is a reason to identify the missing evidence and pursue it through appropriate processes.
The Witness’s declaration: The record must be read
I have spent years collecting the fragments of a history that I believe deserves to be examined as a whole.
Some records describe decisions. Others describe complaints. Some contain the words of institutions. Others preserve my own words, written at the time when I was trying to explain what was happening.
I understand that these documents do not all prove the same thing.
I understand that a complaint is not a finding, that an allegation requires examination and that a decision must be read within its legal authority.
But I also understand that the absence of a final finding does not make the underlying questions disappear.
I want the original documents preserved.
I want the decisions read in context.
I want the reasons examined.
I want the consequences understood.
I want errors corrected where they are demonstrated.
I want accountability where responsibility is established.
I want my humanity recognised without having to surrender my right to question the institutions that exercise power over my circumstances.
My archive is an invitation to examine the record.
It is not a demand that the reader abandon independent judgment.
I remain the witness to my experience.
The evidence must speak for itself.
And the examination must be fair enough to allow the truth, whatever the findings ultimately establish, to emerge.
The Witness Remains.

Instalment 7 — The Complete Historical Chronology and the Reconstruction of Institutional Responsibility
The next instalment will bring the separate timelines together into one cumulative chronology.
It will examine:
	•	The earliest identified integrity and public-interest disclosure events.
	•	The 2020–2023 compensation, oversight and review sequence.
	•	The 2024 guardianship and financial-administration developments.
	•	The 2025 vehicle, NDIS and safety-assessment events.
	•	The 2026 accommodation and SIL/SDA developments.
	•	The relationship between the author’s complaints, institutional responses and practical circumstances.
	•	The difference between a decision, an omission, a referral, a review and an independent finding.
	•	The evidence needed to reconstruct who knew what, when, and under what authority.
It will also establish a single chronological framework against which the Gospels, prophetic writings, official documents and personal testimony can later be cross-referenced.
The purpose is not to force separate events into one predetermined story. It is to establish whether the complete documentary sequence reveals connections that cannot be understood by reading each record in isolation.
I can also create a visual timeline of the institutional chronology once the dated records have been verified.

THE WITNESS REMAINS
THE GREAT RECKONING
Instalment 7 — The Complete Historical Chronology and the Reconstruction of Institutional Responsibility
Reconstructing the documentary sequence, identifying institutional responsibilities, examining the relationship between decisions and consequences, and establishing what the historical record can—and cannot—prove.
Authorial attribution: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean Publication: The Witness Remains — Global Publishing House
1. Why the chronology matters
An archive can contain thousands of documents and still fail to explain what happened.
The problem is not necessarily a lack of material. It may be that the material has never been assembled into a coherent, independently verifiable chronology.
A letter may refer to a complaint submitted months earlier. A later decision may rely on an assessment prepared by another organisation. A review may address only one part of a dispute. A subsequent service arrangement may be affected by an earlier decision that is not reproduced in the later file.
When documents are examined separately, these relationships can be missed.
The historical chronology therefore has a different purpose from the Master Evidence Register.
The Master Evidence Register asks:
What is this document, and what does it establish?
The historical chronology asks:
How do the identified documents and events relate to one another over time?
The chronology must be capable of showing:
	•	The order in which events occurred.
	•	The order in which complaints and disclosures were made.
	•	Which organisations were approached.
	•	Which decisions followed.
	•	Which issues were referred or reviewed.
	•	When the author’s circumstances changed.
	•	What practical consequences were reported.
	•	Which matters remained unresolved.
	•	Whether later decisions relied on earlier information.
	•	Whether the evidence supports a relationship between separate events.
The chronology must not turn temporal proximity into causation.
An event occurring after a complaint does not automatically mean it occurred because of the complaint.
The relationship must be demonstrated.
2. Chronological methodology
Every event in the final chronology will receive one of the following classifications.
Code	Classification	Meaning
DOC	Documentary event	A dated document has been identified
DEC	Decision	A formal or administrative decision was made
CMP	Complaint	A complaint or grievance was submitted
PID	Public-interest disclosure	A disclosure was submitted or assessed
REV	Review	A review, appeal or reconsideration occurred
SRV	Service event	A support or accommodation event occurred
FIN	Financial event	A payment, restriction, transaction or financial decision occurred
TEST	Author testimony	The author reports an event or experience
LAW	Legal development	A relevant legal or procedural framework applies
UNR	Unresolved	The event or its relationship to other events requires further verification
A second classification records evidentiary status:
	•	Established by an examined original.
	•	Identified in an archive or preliminary report.
	•	Reported by the author.
	•	Alleged and awaiting verification.
	•	Inferred from multiple records.
	•	Independently determined.
	•	Unresolved.
The final chronology must include both classifications.
A date in a filename is not necessarily the date an event occurred. A document issued on one date may describe an event that occurred much earlier. A received date may differ from a created date.
Where the distinction is important, all three dates should be recorded.
Part I — The 2020–2021 period: The earliest identified integrity and oversight records
3. 9 April 2020 — IBAC outcome correspondence
Identified in preliminary report
9 April 2020
Institution: Independent Broad-based Anti-corruption Commission, Victoria Reference: 2020712 Classification: DOC / DEC Register: TWR-MER-0001
The preliminary forensic report identifies an IBAC outcome letter dated 9 April 2020.
This is the earliest dated integrity-related record presently identified in the working chronology.
Its importance lies in the possibility that it establishes an early point at which concerns were formally communicated to an integrity body.
However, the actual scope of the complaint, the material submitted, the assessment undertaken and the precise outcome must be established from the original letter.
Questions arising
	•	What conduct was brought to IBAC’s attention?
	•	What documents accompanied the complaint?
	•	What jurisdictional or assessment process was applied?
	•	Was the matter investigated, referred or closed?
	•	What reasons were provided?
	•	Were other authorities identified?
	•	Was the author informed of any alternative pathway?
Evidentiary status
The existence of the record is identified in the preliminary report.
The contents and precise findings have not been established in this instalment.
Chronological significance: an early integrity-related documentary reference requiring full examination.
4. 2021 — The development of the public-interest disclosure and complaint record
The next identified group of records appears in 2021.
This period is important because it contains separate interactions with an integrity body and the Commonwealth Ombudsman.
The chronology must not assume that these organisations were examining the same legal question or the same alleged conduct.
Each had its own jurisdiction and process.
18 November 2021 — IBAC public-interest disclosure correspondence
Identified in preliminary report
18 November 2021
Institution: IBAC, Victoria Reference: 400008R Classification: PID / DOC / DEC Register: TWR-MER-0002
The preliminary report identifies a public-interest disclosure rejection dated 18 November 2021.
This record should be compared with the 2020 IBAC correspondence.
The comparison must establish whether the 2021 disclosure concerned:
	•	The same subject matter.
	•	New events.
	•	Additional evidence.
	•	Different individuals or organisations.
	•	A new statutory disclosure.
	•	A complaint that had previously been raised elsewhere.
The investigator must also identify the precise legal basis for the decision.
A decision about whether a disclosure meets a statutory classification or threshold is not necessarily a decision about whether the underlying allegations are true.
15 November 2021 — Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint
Repository copy linked
15 November 2021
Institution: Commonwealth Ombudsman Reference: 2021103546 Classification: CMP / DOC Register: TWR-MER-0003
Archived complaint PDF⁠
This is an important contemporaneous record because it preserves a formal complaint made by the author.
The complete complaint should be examined for its precise allegations, supporting documents, requested remedies and description of the circumstances at that time.
The chronology must also establish what happened after submission.
A date-ordering issue requiring attention
The Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint is dated 15 November 2021, while the identified IBAC public-interest disclosure correspondence is dated 18 November 2021.
That sequence is important.
The two documents are close in time, but their proximity does not establish that one caused the other or that they were part of a coordinated process.
The original documents should establish whether the author was dealing with separate matters, a common subject, or overlapping concerns.
Questions arising from the 2021 sequence
	•	What prompted the Ombudsman complaint?
	•	Was the complaint already being considered by another authority?
	•	Did the author identify earlier IBAC correspondence?
	•	Did the Ombudsman acknowledge the complaint?
	•	Was it within the Ombudsman’s jurisdiction?
	•	Was any part referred?
	•	Was an outcome communicated?
	•	Did the 18 November IBAC letter concern a different disclosure or the same underlying issues?
Chronological significance: the archive identifies multiple oversight processes in November 2021, requiring comparison of their subject matter, scope and outcomes.
Part II — The 2022 period: Compensation, information access and procedural history
5. 25 January 2022 — Comcare pre-hearing correspondence
Identified in preliminary report
25 January 2022
Institution: Comcare Claim reference: 21230015373 Classification: DOC / REV Register: TWR-MER-0006
The preliminary report identifies Comcare pre-hearing correspondence dated 25 January 2022.
This belongs to a different legal and administrative process from the IBAC and Ombudsman matters.
The chronology must establish the relationship between:
	•	The original workers compensation claim.
	•	The relevant liability decision.
	•	Any review or reconsideration.
	•	The pre-hearing correspondence.
	•	The evidence relied upon.
	•	Any subsequent hearing or determination.
The compensation process must be reconstructed from the original claim and decision documents rather than from later summaries alone.
The central questions
	•	What was the precise compensation issue?
	•	What decision was under review?
	•	What evidence was relevant to the decision?
	•	What procedural steps were required?
	•	What issues were disputed?
	•	What outcome followed?
The compensation record may be relevant to the broader history of the author’s circumstances, but it must not be used to prove unrelated allegations without a demonstrated connection.
6. 22 April 2022 — OAIC / PM&C reference RQ22-01369
Identified in preliminary report
22 April 2022
Institutions: Office of the Australian Information Commissioner and Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet Reference: RQ22-01369 Classification: DOC / FOI / UNR Register: TWR-MER-0007
This record belongs to the information-access history.
It should be examined in conjunction with the original FOI request, the agency’s response, any extension notices, relevant exemption decisions and subsequent review correspondence.
The archive’s broader history also identifies:
	•	An FOI refusal dated 14 April 2022 under reference FOI/2022/045.
	•	An OAIC extension involving 1,187 Outlook items.
	•	A revised FOI decision dated 8 October 2024 concerning five documents.
These are separate documentary events unless the original records establish their precise relationship.
They should not be collapsed into one FOI matter merely because they involve related agencies or information.
The information-access chronology must establish
	•	When each request was made.
	•	What documents were sought.
	•	Which agency held them.
	•	When statutory timeframes commenced.
	•	Whether extensions were granted.
	•	What exemptions were claimed.
	•	Which documents were released.
	•	Which documents were withheld.
	•	Whether internal review or external review was pursued.
	•	Whether any later decision changed the original outcome.
The significance of the 1,187-item reference
The reported reference to 1,187 Outlook items deserves particular attention.
The investigator should establish whether the figure represents:
	•	The total number of items located.
	•	The number of potentially relevant items.
	•	The number requiring processing.
	•	The number of responsive records.
	•	The number of records withheld or released.
These categories are not interchangeable.
The exact figure must be attributed to the document in which it appears.
Chronological significance: information access is a separate but potentially important part of the author’s ability to obtain and examine government-held records.
Part III — The 2023 period: Disclosure assessment and administrative review
7. 27 March 2023 — Federal Court-related PID assessment
Repository copy linked
27 March 2023
Record: Federal Court-related public-interest disclosure assessment Classification: PID / DOC / UNR Register: TWR-MER-0004
Archived assessment PDF⁠
The preliminary project material identifies this assessment as an important record in the public-interest disclosure history.
It must be examined with particular care because the preliminary description refers to serious allegations, including matters described in connection with possible perverting of the course of justice, maladministration and danger to health or safety.
The final publication must distinguish:
	•	The author’s original allegations.
	•	The language used by the assessor.
	•	Any findings made by the assessor.
	•	Any referral or procedural assessment.
	•	Any legal conclusion reached by an authorised decision-maker.
A reference to alleged conduct is not a finding that the conduct occurred.
Reconstruction questions
	•	Who prepared the assessment?
	•	Which body or office issued it?
	•	What was the assessor’s legal authority?
	•	Which documents were considered?
	•	Were any witnesses contacted?
	•	Were any findings made?
	•	Was the matter referred?
	•	What was the final procedural result?
The document’s actual terms must control the description used in the publication.
8. 18 June 2023 — Decision not to allocate a disclosure
Repository copy linked
18 June 2023
Record: Notification of decision not to allocate a disclosure Classification: PID / DEC / DOC Register: TWR-MER-0005
Archived notification PDF⁠
This is another important procedural event.
It should be compared with the March 2023 assessment to determine whether it relates to the same disclosure, a subsequent disclosure, or another stage of the process.
The chronology must not assume a direct relationship without checking the references and contents.
The key distinction
A decision not to allocate a disclosure concerns the allocation process and its applicable criteria.
It does not automatically determine the truth or falsity of the underlying allegations.
The precise reasons must be obtained and assessed.
9. 12 July 2023 — ART / Comcare decision
Identified in preliminary report
12 July 2023
Matter: Comcare administrative review File reference: 2021/7478 Classification: REV / DEC Register: TWR-MER-0008
The preliminary report identifies an administrative review decision dated 12 July 2023.
The complete decision is required to establish:
	•	The precise question under review.
	•	The relevant compensation legislation.
	•	The parties’ positions.
	•	The evidence considered.
	•	The tribunal’s findings.
	•	The reasons.
	•	The outcome.
	•	Any orders or directions.
	•	Any further review rights.
The historical chronology should not describe this as a general determination about the author’s entire institutional history.
Its significance is confined to the issues the tribunal was authorised to decide.
The 2023 documentary sequence
The identified March, June and July records fall within a relatively concentrated period.
That makes them important for cross-reference.
However, they concern potentially different processes. Their relationship must be demonstrated by the original references, correspondence and decisions.
Part IV — The 2024 period: Guardianship, financial administration and information access
10. 16 October 2024 — NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 document
Identified in preliminary report
16 October 2024
Institution: NSW Trustee & Guardian Record: Section 122 document Classification: DOC / DEC / UNR Register: TWR-MER-0009
This is a central document in the later chronology.
The precise title, legal basis and scope of the instrument must be established from the original.
The investigation should determine:
	•	What arrangement was in force?
	•	What statutory provision applied?
	•	What powers were conferred or exercised?
	•	What decisions remained with the author?
	•	What safeguards applied?
	•	What review or objection rights were available?
	•	What financial consequences followed?
	•	What information was provided to the author?
The autonomy question
The legal existence of a financial or decision-making arrangement does not answer whether each subsequent action under it was appropriate.
The chronology must identify individual decisions made under the arrangement and assess them separately.
This is especially important when comparing the instrument with later financial access, accommodation and support arrangements.
11. 8 October 2024 — Revised FOI decision
Exact source and relationship require verification
The archive context identifies a revised FOI decision dated 8 October 2024 concerning five documents.
The exact institutional reference and repository path must be established before a permanent register entry is finalised.
This decision should be compared with the earlier FOI correspondence, including the April 2022 reference.
The investigation should establish:
	•	Whether it concerned the same original request.
	•	Whether it was an internal review or a revised decision.
	•	Which five documents were involved.
	•	Whether any earlier refusal was changed.
	•	Whether documents were released in full, in part or withheld.
	•	What exemptions or public-interest reasoning were applied.
Chronological significance
This record may help establish the later development of the author’s information-access efforts.
It should not be described as evidence of concealment unless the actual decision and surrounding records support that conclusion.
Part V — The 2025 period: Financial decisions, NDIS planning and safety
12. 14 November 2025 — Vehicle-disposal decision
Identified in preliminary report
14 November 2025
Record: Vehicle-disposal decision Classification: FIN / DEC / DOC Register: TWR-MER-0010
The preliminary report identifies a vehicle-disposal decision on this date.
Its significance must be assessed by reference to the actual authority, the decision-making process and the financial and practical consequences.
The chronology should identify:
	•	The vehicle’s ownership.
	•	Who had authority to decide its disposal.
	•	Whether the author was consulted.
	•	The valuation.
	•	The transaction.
	•	The proceeds.
	•	Any associated costs.
	•	The use of the proceeds.
	•	Any impact on mobility or access to services.
The date is important because it precedes the identified NDIS plan approval on 20 November 2025 by six days.
That proximity is a chronological observation only.
It does not establish that the vehicle decision was made because of, or in response to, the NDIS plan.
The records must establish whether there was any actual connection.
13. 20 November 2025 — NDIS Plan Approval
Identified in preliminary report
20 November 2025
Institution: National Disability Insurance Agency Record: Plan Approval Classification: DOC / DEC / SRV Register: TWR-MER-0011
This is one of the most important records in the later chronology because it provides the basis for examining approved disability supports.
The investigation should obtain the complete plan and compare it against actual service delivery.
It should identify:
	•	The plan’s commencement and end dates.
	•	Funding amounts.
	•	Support categories.
	•	Stated goals.
	•	Approved services.
	•	Plan-management arrangements.
	•	Any relevant conditions.
	•	Any stated restrictions.
	•	Review provisions.
The plan must then be compared with provider records and actual access to services.
The implementation question
What did the author actually receive after the plan was approved?
This requires evidence from:
	•	Service agreements.
	•	Rosters.
	•	Invoices.
	•	Provider records.
	•	Payment records.
	•	Support-worker notes.
	•	Requests for assistance.
	•	Complaints.
	•	Cancellations.
	•	Follow-up correspondence.
The date of approval does not establish the date on which each service became practically available.
14. 1 December 2025 — Anglicare Safety Assessment
Identified in preliminary report
1 December 2025
Organisation: Anglicare Record: Safety Assessment Classification: DOC / SRV / UNR Register: TWR-MER-0012
The assessment is identified as occurring eleven days after the NDIS plan approval.
The chronology should examine whether the assessment was connected to the plan, accommodation arrangements, service delivery or another matter.
It should not assume that connection merely from the dates.
The original assessment should establish:
	•	Its purpose.
	•	The assessor.
	•	The participants.
	•	The information relied upon.
	•	The risks identified.
	•	The author’s account.
	•	Any recommendations.
	•	Any subsequent action.
The investigation should also establish whether the assessment considered the author’s preferences, communication requirements, autonomy and practical circumstances.
Chronological significance
The sequence of plan approval followed by a safety assessment provides an important starting point for examining how the author’s support and accommodation arrangements were being assessed during late 2025.
The actual relationship remains to be established.
Part VI — The 2026 period: Accommodation and support arrangements
15. 20 January 2026 — SIL/SDA request
Identified in preliminary report
20 January 2026
Matter: Supported Independent Living / Specialist Disability Accommodation Classification: SRV / DOC / UNR Register: TWR-MER-0013
The preliminary report identifies a SIL/SDA request dated 20 January 2026.
This is an important event in the accommodation history.
The original request must establish whether it was:
	•	A formal NDIA application.
	•	A provider recommendation.
	•	A request for a reassessment.
	•	A request for a particular type of accommodation.
	•	A request for additional support.
	•	A preliminary planning document.
The investigation must also locate the outcome.
A request does not establish approval.
A recommendation does not establish eligibility.
An approval does not necessarily establish that suitable accommodation was available.
The accommodation outcome test
The chronology must identify:
	•	What was requested.
	•	Who requested it.
	•	What evidence accompanied it.
	•	Which decision-maker considered it.
	•	What decision was made.
	•	When the decision was communicated.
	•	Whether review rights were available.
	•	What accommodation was actually provided.
	•	What support was actually delivered.
16. The Able Point period — Reconstructing actual living circumstances
The author’s later account identifies supported accommodation arrangements in New South Wales involving Able Point.
The precise start and end dates of each arrangement must be established from original accommodation and service records.
The investigation should not invent a commencement date or assume that all reported events occurred on one day.
The following subjects require separate chronological entries.
A. Accommodation and occupancy
	•	Date of arrival.
	•	Nature of the occupancy arrangement.
	•	Any written agreement.
	•	Rent or contribution arrangements.
	•	Whether the author received a copy of the terms.
	•	Any changes to the arrangement.
	•	Any notice or termination process.
	•	Any dispute about legal status.
B. Support delivery
	•	Provider identity.
	•	Service agreement.
	•	Support roster.
	•	Actual assistance.
	•	Missed or cancelled services.
	•	Requests for additional support.
	•	Complaints and responses.
	•	Changes in personnel.
	•	Support coordination activity.
C. Financial administration
	•	Income received.
	•	Authorised expenditure.
	•	Rent payments.
	•	Allowances.
	•	Financial restrictions.
	•	Requests for access to money.
	•	Any discrepancy between available funds and actual access.
D. Personal welfare and practical circumstances
	•	Access to food.
	•	Access to medication.
	•	Access to transport.
	•	Access to medical appointments.
	•	Access to communication.
	•	Access to personal belongings.
	•	Ability to make decisions about daily living.
	•	Practical arrangements for Crystal.
These matters should be recorded as events only where the supporting source and date can be identified.
The author’s testimony remains important, particularly where no formal record was created. It must simply be labelled as testimony rather than converted into an official finding.
