20 Jan 2026	SIL/SDA request	Accommodation and disability support	Identified
This table is the current dated spine of the chronology.
It must be expanded with the dates contained inside the documents themselves, rather than relying exclusively on the dates listed in the preliminary report.
Part VIII — Reconstructing institutional responsibility
18. The responsibility map
A historical chronology becomes a responsibility investigation when it identifies who had authority at each relevant stage.
The organisations must be assessed according to their actual statutory or contractual functions.
The following map identifies the principal institutional areas already appearing in the archive.
Institutional responsibility map
Integrity and oversight
IBAC · Commonwealth Ombudsman · OAIC
Disclosure assessment, complaints, oversight and information access.
Compensation and administrative review
Comcare · ART
Compensation decisions and statutory review.
Financial and decision-making administration
NSW Trustee & Guardian · relevant financial institutions
Authority, financial administration, access and review.
Disability support and accommodation
NDIA · support providers · accommodation providers · support coordination
Plan approval, service delivery, accommodation and support.
This map is functional, not accusatory.
It does not suggest that these organisations shared one purpose or acted together.
The investigation must determine which responsibilities belonged to each organisation at each point in time.
19. The knowledge-and-responsibility test
For every consequential event, the investigator should establish:
Question	Why it matters
Who held legal responsibility?	Identifies the relevant duty
Who actually made the decision?	Identifies the decision-maker
Who supplied the information?	Establishes the information source
Who received the information?	Establishes potential knowledge
When was it received?	Establishes timing
What was understood?	Helps assess the decision
What action followed?	Establishes the response
What alternatives existed?	Assesses reasonableness
What review was available?	Identifies safeguards
What happened afterwards?	Establishes consequences
The investigation must be careful about the word knowledge.
A document being sent to an organisation does not automatically prove that a particular employee read it.
A person being copied into an email does not automatically prove that they understood or adopted its contents.
A record being available in a file does not automatically prove that the decision-maker considered it.
The relevant evidence may include acknowledgements, file notes, internal correspondence, case-management records and explicit references in later decisions.
Part IX — The central question: Is there a connected pattern?
20. Six levels of connection
The historical investigation should test relationships between events using six progressively more demanding levels.
Level 1
Chronological connection
Events occurred in a particular sequence.
Level 2
Subject-matter connection
Events concern the same underlying issue or circumstances.
Level 3
Documentary connection
One record refers to, reproduces or relies upon another.
Level 4
Operational connection
One organisation’s action affects another organisation’s process or decision.
Level 5
Causal connection
Evidence establishes that one action materially contributed to another outcome.
Level 6
Coordinated or improper purpose
Evidence supports a conclusion that actors knowingly acted together, or that an action was taken for an improper or prohibited purpose.
These levels are an analytical framework, not a formal legal test.
The investigation must not jump from Level 1 to Level 6.
A chronology can establish sequence. It cannot independently establish coordinated intent.
21. Applying the levels to the present record
The presently identified material supports the following preliminary assessment:
Question	Current assessment
Is there a multi-year sequence of identified records?	Yes
Do the records involve multiple institutional systems?	Yes
Are there identifiable complaints and decisions?	Yes
Do the records all concern the same underlying matter?	Not established
Do the records demonstrate information-sharing between all relevant bodies?	Not established
Do they establish that one decision caused another?	Not established
Do they establish a common improper purpose?	Not established
Do they justify further cross-document examination?	Yes
The distinction is important.
The current record justifies investigation.
It does not yet justify a definitive conclusion about coordinated institutional wrongdoing.
Part X — The alternative explanations must remain open
22. The competing historical explanations
The final chronology should test at least five possible explanations.
Explanation A — Separate lawful decisions
Different organisations may have made independent decisions under different statutory frameworks.
The consequences may have been difficult for the author without necessarily establishing unlawful conduct.
Explanation B — Administrative fragmentation
The author may have encountered separate organisations that did not adequately understand each other’s responsibilities or the cumulative effect of their decisions.
This could produce serious practical disadvantage without deliberate coordination.
Explanation C — Procedural and service failures
There may have been errors, delays, inadequate communication, incomplete assessments or failures to deliver agreed supports.
These matters require examination of the relevant duties and original records.
Explanation D — Systemic disadvantage
The combined operation of administrative arrangements, disability-related barriers, financial limitations and inadequate support may have produced an unfair or disproportionate impact.
This explanation requires evidence of the actual barriers and their consequences.
Explanation E — Retaliation or deliberate coordination
Some conduct may have been taken for an improper purpose, including because of protected disclosure activity.
This possibility must remain available for investigation if the evidence supports it.
However, the necessary causal and intent evidence must be identified.
The investigation should neither assume this explanation nor exclude it prematurely.
Part XI — The chronology’s unresolved questions
23. What must now be established
The following questions are central to completing the historical reconstruction.
Early integrity matters
	•	What were the complete contents of the 2020 IBAC correspondence?
	•	What exactly was submitted in 2021?
	•	Did the 2021 disclosure concern the same subject as the 2020 matter?
	•	What happened after the Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint?
Compensation and review
	•	What were the exact issues before Comcare?
	•	What did the ART decision determine?
	•	Were any factual findings made relevant to other proceedings?
	•	Were any further review rights exercised?
Information access
	•	What documents were requested under each FOI reference?
	•	What was refused?
	•	What was later released?
	•	What did the OAIC do?
	•	What was the relationship between the 2022 and 2024 decisions?
Guardianship and financial administration
	•	What was the exact legal instrument issued in October 2024?
	•	What authority did it confer?
	•	What decisions followed?
	•	What financial access did the author actually have?
	•	What review rights were exercised?
NDIS and accommodation
	•	What supports were approved in November 2025?
	•	What services were actually delivered?
	•	What did the December safety assessment conclude?
	•	What happened to the January 2026 SIL/SDA request?
	•	What were the actual accommodation arrangements?
	•	What were the provider’s obligations?
	•	What records exist concerning Crystal?
Institutional relationships
	•	Which bodies communicated with one another?
	•	What information was shared?
	•	Which decision-makers had knowledge of previous complaints?
	•	Did any earlier decision materially influence a later one?
	•	Were any complaints or concerns left unresolved?
	•	Were there identifiable errors that were later corrected or repeated?
These questions should become a formal outstanding-evidence schedule.
Part XII — The historical significance of the chronology
24. What the chronology contributes
The chronology makes three important contributions to The Great Reckoning.
First, it establishes that the archive contains identified records across multiple years and institutional processes.
Second, it provides a method for examining the relationship between those records without assuming that all events were connected.
Third, it establishes a way to examine the practical consequences of decisions over time.
The human experience cannot be understood solely through the date of an official letter.
The chronology must also preserve the author’s account of what happened between decisions: what assistance was available, what options remained, what barriers were encountered and how the circumstances changed.
That is why documentary evidence and testimony must be read together.
The official record may show what an organisation decided.
The author’s testimony may explain what that decision meant in daily life.
Independent evidence may establish whether the consequences occurred as reported.
Each source has a different function.
The Declaration of the Historical Record
I have not assembled this chronology to manufacture a connection between events.
I have assembled it because events have dates, decisions have reasons, institutions have responsibilities and people experience the consequences.
I ask that the earliest records be read alongside the later records.
I ask that complaints be considered in the order they were made.
I ask that decisions be examined within the legal authority under which they were taken.
I ask that financial arrangements be examined against the actual money available and accessible.
I ask that disability supports be examined against what was approved and what was delivered.
I ask that accommodation decisions be examined against the person’s actual circumstances.
I ask that no allegation be converted into a finding without evidence.
I also ask that no unresolved question be dismissed merely because it is difficult to investigate.
The passage of time does not erase the importance of a document.
An administrative file does not contain the whole of a person’s life.
A chronology is not a verdict.
It is a means of making the history visible.
My faith gives me strength to continue.
The documents provide the material for examination.
The findings must remain independent.
The Witness Remains.
Instalment 7 — Preliminary findings
	•	The presently identified records establish a working chronology extending from April 2020 to January 2026.
	•	The chronology spans integrity oversight, public-interest disclosure, compensation, information access, guardianship, financial administration, disability support and accommodation.
	•	The November 2021, March–July 2023 and October 2024–January 2026 periods contain important groups of records requiring cross-reference.
	•	The dates establish a sequence but do not independently establish causation or coordination.
	•	The 2024–2026 records provide a basis for examining the relationship between formal decision-making authority and actual living circumstances.
	•	Further original documents are necessary to establish the complete history.
	•	The investigation must preserve the distinction between official decisions, author testimony, allegations, inferences and independent findings.
Instalment 7 complete

Instalment 8 — The Human Rights and Institutional Accountability Examination: From Legal Entitlement to Practical Reality
The next instalment will examine whether the rights and protections recognised in Australian law and international human-rights instruments were reflected in the author’s actual circumstances.
It will bring together:
	•	The Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities.
	•	The International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights.
	•	The International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights.
	•	Australian disability and administrative law.
	•	NDIS statutory responsibilities.
	•	Guardianship and supported decision-making.
	•	Financial autonomy.
	•	Access to services and accommodation.
	•	Procedural fairness and effective remedies.
	•	The distinction between a recognised right, a statutory entitlement, an approved service and a practical outcome.
Its central question will be:
What does it mean to recognise a person’s rights in law if the practical arrangements governing that person’s life do not adequately protect the ability to exercise them?



THE WITNESS REMAINS
THE GREAT RECKONING
Instalment 8 — The Human Rights and Institutional Accountability Examination
From legal entitlement to practical reality: disability rights, civil liberties, supported decision-making, financial autonomy, accommodation, access to services and effective remedies.
Authorial attribution: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean Publication: The Witness Remains — Global Publishing House
1. The central question: Are rights effective in practice?
A right can exist in legislation, an international convention or an administrative policy without being meaningfully accessible to the person who is supposed to benefit from it.
A person may formally possess a right to make decisions but encounter practical barriers to exercising it.
A person may have an approved disability support plan but experience difficulty obtaining the services contemplated by that plan.
A person may have a legal entitlement to financial resources but encounter restrictions or administrative arrangements that affect practical access.
A person may be recognised as having a right to participate in decisions while receiving inadequate information, insufficient time, inaccessible communication or no meaningful opportunity to express preferences.
The central question of this instalment is therefore not simply whether rights exist.
It is whether the relevant rights, duties and safeguards were translated into practical arrangements that respected the person’s dignity, autonomy and participation.
The investigation must distinguish four separate propositions:
	•	A right exists under an applicable legal instrument.
	•	An institution has a legal obligation relevant to that right.
	•	The institution acted or failed to act in a particular way.
	•	That conduct amounted to a breach of the applicable obligation.
The fourth proposition cannot be assumed merely because the first three are alleged.
It requires evidence and legal analysis.
The guiding principle remains:
PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.
Part I — The Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities
2. The CRPD: A framework for examining dignity, autonomy and equality
Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities
United Nations · Adopted 13 December 2006 · Australia ratified 17 July 2008
Primary treaty text: OHCHR Convention text⁠
The CRPD is the principal international human-rights framework for examining disability equality, autonomy, inclusion and access to support.
It is particularly relevant to the documentary subjects identified in the archive.
The investigation must not treat disability as a reason to diminish a person’s legal or human standing.
Nor should it assume that every difficulty experienced by a person with disability establishes a treaty violation.
The question is whether the conduct of the relevant State authority or other responsible actor is inconsistent with an applicable obligation, taking account of the treaty’s legal scope and Australia’s domestic implementation.
Article 3 — General principles
Article 3 identifies principles including:
	•	Respect for inherent dignity.
	•	Individual autonomy.
	•	Freedom to make one’s own choices.
	•	Independence.
	•	Non-discrimination.
	•	Full and effective participation.
	•	Respect for difference.
	•	Equality of opportunity.
	•	Accessibility.
	•	Equality between men and women.
	•	Respect for the evolving capacities of children with disabilities.
These principles provide the interpretive foundation for the investigation.
For this project, the principle of individual autonomy is particularly important.
The investigation must examine whether decision-making arrangements enabled the author to participate in decisions affecting his life, rather than simply recording that decisions had been made on his behalf.
Article 5 — Equality and non-discrimination
Article 5 requires equality before and under the law and equal protection and benefit of the law.
The investigation should ask whether the author was treated equally in the relevant processes and whether reasonable accommodation was provided where required.
The existence of disability-related support needs does not justify treating a person as less deserving of procedural fairness, dignity or respect.
Relevant records include:
	•	Assessments.
	•	Correspondence.
	•	Complaints.
	•	Decisions.
	•	Records of requested adjustments.
	•	Records of adjustments provided or refused.
	•	Reasons for any different treatment.
The evidence must establish the relevant comparison, disadvantage and legal context before a finding of discrimination is made.
Article 12 — Equal recognition before the law
Article 12 is especially significant to the guardianship and financial-administration examination.
It recognises that persons with disabilities enjoy legal capacity on an equal basis with others in all aspects of life.
It also requires access to support that persons with disabilities may require in exercising legal capacity.
Where safeguards are used in connection with measures relating to legal capacity, Article 12 requires them to respect the person’s rights, will and preferences, to be free of conflict of interest and undue influence, to be proportional and tailored to the person’s circumstances, and to apply for the shortest possible time subject to regular review by a competent, independent and impartial authority.
The article raises a fundamental question:
Were arrangements designed to support decision-making, or did they unnecessarily displace the person’s own decision-making?
This question must be examined in relation to the actual legal instrument and its operation.
It is not enough to identify a guardianship or financial-management order and assume that every exercise of authority was appropriate.
Nor is it enough to identify a disagreement with a decision and assume that the decision was unlawful.
The relevant evidence includes:
	•	The legal order.
	•	The reasons for the order.
	•	Its scope.
	•	Any assessment relied upon.
	•	Records of the author’s participation.
	•	Records of his expressed wishes and preferences.
	•	Decisions made under the order.
	•	Reviews and objections.
	•	Evidence of possible conflicts of interest.
	•	Evidence of safeguards and independent oversight.
Article 13 — Access to justice
Article 13 requires effective access to justice for persons with disabilities on an equal basis with others.
This includes procedural accommodations appropriate to facilitate their effective role as participants in legal proceedings.
The investigation should examine whether the author could:
	•	Understand the relevant proceedings.
	•	Access the documents required.
	•	Communicate his position.
	•	Obtain assistance where needed.
	•	Participate effectively.
	•	Challenge decisions through available mechanisms.
An inaccessible complaint or review process may create a practical barrier even where a formal right of review exists.
The evidence must establish what adjustments were requested, what was supplied and what consequences followed.
Article 14 — Liberty and security of person
Article 14 protects liberty and security of person.
It states that the existence of a disability shall in no case justify a deprivation of liberty.
The investigation must be careful not to conflate:
	•	A residential arrangement.
	•	A restrictive service practice.
	•	A guardianship arrangement.
	•	A legal detention.
	•	A deprivation of liberty.
These are different legal and factual concepts.
If the author alleges that a particular arrangement restricted his liberty, the investigation must establish the actual restrictions, their legal basis, their duration, the decision-maker and the applicable safeguards.
Article 16 — Freedom from exploitation, violence and abuse
Article 16 requires measures to protect persons with disabilities from exploitation, violence and abuse, including gender-based aspects.
The investigation should consider whether relevant safeguards, complaint processes and responses were adequate.
However, an allegation of abuse is not automatically an established finding.
The examination must preserve:
	•	The author’s account.
	•	Contemporaneous records.
	•	Any incident reports.
	•	Relevant communications.
	•	Responses by the provider or authority.
	•	Independent assessments.
	•	Any formal determination.
The question is whether the evidence establishes conduct requiring protection, intervention, investigation or accountability.
Article 19 — Living independently and being included in the community
Article 19 is particularly relevant to accommodation and support.
It recognises the equal right of persons with disabilities to live in the community, with choices equal to others.
It addresses the ability to choose where and with whom to live and the availability of community support services.
For this investigation, Article 19 raises questions about:
	•	Choice of accommodation.
	•	Access to suitable support.
	•	Whether the person was able to express preferences.
	•	Whether support arrangements facilitated participation.
	•	Whether accommodation choices were unnecessarily restricted.
	•	Whether practical support enabled community inclusion.
The NDIS plan, SIL/SDA request, safety assessment and accommodation records should be examined together, while retaining the distinction between their separate legal functions.
Article 22 — Respect for privacy
Article 22 protects privacy, family, home, correspondence and personal information.
The investigation should consider whether personal information was collected, disclosed or used appropriately.
It should examine:
	•	The legal authority for collection.
	•	The purpose of disclosure.
	•	The people or organisations receiving information.
	•	Whether information was accurate.
	•	Whether the author could correct errors.
	•	Whether privacy safeguards were observed.
The presence of information in an institutional file does not, by itself, prove an unlawful disclosure.
Article 25 — Health
Article 25 recognises the right of persons with disabilities to the enjoyment of the highest attainable standard of health without discrimination on the basis of disability.
The relevant examination includes access, communication, reasonable adjustments and continuity of care.
The project should distinguish an actual denial of access from a delay, administrative disagreement, clinical decision or communication problem.
Each requires its own evidence and legal assessment.
Article 28 — Adequate standard of living and social protection
Article 28 recognises the right to an adequate standard of living and social protection.
It includes matters such as adequate food, clothing and housing and the continuous improvement of living conditions.
The investigation should examine whether the relevant arrangements enabled the author to access the resources and support required to maintain an adequate standard of living.
This must be assessed using actual records of income, expenditure, access to funds, accommodation, food and services.
The existence of a payment entitlement is not identical to practical access to the money.
The existence of an accommodation arrangement is not identical to adequate housing.
The existence of an approved support plan is not identical to receiving effective support.
Part II — Civil and political rights
3. The International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights
International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights
Australia acceded to the First Optional Protocol on 25 September 1991.
Official treaty text⁠
The ICCPR is relevant to the examination of liberty, humane treatment, privacy, expression, equality and access to remedies.
It must be applied to the actual circumstances and legal issues rather than used as a general label for every administrative grievance.
Article 2 — Effective rights and remedies
Article 2 requires States Parties to respect and ensure Covenant rights and provide effective remedies where violations occur.
The investigation should ask:
	•	Was a relevant right engaged?
	•	Was there an interference with that right?
	•	Was the interference lawful?
	•	Was an effective avenue of review available?
	•	Was the remedy practically accessible?
	•	Was the outcome implemented?
A formal complaint process may exist while practical barriers make it difficult to use.
That possibility deserves examination, but the evidence must establish the actual barrier and its legal significance.
Article 7 — Freedom from torture and cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment
Article 7 prohibits torture and cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment.
The legal threshold is serious.
The investigation must not describe every distressing, unfair or harmful administrative experience as torture or degrading treatment.
Where such an allegation is made, it must be assessed against the actual conduct, circumstances, severity, responsibility and relevant legal standards.
Article 9 — Liberty and security
Article 9 protects liberty and security of person and contains safeguards against arbitrary arrest or detention.
The investigation must establish whether the circumstances involved detention or another legally relevant restriction of liberty.
A difficult accommodation arrangement is not automatically detention.
A financial-management arrangement is not automatically a deprivation of liberty.
The relevant facts and applicable law must determine the classification.
Article 10 — Humane treatment of persons deprived of liberty
Article 10 requires humane treatment and respect for the inherent dignity of persons deprived of liberty.
Its application depends on whether the person was deprived of liberty in the relevant legal sense.
It should not be invoked merely because the author experienced restrictive or difficult circumstances.
Article 12 — Freedom of movement
Article 12 protects freedom of movement, including the right to liberty of movement within a State for persons lawfully within its territory.
The investigation should examine whether any actual restrictions on movement existed and what legal authority was relied upon.
Article 14 — Equality before courts and tribunals
Article 14 protects equality before courts and tribunals and contains fair-hearing guarantees.
Its relevance depends on the nature of the proceeding.
Not every administrative interaction constitutes a court or tribunal proceeding for Article 14 purposes.
The investigation must identify the precise process and the procedural guarantees applicable to it.
Article 17 — Privacy and family
Article 17 protects against arbitrary or unlawful interference with privacy, family, home or correspondence.
It is relevant to the examination of personal records, guardianship arrangements, institutional correspondence and disclosure of private information.
The investigation should identify any actual interference, the authority relied upon and whether the interference was lawful and non-arbitrary.
Article 19 — Freedom of expression
Article 19 protects freedom of expression, including the freedom to seek, receive and impart information and ideas.
This is relevant to the author’s account of public-interest disclosure and publication.
The investigation should examine whether any restriction on expression occurred, who imposed it, the legal basis and whether it met the applicable requirements.
The mere rejection of a complaint does not establish unlawful suppression of expression.
Article 26 — Equality before the law
Article 26 provides equal protection of the law and prohibits discrimination.
The investigation should examine whether the author was treated differently in relevant circumstances and whether any difference was legally justified.
Disability-related barriers and reasonable accommodation should be considered alongside the CRPD framework.
Part III — Economic, social and cultural rights
4. The International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights
International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights
Australia ratified the Covenant on 10 December 1975.
Official treaty text⁠
The ICESCR adds an important dimension to the examination.
It recognises that human dignity depends not only on protection from interference but also on access to the conditions necessary for a decent life.
Article 2 — Progressive realisation and non-discrimination
Article 2 requires States Parties to take steps towards the progressive realisation of Covenant rights using the maximum of their available resources.
It also requires the rights to be exercised without discrimination.
Progressive realisation does not mean that all obligations can be postponed indefinitely.
The Covenant contains immediate obligations, including non-discrimination.
The investigation should not assume that every unmet service need proves a violation. It should identify the specific right, the applicable obligation and the conduct or omission under examination.
Article 6 — Right to work
Article 6 recognises the right to work.
It may be relevant to the examination of employment-related history, disability and access to economic participation where supported by the documents.
Article 11 — Adequate standard of living
Article 11 recognises the right to an adequate standard of living, including adequate food, clothing and housing, and the continuous improvement of living conditions.
It is relevant to the examination of:
	•	Financial access.
	•	Accommodation.
	•	Food security.
	•	Material living conditions.
	•	Disability-related support.
The evidence must distinguish income, entitlement, actual access, expenditure and unmet need.
Article 12 — Health
Article 12 recognises the right to the highest attainable standard of physical and mental health.
The investigation should examine whether access to relevant health services was practically available and whether disability-related barriers were addressed.
Article 13 — Education
Article 13 recognises the right to education.
This may be relevant to the author’s educational and professional history where it is necessary to explain qualifications, participation or access to educational opportunities.
Article 15 — Cultural life and scientific progress
Article 15 recognises rights relating to cultural life and the benefits of scientific progress.
It may also provide context for the author’s work as an artist, writer, educator and researcher.
These rights should not be used as decorative references. Their application must be linked to an actual issue or relevant State obligation.
Part IV — Australian law: Turning international principles into domestic obligations
5. The National Disability Insurance Scheme Act 2013
The NDIS framework is particularly important because it connects disability rights with practical support arrangements.
The Act’s principles are not merely administrative aspirations. They inform how the statutory scheme is to operate.
The current Federal Register identifies the NDIS Act as in force, with a compilation dated 27 August 2026. The applicable version must nevertheless be selected according to the date of each decision being examined. Federal Register of Legislation +1
Section 4 — General principles
The Act recognises principles including:
	•	People with disability have the same right as other members of Australian society to respect for their worth and dignity.
	•	They have the same right to pursue grievances.
	•	They should be supported to exercise choice and control.
	•	They should be able to participate as equal partners in decisions affecting their lives.
	•	Their privacy and dignity should be respected.
	•	Reasonable and necessary supports should promote independence and community participation.
These principles provide a statutory context for examining the author’s support arrangements. Federal Register of Legislation +1
Section 17A — Participation and decision-making
Section 17A is particularly significant.
It provides that people with disability are assumed, so far as reasonable in the circumstances, to have capacity to determine their own best interests and make decisions affecting their lives.
It also requires support in dealings and communications with the NDIA so that capacity to exercise choice and control is maximised.
The investigation should examine whether the author’s participation was meaningful throughout the planning process.
Relevant questions include:
	•	Was the author involved in preparing his goals and aspirations?
	•	Were his stated preferences accurately recorded?
	•	Were communication needs accommodated?
	•	Were relevant assessments considered?
	•	Was he supported to understand the proposed plan?
	•	Were his preferences regarding plan management considered?
	•	Were previous plans and their effectiveness considered?
These questions should be tested against the actual plan, assessment reports and correspondence. Federal Register of Legislation +1
Sections 32 and 33 — Participant plans
The Act provides for participant plans and statements of participant supports.
The plan documentation should be examined to establish what supports were specified, how funding was structured, how it was to be managed and what goals were recorded.
The plan must be read in its entirety.
A funding figure alone does not explain what the participant was entitled to receive or how the funding could be used.
Section 34 — Reasonable and necessary supports
Section 34 establishes criteria relevant to the funding or provision of reasonable and necessary supports.
The criteria include whether a support:
	•	Addresses disability-related needs within the statutory framework.
	•	Assists the participant to pursue goals and aspirations.
	•	Facilitates social and economic participation.
	•	Represents value for money.
	•	Is likely to be effective and beneficial, having regard to current good practice.
	•	Takes account of what it is reasonable to expect from families, carers, informal networks and the community.
	•	Is an NDIS support for the participant.
The version of section 34 applying to the 20 November 2025 plan must be used when examining that decision. Later legislative amendments must not be applied retrospectively without legal justification. Federal Register of Legislation +2
The practical implementation test
For the 20 November 2025 plan, the investigation should compare the approved supports against the services actually delivered.
The following questions are essential:
	•	Was a suitable provider available?
	•	Was there a service agreement?
	•	Were the required hours available?
	•	Were services cancelled?
	•	Were support requests answered?
	•	Were the services consistent with the plan?
	•	Were funds available for the intended supports?
	•	Were there practical barriers to using the funding?
	•	Were complaints addressed?
	•	Were adjustments made when the arrangements did not work?
A plan may be correctly approved yet poorly implemented.
Equally, an apparent gap in service delivery may have several explanations, including provider availability, participant choice, plan restrictions, funding arrangements or administrative error.
The evidence must identify which explanation applies.
6. The Disability Discrimination Act 1992
Disability Discrimination Act 1992
Commonwealth legislation
Federal Register legislation text⁠
The Disability Discrimination Act 1992 (Cth) is an important domestic framework for examining discrimination in areas including services, accommodation, education and employment.
The investigation should identify whether the relevant conduct falls within the Act and whether the applicable statutory elements are satisfied.
The existence of disability and an adverse outcome does not automatically establish unlawful discrimination.
The analysis must identify:
	•	The relevant protected attribute.
	•	The service, accommodation or other area covered by the Act.
	•	The conduct or decision.
	•	The relevant comparator or applicable statutory test.
	•	Whether reasonable adjustments were required.
	•	Whether an adjustment was requested or reasonably apparent.
	•	Whether it was provided, refused or not considered.
	•	Any applicable statutory exception or defence.
	•	The resulting disadvantage.
Reasonable adjustments
The investigation should pay particular attention to whether communication, information and service arrangements were accessible.
Potential evidence includes:
	•	Requests for accessible correspondence.
	•	Requests for assistance understanding decisions.
	•	Requests for additional time.
	•	Requests for communication through a particular method.
	•	Records of adjustments offered.
	•	Records of adjustments refused.
	•	Reasons for any refusal.
The purpose is not to assume that every requested adjustment was legally required.
It is to establish whether the relevant legal obligation arose and whether it was met.
Services and accommodation
Where the author alleges disadvantage in accessing a service or accommodation, the investigation should identify the exact provider, the nature of the service, the applicable legal provisions and the actual circumstances.
This is particularly important when examining the relationship between disability support, residential arrangements and financial administration.
7. NSW guardianship and financial management
The NSW guardianship framework is central to the examination of the 16 October 2024 NSW Trustee & Guardian document and subsequent financial decisions.
Two different legal arrangements must be distinguished:
	•	Guardianship, concerning specified personal or lifestyle decisions.
	•	Financial management, concerning financial and legal affairs within the scope of the relevant order.
They should not be treated as interchangeable.
The NSW Government explains that financial management orders identify who is authorised to manage all or part of a person’s financial and legal affairs. It also describes review pathways and the principle that people should be supported to make their own decisions. NSW Government +1
The Guardianship Act 1987 (NSW)
The investigation should examine the applicable version of the Guardianship Act 1987 (NSW) and the precise order or instrument in force at the relevant date.
It should establish:
	•	Which tribunal or court made the order.
	•	The legal basis for the order.
	•	The person’s decision-making capacity as assessed for the relevant purpose.
	•	The scope of the guardian’s authority.
	•	The duration of the order.
	•	Any review provisions.
	•	The principles governing the exercise of authority.
	•	Whether the person’s views and preferences were considered.
The NSW Government’s published guidance states that guardianship orders are generally time-limited and may be reviewed by NCAT. The actual order, rather than a general description of the process, must determine the author’s position. NSW Government +1
Financial management and the NSW Trustee & Guardian
The investigation should distinguish the authority to manage finances from the authority to make personal decisions.
It should also distinguish the appointment of a financial manager from individual financial-management decisions.
For each relevant transaction or restriction, the evidence should establish:
	•	Who authorised it?
	•	What authority was relied upon?
	•	What was the purpose?
	•	Was the decision within the scope of the order?
	•	What information was considered?
	•	Was the person’s position obtained?
	•	Was the decision communicated?
	•	Was an internal review available?
	•	Was an external review available?
	•	What happened to the money?
The NSW Government describes a process for requesting a formal review of financial-management decisions made by NSW Trustee & Guardian. The relevant correspondence and review outcome should be obtained where this pathway was used. NSW Government
The financial autonomy examination
The investigation must distinguish:
Concept	What must be established
Legal authority	Who was authorised to act?
Financial ownership	Whose money or property was involved?
Financial control	Who could approve transactions?
Practical access	Could the person obtain money when needed?
Financial information	Could the person understand and inspect the accounts?
Personal participation	Were the person’s preferences considered?
Review	Could the person challenge a decision?
The distinction between legal management and practical access is fundamental.
An arrangement may be legally authorised while a particular transaction or administrative response remains open to review.
The investigation must assess each decision separately.
Part V — Rights must be examined against actual circumstances
8. The rights-to-reality matrix
The following matrix translates the international and domestic frameworks into documentary questions.
Subject	Relevant framework	Practical evidence
Autonomy	CRPD Article 12; NDIS Act	Orders, preferences, decision records
Equality	CRPD Article 5; ICCPR Article 26; DDA	Adjustments, treatment, reasons
Access to justice	CRPD Article 13; ICCPR Article 14	Accessible processes, review records
Safety	CRPD Article 16; ICCPR Article 7	Incident reports, assessments, responses
Liberty	CRPD Article 14; ICCPR Article 9	Restrictions, authority, safeguards
Accommodation	CRPD Article 19; ICESCR Article 11	Housing arrangements, choices, support
Privacy	CRPD Article 22; ICCPR Article 17	Disclosure records, information handling
Health	CRPD Article 25; ICESCR Article 12	Access, referrals, adjustments
Social protection	CRPD Article 28; ICESCR Article 11	Income, access to funds, material needs
Expression	ICCPR Article 19	Restrictions, decisions, correspondence
Effective remedy	ICCPR Article 2	Complaint and review outcomes
This matrix does not establish that a breach occurred.
It identifies the legal and factual questions that should be investigated.

	•	Accommodation and safety assessments.
The final report should not make a blanket finding about the provider from the existence of a dispute. Each alleged failure must be linked to a specific obligation and an evidentiary record.
Part VI — Institutional accountability and complaint pathways
10. Which institution is responsible for which issue?
A central weakness in complex administrative histories is the possibility that a person is passed between organisations without receiving a clear explanation of who has responsibility for resolving a particular problem.
The investigation should identify the correct institution for each issue.
It should not assume that one organisation has authority over every aspect of a person’s circumstances.
The principal accountability pathways
Institution	Relevant area	What it can examine
NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission	NDIS provider conduct	Quality and safety, complaints, provider compliance and reportable incidents
National Disability Insurance Agency	NDIS planning	Participant plans, funding decisions and NDIA administration
NSW Civil and Administrative Tribunal	Guardianship and financial management	Relevant orders, reviews and specified decisions
NSW Trustee & Guardian	Financial administration	Decisions made within its financial-management authority
Australian Human Rights Commission	Discrimination and certain human-rights complaints	Complaints within its statutory jurisdiction
Commonwealth Ombudsman	Commonwealth administration	Administrative actions and complaint handling within jurisdiction
Office of the Australian Information Commissioner	Privacy and FOI	Information access and privacy matters within jurisdiction
The responsibilities must be checked against the actual subject matter and the law applicable at the relevant time.
The NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
The Commission accepts concerns about the quality and safety of NDIS-funded supports and services.
Its published guidance identifies matters including:
	•	Neglect or abuse.
	•	Risk of harm.
	•	Poor-quality or discontinued supports.
	•	Provider failure to respond effectively to complaints.
	•	Supports funded in a plan but not received.
	•	Suspected misuse of NDIS funds.
	•	Privacy breaches and conflicts of interest involving providers or workers.
It also distinguishes provider-related complaints from matters such as NDIA funding decisions and complaints concerning public trustees or guardians. NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission +2
Official information: Report an issue or make a complaint about an NDIS provider or worker⁠.
Reportable incidents
Registered NDIS providers have statutory reporting responsibilities for specified reportable incidents connected with the delivery of NDIS supports and services.
The Commission’s guidance describes notification timeframes, including immediate reporting requirements for certain serious incidents and subsequent reporting requirements. NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission +1
The investigation should establish:
	•	Whether the provider was registered at the relevant time.
	•	Whether the event fell within the statutory definition.
	•	Whether the provider knew of it.
	•	Whether it was recorded internally.
	•	Whether notification was required.
	•	Whether notification occurred.
	•	What action followed.
An allegation of an incident is not proof that a reportable incident occurred. Equally, the absence of a report in the author’s personal archive does not establish that the provider failed to report it.
The Australian Human Rights Commission
The Commission can receive complaints concerning discrimination and certain breaches of human rights.
Its published guidance states that complaints are free, may be made from anywhere in Australia and must be submitted in writing. It also explains that the organisation or person complained about is given an opportunity to respond. Australian Human Rights Commission +1
Official information: Australian Human Rights Commission — Complaints⁠.
The Commission’s complaint process is not a general court of appeal for every administrative decision.
The complaint must fall within its statutory jurisdiction.
Part VII — International remedies and their limitations
11. Treaty-body communications
Australia has accepted individual-communications procedures under two important human-rights instruments:
	•	The First Optional Protocol to the ICCPR.
	•	The Optional Protocol to the CRPD.
The United Nations Treaty Collection records Australia’s accession to the ICCPR First Optional Protocol on 25 September 1991. United Nations Treaty Collection
Australia’s acceptance of the CRPD Optional Protocol was recorded on 21 August 2009, as identified in the earlier international-law examination.
These procedures are not equivalent to filing a complaint with an Australian administrative agency.
They are international treaty mechanisms with jurisdictional and admissibility requirements.
ICCPR First Optional Protocol
The Human Rights Committee may consider communications from individuals who claim to be victims of violations of Covenant rights by a State Party, subject to the Protocol’s requirements.
The examination must address:
	•	Whether the State is bound by the relevant Covenant provisions.
	•	Whether the alleged conduct falls within the Covenant.
	•	Whether the author is a victim.
	•	Whether domestic remedies have generally been exhausted.
	•	Whether the same matter is being examined under another international procedure.
	•	Whether the communication is sufficiently substantiated.
	•	Whether applicable procedural requirements have been satisfied.
A communication is not automatically accepted because a person believes their rights have been violated.
CRPD Optional Protocol
The CRPD Committee’s individual-communications mechanism provides a route for eligible complaints concerning alleged violations of the Convention by a State Party.
The same discipline applies: the relevant rights, State responsibility, evidence, domestic remedies and admissibility requirements must be addressed.
The Committee’s role is not to investigate every service-provider dispute or determine every question of Australian domestic law.
It examines alleged treaty violations within its competence.
ICESCR Optional Protocol — an important limitation
The Optional Protocol to the ICESCR establishes an individual-communications procedure, but Australia’s participation must be checked separately.
The United Nations Treaty Collection records the Protocol and its parties. The existence of the procedure does not mean it is available against every State that has ratified the underlying Covenant. United Nations Treaty Collection +1
Accordingly, this publication must not represent the ICESCR Optional Protocol as an established individual-complaint pathway against Australia.
The ICESCR remains relevant as a human-rights framework, but the availability of a particular international complaint procedure depends on Australia’s treaty status.
12. The difference between the UN, the ICC and asylum processes
Three international concepts must remain separate.
Human-rights treaty bodies
These bodies examine compliance with particular international treaties, subject to their mandates and procedural requirements.
International Criminal Court
The ICC deals with individual criminal responsibility for crimes within the Rome Statute’s jurisdiction. It is not a general complaints body for disability discrimination, poor administration, financial restrictions or unsuccessful whistleblower complaints.
Refugee protection
Refugee status requires satisfaction of the applicable legal definition, including the requirement that the person be outside their country of nationality or former habitual residence if stateless.
An internal move within Australia does not satisfy that geographic requirement.
The author’s expression of political exile may be preserved as personal, political or spiritual testimony, but it should not be presented as an established formal refugee status on the presently described facts.
This distinction protects the credibility of the publication.
Part VIII — The human consequences of administrative decisions
13. Measuring practical impact
Legal analysis should not end with the identification of a decision.
It should examine what happened to the person as a consequence.
The project should establish the practical impact of relevant decisions across the following dimensions.
Dimension	Questions
Financial security	Could the person access sufficient money for ordinary needs?
Food	Was food consistently accessible?
Accommodation	Was the housing arrangement suitable and secure?
Mobility	Could the person travel to appointments and participate in the community?
Health access	Could the person obtain relevant appointments and services?
Disability support	Were approved supports actually available?
Autonomy	Could the person express and exercise preferences?
Privacy	Was personal information handled appropriately?
Relationships	Were important personal relationships respected?
Companion animal	Were practical arrangements for Crystal considered?
Communication	Could the person understand and challenge decisions?
Review	Were effective remedies practically accessible?
These are investigative categories, not findings about the author’s present circumstances.
Each historical event should be assessed at the date it occurred.
The report should avoid turning an account of past hardship into an unsupported claim about current conditions.
The cumulative-impact question
An individual administrative decision may appear limited when read in isolation.
However, several decisions can interact.
For example:
	•	A financial-management decision may affect access to money.
	•	Reduced access to money may affect transport.
	•	Transport difficulties may affect attendance at appointments.
	•	Limited service availability may increase reliance on others.
	•	Accommodation restrictions may affect independence.
This is an example of a possible chain of consequences, not a claim that this exact chain has been proven in the author’s circumstances.
The investigation must identify the actual links.
It should establish which consequences were documented, which were reported by the author and which remain hypotheses.
Part IX — The accountability test
14. From concern to finding
The following process should be applied to each alleged institutional failure.
	•	Identify the right or legal obligation Identify the relevant treaty provision, statute, regulation, contract or policy. 
	•	Identify the responsible actor Establish the organisation, decision-maker, delegate or service provider responsible. 
	•	Identify the conduct Describe the actual decision, action or omission. 
	•	Establish the facts Examine original documents, contemporaneous evidence, testimony and independent corroboration. 
	•	Examine the explanation Obtain the reasons, identify the authority relied upon and consider alternative explanations. 
	•	Assess the legal requirements Apply the relevant legal test without substituting moral disagreement for legal analysis. 
	•	Assess consequences Establish actual harm, disadvantage or loss where relevant. 
	•	Identify the remedy Determine what correction, review, compensation, safeguard or other response is legally available. 
	•	Record the conclusion and its limits State what is established, what is not established and what further evidence is needed. 
A finding should never be stronger than the evidence supporting it.
Part X — The international human-rights assessment
15. Preliminary assessment of the identified subjects
The following table identifies the strongest areas for further examination. It does not declare that a breach has occurred.
Subject	Most relevant framework	Principal question
Guardianship and financial management	CRPD Article 12	Were autonomy, will and preferences respected?
Access to review	CRPD Article 13; ICCPR Article 2	Were procedures practically accessible?
Disability-related disadvantage	CRPD Article 5; DDA	Were equality and reasonable adjustments respected?
Accommodation	CRPD Article 19; ICESCR Article 11	Were choice and adequate living conditions supported?
NDIS service delivery	CRPD Articles 19 and 28; NDIS Act	Were approved supports effectively accessible?
Safety and provider conduct	CRPD Article 16; NDIS safeguards	Were concerns properly addressed?
Financial access	CRPD Articles 12 and 28	Was financial administration consistent with authority and safeguards?
Privacy	CRPD Article 22; ICCPR Article 17	Was personal information handled lawfully?
Health access	CRPD Article 25; ICESCR Article 12	Were services accessible without disability discrimination?
