These are not 50 independent findings. They are 50 documentary work items, some of which may contain multiple documents or may ultimately prove duplicative.
The distinction matters because the final register must count individual documents, not merely headings or bundles.
Part VI — The complete evidence architecture
8. How the 200-plus-document register should be constructed
The earlier publication brief identifies a goal of examining more than 200 significant documents.
That target should be pursued through a controlled inventory, not achieved by inventing entries or counting every mention of a document as a separate record.
The register should ultimately contain the following categories.
Category	Target examination scope
Public-interest disclosures and integrity bodies	All identified submissions, acknowledgments, allocation decisions, referrals and outcomes
Ombudsman and complaint processes	Original complaints, assessment notices, closure decisions and review correspondence
FOI and privacy	Requests, extensions, search correspondence, decisions, schedules and released records
Comcare and workers compensation	Claims, employment evidence, determinations, tribunal documents and review records
Guardianship and financial management	Orders, instruments, reviews, expenditure statements and decisions
NDIS and disability services	Plans, funding decisions, service agreements, provider records, incident reports and complaints
Accommodation and safeguarding	Occupancy records, assessments, support rosters, incident responses and service correspondence
Police and law enforcement oversight	Statements, event records, investigations, referrals and outcomes
Banking and insurance	Restrictions, notices, account records, policies, claims and complaint outcomes
Health and clinical records	Relevant assessments, referrals, consent records and treatment decisions
Human-rights and international correspondence	Submissions, acknowledgment letters, referrals and any formal responses
Personal testimony and contemporaneous statements	Original statements, emails, diary records and declarations
Digital preservation	Original files, repository versions, hashes, metadata and commit records
The target is to establish a complete inventory of significant documents within the defined repositories and periods, then identify which are genuinely relevant to the central questions.
A document should not be included merely because its title sounds important.
9. The cross-institutional relationship register
A second register is needed to identify where one institution’s action may have affected another process.
This is not a register of presumed conspiracy. It is a method for testing documentary relationships.
Relationship ID	Possible connection to examine	Evidence needed
X001	FOI records and subsequent administrative decisions	Evidence that later decision-makers relied on, or lacked access to, the requested records
X002	Guardianship authority and banking restrictions	Original order, bank instructions, bank interpretation and access logs
X003	Financial access and accommodation payments	Bank records, rent records, payment schedules and provider accounts
X004	NDIS plan approval and actual service delivery	Plan, service agreement, rosters, invoices and progress notes
X005	Safety assessment and provider response	Assessment, recommendations, provider communications and incident records
X006	Police complaints and safeguarding actions	Police event records, provider incident reports and referral records
X007	Public-interest disclosures and subsequent treatment	Disclosure contents, decision records, communications and evidence of any alleged reprisal
X008	Comcare employment classification and other institutional decisions	Employment records, statutory tests and actual reliance between decision-makers
X009	FOI refusal and later revised decision	Search records, scope correspondence, revised schedule and released documents
X010	Health access and financial or transport constraints	Appointment records, available funds, transport arrangements and contemporaneous communications
X011	Legal assistance and guardianship proceedings	Advice requests, eligibility decisions, court or tribunal deadlines and representation records
X012	Accommodation, Crystal and community participation	Occupancy records, support plans and evidence of actual access or restrictions
For each proposed relationship, the investigation must ask:
	•	Did the first event occur?
	•	Did the second event occur?
	•	Is there evidence that the second institution knew about the first?
	•	Is there evidence that it relied upon the first decision?
	•	Is there a demonstrated causal connection?
	•	Is there a reasonable alternative explanation?
	•	What legal or contractual obligation applied?
	•	What remedy or review mechanism existed?
A chronological relationship is not necessarily a causal relationship.
A causal relationship is not necessarily proof of improper purpose.
And evidence of improper purpose must be established through evidence of the relevant person’s knowledge, conduct, authority and intention.
Part VII — The documentary integrity examination
10. Authenticity, integrity and truth are different questions
The archive reports a substantial digital inventory, including 21,211 archive-path file instances, 3,215 unique complete PDFs, 3,667 unique PDF blobs including stubs, and 8,822 unique file blobs.
The same methodology reports 2,655 lines in SHA256SUMS.txt, representing 2,569 distinct SHA-256 values.
These are reported archive measurements, not independently audited findings.
The methodology also acknowledges that the published hashes were not all recomputed against the inventory.
This means that the digital preservation work is important, but the following questions must remain separate.
Question	What it establishes
Does a file exist at a repository path?	Its presence at that location
Does its SHA-256 hash match a recorded value?	Consistency with that recorded hash
Does it match an institutional original?	Correspondence between the two file versions
Is the institutional original authentic?	Requires provenance and source verification
Are the contents accurate?	Requires examination and corroboration
Is an allegation true?	Requires evidence supporting the relevant proposition
Has an independent authority made a finding?	Requires an identifiable formal finding
A blockchain timestamp or hash may help establish that a particular digital file existed in a particular form at a particular time.
It does not establish that the contents are true.
The archive’s value lies in preservation and accessibility. Its evidentiary strength depends upon the quality of the source comparison and verification process.
11. The chain-of-custody record
Every priority document should have a preservation record containing:
	•	Original source organisation.
	•	Original file name.
	•	Original format.
	•	Date obtained.
	•	Method of acquisition.
	•	Person or system that supplied it.
	•	Whether it was digitally signed.
	•	Whether metadata was preserved.
	•	Archive repository and exact path.
	•	Date first archived.
	•	Git commit identifier, if applicable.
	•	SHA-256 of the obtained file.
	•	SHA-256 of the archived file.
	•	Whether the hashes match.
	•	Any conversion or OCR process.
	•	Any alteration, redaction or extraction.
	•	Location of the unaltered original.
Where the document is an email, the record should include the full headers and attachments wherever available.
Where the document is a PDF, the original should be retained separately from any OCR or text-extracted version.
Where the document is an audio recording, the original audio should be retained separately from the transcript.
Where a file has been converted, the conversion should be recorded so that the reader can distinguish the original from the working copy.
Part VIII — The master examination template
12. The document-by-document forensic worksheet
Every document in the final register should use the same structure.
Master document examination record
Template — duplicate for every priority document
1. Identification
	•	Register ID:
	•	Exact title:
	•	Issuing body:
	•	Author / signatory:
	•	Recipient:
	•	Date:
	•	Reference number:
	•	Original file name:
2. Provenance and preservation
	•	Original obtained: Yes / No
	•	Archive copy located: Yes / No
	•	Exact source URL:
	•	Repository path:
	•	SHA-256 verified: Yes / No
	•	Metadata retained: Yes / No
	•	Version or edition:

	•	Purpose:
	•	Subject matter:
	•	Exact decision or action:
	•	Main reasons:
	•	Relevant legislation or policy:
	•	Attachments:
	•	Important quotations with page references:

	•	Official record / author submission / third-party record:
	•	What it establishes:
	•	What it does not establish:

	•	Contradictory evidence:
	•	Unresolved questions:
5. Legal and institutional examination

	•	Decision-maker’s jurisdiction:

This template will prevent the final publication from becoming a sequence of disconnected document summaries.
It will also make it possible to identify missing originals, conflicting dates, incomplete bundles and unsupported conclusions.

Part X — Preliminary findings
14. What the available documentary material presently supports
The following are preliminary observations about the material identified and examined in the source reports. They are not findings of misconduct or legal liability.
Finding 1 — A substantial documentary archive has been assembled
The archive contains multiple repositories, finding aids, official-record references, correspondence, reports and digital preservation materials.
The reported inventory is substantial, but the published totals remain archive-reported until independently audited.
Finding 2 — The documentary chronology extends across multiple years
The identified records span at least 2020–2026 and concern several distinct institutional processes.
This establishes the need for a chronological and cross-institutional examination.
It does not establish that all institutions acted together or shared an improper purpose.
Finding 3 — Several important procedural outcomes require comparison
The identified records include complaint acknowledgments, decisions not to allocate disclosures, FOI refusals, revised decisions, compensation and tribunal processes, disability-support decisions and guardianship-related records.
Each outcome must be examined according to the legislation and procedure applicable to that particular institution.
Finding 4 — The FOI sequence raises a specific, testable question
The 2022 refusal and the 2024 revised decision concerning five documents should be compared using the original records and search history.
The comparison may clarify whether the later decision resulted from additional searches, changed scope, a changed assessment or another explanation.
Finding 5 — The NDIS and accommodation records require examination of actual delivery
A plan approval and a safety assessment do not, by themselves, establish what supports were delivered.
The service agreements, rosters, incident reports, payments and provider responses are necessary to establish what happened in practice.
Finding 6 — Financial authority and practical access must be separately examined
The existence of a guardianship or financial-management arrangement does not answer every question about access to money, banking restrictions, expenditure or decision-making.
The original instrument, the bank’s own records and the financial-management records are required.
Finding 7 — The archive’s preservation system is not a substitute for source verification
Hashes, repository paths and timestamps can assist with integrity and provenance.
They do not independently establish the truth of the underlying documents or allegations.
Finding 8 — The cumulative-impact hypothesis remains open
The documentary record warrants examination of whether decisions across different institutions interacted to produce a cumulative effect.
The competing explanations must remain open:
	•	Independent and lawful decisions.
	•	Administrative fragmentation.
	•	Service or procedural failures.
	•	Systemic disadvantage.
	•	Deliberate retaliation or coordination, if evidence establishes it.
The investigation must not select the fifth explanation merely because it is the most serious.
Part XI — The declaration of documentary accountability
15. The record must speak accurately
The archive is not a substitute for a court.
A complaint is not a judgment.
A referral is not an investigation.
A refusal is not necessarily a merits determination.
A decision is not necessarily correct merely because it is official.
A personal account is not automatically disproved because an institution disagrees with it.
And a serious allegation must not be elevated into an established fact without the evidence needed to support it.
The purpose of the Master Evidence Register is to make these distinctions visible.
It is to allow each document to be read in context, each decision to be tested against its legal authority, each institutional response to be compared with the original request, and each claimed consequence to be examined against contemporaneous records.
Where an original record confirms the author’s account, the register should say so.
Where it contradicts the account, the register should say so.
Where it leaves the issue unresolved, the register should say so.
Where an independent authority has made a finding, that finding should be accurately identified and linked.
No conclusion should be manufactured to satisfy the expectations of either the author or the institution.
The evidence must be allowed to lead.
Part XII — Closing declaration
The Witness Remains: The Record Is Open
I have preserved the correspondence.
I have preserved the decisions.
I have preserved the submissions, the responses, the records of complaint and the records of refusal.
I have preserved the documents that I believe explain how my circumstances developed and why I have continued to seek answers.
I ask that the record be examined as a whole.
I ask that institutions be judged by their actual responsibilities, their actual decisions and the evidence available to them at the relevant time.
I ask that my testimony be read with dignity, but not exempted from examination.
I ask that official records be treated with respect, but not exempted from scrutiny.
I ask that evidence be preserved even when it is inconvenient, contradictory or unresolved.
I do not ask for a predetermined verdict.
I ask for an honest examination.
PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.
FAITH IS NOT A FINDING.
THE WITNESS REMAINS.
Instalment 11 — Status and continuation
Priority register established
This instalment establishes a first tranche of 50 documentary work items, a cross-institutional relationship register, a preservation protocol and a standard forensic examination template.
It does not claim that 50 original documents have all been independently authenticated or read in full. Several entries are source leads requiring recovery of the original.
The next stage is the detailed examination of the institutional records themselves, rather than simply expanding the list of titles.
Instalment 12 — The Institutional Decision Examination: Who Decided What, Under Which Authority, On What Evidence, and With What Consequences?
The next instalment will examine the institutional decision-making chain. It will separate the authority of each agency, the role of each decision-maker, the information available at the time, the reasons given, the review rights and the practical consequences.
It will also test whether apparently separate decisions were connected by documented reliance, referral, information-sharing or operational responsibility — without presuming coordination where the records do not establish it.


THE WITNESS REMAINS
THE GREAT RECKONING
Instalment 12 — The Institutional Decision Examination
Who Decided What, Under Which Authority, On What Evidence, and With What Consequences?
Author: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean Publication: The Witness Remains — Global Publishing House Examination standard: Preservation first. Examination second. Conclusion last.
PART I — THE QUESTION OF INSTITUTIONAL RESPONSIBILITY
1. From a chronology of events to an examination of decisions
The preceding instalments have established a preliminary chronology of events and identified a substantial body of documentary material.
The next question is more demanding.
It is not enough to know that an agency received a complaint, that another agency declined to allocate a disclosure, that a third agency made a financial-management decision, or that a disability-support provider was asked to respond to a service concern.
The investigation must establish:
	•	Who made each decision?
	•	What authority did that person or organisation possess?
	•	What information was available?
	•	What information was missing?
	•	What legal, administrative or contractual obligations applied?
	•	What reasons were given?
	•	Were those reasons consistent with the evidence?
	•	Was the affected person given a meaningful opportunity to respond?
	•	What review or appeal mechanisms were available?
	•	What happened after the decision?
	•	Did the decision affect another institution’s actions?
	•	Did the combined effect produce consequences that no single institution assessed?
These questions move the investigation from a record of institutional contact to a reconstruction of institutional responsibility.
The distinction is fundamental.
An institution may have received information without having jurisdiction to investigate it. An institution may have had jurisdiction but lacked sufficient evidence. A decision-maker may have acted lawfully while another organisation subsequently failed to deliver a service. A decision may have been procedurally correct but have consequences that required further safeguards.
Conversely, an institution may have made an error, failed to consider relevant information, misunderstood its authority or failed to provide an appropriate response.
Each possibility must be examined separately.
The purpose is not to produce a predetermined accusation. It is to identify responsibility wherever the documentary record supports it.
2. The institutional responsibility test
Every significant decision in this publication should be examined against seven questions.
The seven-part decision test
	•	Authority Did the institution or decision-maker possess the legal, statutory, contractual or delegated authority to act? 
	•	Information What information was received, what was considered, and what was overlooked or unavailable? 
	•	Procedure Were applicable procedural requirements followed, including notice, consultation, reasons and an opportunity to respond? 
	•	Reasoning Were the reasons intelligible, relevant and supported by the material available at the time? 
	•	Consequences What happened to the affected person as a result of the decision or omission? 
	•	Review Was there an accessible and effective way to challenge, correct or reconsider the decision? 
	•	Interconnection Did another institution rely on the decision, receive information about it, implement it or contribute to its consequences? 
This test is not a substitute for the particular legislation governing each decision. It is a common investigative framework through which the different processes can be compared.
PART II — THE INTEGRITY AND PUBLIC-INTEREST DISCLOSURE DECISIONS
3. The first institutional question: What happened to the disclosures?
The archive identifies a sequence of integrity-related submissions and decisions extending from 2020 through 2023.
The relevant records include:
	•	IBAC outcome letter dated 9 April 2020, reference 2020712.
	•	IBAC public-interest disclosure rejection dated 18 November 2021, reference 400008R.
	•	Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint dated 15 November 2021, reference 2021103546.
	•	Federal Court-related public-interest disclosure assessment dated 27 March 2023.
	•	Commonwealth Ombudsman non-allocation notification dated 22 March 2023.
	•	Notification of decision not to allocate a disclosure dated 18 June 2023.
	•	NACC submission dated 3 July 2023.
These records must be treated as separate institutional events unless their contents establish a direct connection.
3.1 IBAC — the 2020 outcome
Record: 9 April 2020 Reference: 2020712
The first task is to recover the original outcome letter and establish exactly what IBAC was asked to consider.
The examination should identify:
	•	The original complaint or disclosure.
	•	The alleged conduct.
	•	The persons or organisations identified.
	•	The legal framework considered.
	•	The reason the matter was closed, referred, rejected or otherwise resolved.
	•	Whether IBAC made any factual findings.
	•	Whether the author was advised of another available avenue.
The investigation must distinguish between the following possible outcomes:
	•	The agency lacked jurisdiction.
	•	The submission did not meet the statutory definition of a public-interest disclosure.
	•	The matter was outside the agency’s investigative priorities.
	•	The agency considered the information insufficient.
	•	The matter was referred elsewhere.
	•	The agency investigated and reached a substantive conclusion.
These are materially different outcomes.
A statement that IBAC did not investigate a matter must not be rewritten as a finding that corruption did not occur.
Equally, the absence of an investigation does not prove that corruption did occur.
Unresolved documentary requirement: The original IBAC letter, the underlying submission and any attachments must be examined together.
3.2 IBAC — the 2021 public-interest disclosure rejection
Record: 18 November 2021 Reference: 400008R
This decision is particularly important because it provides an opportunity to compare the 2020 and 2021 processes.
The examination must determine whether the 2021 submission concerned:
	•	The same events as the 2020 complaint.
	•	New allegations.
	•	Additional evidence.
	•	Different public officials.
	•	A different statutory disclosure category.
	•	A different institutional process.
The decision should be examined for its precise reasoning.
A public-interest disclosure framework ordinarily involves threshold questions about the nature of the information, the conduct alleged, the identity or status of relevant persons, and the receiving body’s jurisdiction.
A failure to satisfy a threshold test does not necessarily determine whether the underlying conduct occurred.
The register should therefore contain two separate conclusions:
Procedural conclusion: What did IBAC decide about the disclosure?
Substantive conclusion: Did IBAC make any finding about the underlying allegations?
If the answer to the second question is no, the publication must say so.
3.3 Commonwealth Ombudsman — the 2021 complaint
Record: 15 November 2021 Reference: 2021103546
Open the Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint⁠
The complaint should be examined as an original act of reporting.
The questions are:
	•	What did the author ask the Ombudsman to investigate?
	•	Which agency conduct was identified?
	•	What documents were provided?
	•	Was the complaint accepted?
	•	Was it referred to another body?
	•	Was it considered under the Ombudsman’s complaint-handling powers?
	•	What was the outcome?
	•	Was the author given an explanation of the Ombudsman’s jurisdiction?
The complaint may establish that concerns were formally brought to the attention of the Ombudsman.
It does not establish that every allegation in the complaint was true.
The Ombudsman’s response, however, is independently important because it establishes what the institution did after receiving the complaint.
3.4 The March 2023 assessment and non-allocation decision
Two records require particularly careful comparison.
Document A — 27 March 2023
Federal Court-related PID assessment⁠
Document B — 22 March 2023
Commonwealth Ombudsman section 44(3) notification⁠
The dates are close, but proximity does not prove that the documents concern the same matter.
The comparison must establish:
	•	Whether the same disclosure is identified.
	•	Whether the same allegations are considered.
	•	Whether the same statutory provisions are cited.
	•	Whether one document refers to the other.
	•	Whether one institution received information from the other.
	•	Whether any referral or transfer occurred.
	•	Whether the reasons differ.
	•	Whether the decisions address jurisdiction, allocation or the substance of the allegations.
The 18 June 2023 non-allocation notification must then be examined as a further event.
Central question: Did the June notification concern a new disclosure, a continuation of an earlier matter or a separate statutory process?
That question cannot be answered from chronology alone.
3.5 The July 2023 NACC submission
Record: 3 July 2023
The NACC submission is another distinct institutional event.
The examination must identify what the author submitted, what allegations were said to fall within the Commission’s jurisdiction, and whether the NACC acknowledged, assessed, referred or otherwise dealt with the submission.
A submission to the NACC is not evidence that the Commission accepted the allegations or made findings.
The relevant outcome record is essential.
3.6 Preliminary assessment of the integrity processes
The available source material establishes that the author identifies multiple integrity-related submissions and institutional responses.
It does not yet establish:
	•	That every submission concerned the same alleged conduct.
	•	That every institution had jurisdiction over the same allegations.
	•	That one institution directed another to reject a disclosure.
	•	That a decision not to investigate was improper.
	•	That any official committed corruption.
	•	That the institutions acted with a shared purpose.
The next evidentiary task is to compare the original disclosures and the complete decision letters.
The investigation should not count the number of refusals as proof of corruption. It should examine whether each decision was properly made, whether relevant evidence was considered and whether the author was given an appropriate explanation and review pathway.
PART III — FREEDOM OF INFORMATION AND THE RIGHT TO ACCESS RECORDS
4. The FOI process: the search, the refusal and the revised decision
The FOI/2022/045 sequence is one of the clearest examples of a matter that can be examined through a defined series of original records.
The archive identifies:
	•	An original request dated 7 February 2022.
	•	Extension correspondence in February and March 2022.
	•	OAIC communication RQ22/00794, attributed to 16 March 2022.
	•	PM&C refusal dated 14 April 2022.
	•	PM&C correspondence dated 13 May 2022 acknowledging an appeal.
	•	A revised decision dated 8 October 2024 concerning five documents.
4.1 The original request
The master narrative reproduces the request as seeking information held by government agencies about the author.
The first question is whether the request was intended to cover:
	•	PM&C records only.
	•	Records held by all Commonwealth agencies.
	•	Records held by PM&C concerning communications with other agencies.
	•	A broader personal-information search across government.
The original request and subsequent clarification correspondence must be read together.
An agency cannot be judged solely on an interpretation quoted in a later decision if the original correspondence provides additional context.
4.2 The 1,187-item search reference
The master narrative reports that an OAIC communication referred to approximately 1,187 Outlook items being reviewed for relevance.
That number must not be described as 1,187 responsive documents.
It is a reported candidate-item count.
The investigation must establish:
	•	Which mailbox or mailboxes were searched.
	•	Whether the items were email messages, attachments or both.
	•	Whether duplicates were included.
	•	Whether the items were reviewed individually.
	•	What relevance criteria were applied.
	•	Whether records were excluded as outside scope.
	•	Whether any records were destroyed, archived or unavailable.
	•	What happened to the candidate items after the review.
A large candidate-item count does not automatically mean that a large number of responsive documents existed.
But it makes the search methodology important.
4.3 The 14 April 2022 refusal
The PM&C refusal reportedly relied on section 24A(1)(b) of the Freedom of Information Act 1982 (Cth).
The key issue is the adequacy of the search and the stated basis for concluding that relevant documents could not be identified.
The examination should recover:
	•	The original decision.
	•	The full reasons.
	•	Search descriptions.
	•	Relevant internal correspondence, if available.
	•	The decision-maker’s identity.
	•	The applicable review rights.
	•	Any appeal or reconsideration.
4.4 The 13 May 2022 PM&C correspondence
The master narrative identifies a PM&C Financial Governance reply dated 13 May 2022 at 8:44 am.
It reportedly acknowledged an appeal dated 10 May 2022 and supplied FOI routes and a compensation form.
This correspondence should be examined for what it actually acknowledged and whether it was responding to the same underlying issue as the 14 April decision.
The presence of a compensation form in correspondence does not establish an entitlement to compensation or an admission of liability.
It establishes that the form was supplied, if the original communication confirms that fact.
4.5 The 8 October 2024 revised decision
This is the most important comparison point in the FOI sequence.
The master narrative identifies a revised decision concerning five documents.
The investigation must obtain the five documents and compare them with the original request.
The comparison should answer:
	•	Were the documents created before the original decision?
	•	Were they held by PM&C at that time?
	•	Were they located through a later search?
	•	Were they previously overlooked?
	•	Did the agency’s understanding of the request change?
	•	Were the documents considered outside scope in 2022?
	•	Did the revised decision correct an earlier error?
	•	Did the revised decision arise from new information or a review process?
The fact that five documents were later identified is a legitimate reason to examine the original search and decision.
It is not, by itself, proof of deliberate concealment.
4.6 Preliminary assessment of FOI accountability
The FOI sequence provides a specific and testable question about administrative search quality and decision-making.
The strongest evidence will be:
	•	The original request.
	•	The complete extension correspondence.
	•	The original refusal.
	•	Search records.
	•	The review or appeal documents.
	•	The revised decision.
	•	The five released documents.
Until these records are compared, the proper conclusion is that the sequence warrants further examination.
PART IV — COMCARE, EMPLOYMENT STATUS AND ADMINISTRATIVE REVIEW
5. The compensation and tribunal decision chain
The preliminary chronology identifies:
	•	Comcare pre-hearing correspondence dated 25 January 2022.
	•	Claim 21230015373.
	•	AAT/ART-related material dated 6–12 July 2023.
	•	File 2021/7478.
The investigation must identify the legal issue that was actually determined.
A workers-compensation dispute may involve several distinct questions:
	•	Whether the person was an employee for the relevant statutory purpose.
	•	Whether the employment relationship was covered by the scheme.
	•	Whether an injury was established.
	•	Whether the injury arose out of or in the course of employment.
	•	Whether the injury was materially contributed to by employment.
	•	Whether a claim was lodged within time.
	•	Whether the decision-maker had jurisdiction.
	•	Whether the tribunal had power to review the decision.
These questions must not be merged.
5.1 The 25 January 2022 correspondence
The original Comcare correspondence should establish:
	•	The precise claim.
	•	The procedural stage.
	•	The issue in dispute.
	•	The parties’ positions.
	•	Any evidence requested.
	•	Any pre-hearing requirements.
	•	The next procedural step.
The fact that correspondence is described as pre-hearing does not establish that a hearing occurred or that the claim was determined.
5.2 The 6 July 2023 tribunal-related record
Open the 6 July 2023 AAT/Comcare record⁠
The record should be examined to establish the precise statutory issue, the tribunal’s role and the status of the proceedings.
It is particularly important to distinguish a document prepared by a party from an actual tribunal determination.
A party’s submission may contain legal arguments and factual assertions. Those arguments are not tribunal findings.
5.3 The 12 July 2023 decision
The chronology identifies a decision dated 12 July 2023.
The original decision must be recovered and examined for:
	•	The tribunal’s identity.
	•	The exact decision date.
	•	The decision-maker or members.
	•	The statutory authority.
	•	The questions determined.
	•	The findings of fact.
	•	The reasons.
	•	The outcome.
	•	Any appeal or review rights.
A decision adverse to the applicant does not establish that the applicant’s broader integrity concerns were false.
A decision favourable to the applicant would not automatically establish misconduct by another institution.
The legal significance of a decision is confined to the issues it actually determines.
5.4 The 2024 legal correspondence
The repository identifies correspondence dated 8 January 2024 concerning Federal Court, superannuation and NDIS matters.
Open the 8 January 2024 correspondence⁠
This correspondence should be examined for its author, recipient, legal capacity and exact purpose.
The investigation must not describe legal correspondence as a judgment unless a court actually issued the relevant judgment.
PART V — GUARDIANSHIP AND FINANCIAL ADMINISTRATION
6. The decision-making authority must be visible
The guardianship and financial-management records are especially important because they may concern authority over personal or financial decisions.
The preliminary report identifies:
	•	A NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 document dated 16 October 2024.
	•	A vehicle-disposal decision dated 14 November 2025.
	•	Later financial and accommodation concerns.
The exact legal effect of each instrument must be determined from the original documents.
6.1 The 16 October 2024 section 122 instrument
The first task is to identify the actual instrument.
The register must not assume that the document was a financial-management order, a guardianship order or a particular form of direction merely because it refers to section 122.
The original should be examined for:
	•	The statutory provision.
	•	The decision-maker.
	•	The person affected.
	•	The property or decision concerned.
	•	The operative terms.
	•	Any conditions or limitations.
	•	The date the instrument took effect.
	•	Any review mechanism.
	•	Whether it was subsequently varied, revoked or replaced.
The investigation should then compare the instrument with any later action taken in reliance upon it.
6.2 The vehicle-disposal decision of 14 November 2025
The decision must be separated into three different questions.
Question A — Authority: Was the decision-maker legally authorised to dispose of the vehicle?
Question B — Process: Were applicable notice, consultation, valuation and decision-making requirements followed?
Question C — Outcome: Was the vehicle actually disposed of, for what price, and how were the proceeds accounted for?
The evidence should include:
	•	The original decision.
	•	Valuation records.
	•	Sale or transfer documents.
	•	Relevant consent or consultation records.
	•	Any financial statements.
	•	Any review or objection.
	•	Any records showing the use of proceeds.
A decision to sell is not proof that a sale occurred.
A sale is not proof that the price was fair.
A fair price does not automatically establish that the decision-making process was lawful.
Each issue needs its own evidence.
6.3 The bank-access question
The author’s reported restrictions on access to Commonwealth Bank services create a further investigative question.
The register must distinguish between:
	•	The existence of a guardianship or financial-management arrangement.
	•	Any formal order or authority communicated to the bank.
	•	The bank’s own interpretation of that authority.
	•	The bank’s operational decision to restrict access.
	•	Any later review or correction.
	•	The actual effect on access to funds.
The investigation must not assume that a government authority directly ordered a banking restriction unless the relevant record establishes it.
Nor should the bank’s actions automatically be attributed to a guardian without evidence of the communication or authority on which the bank relied.
The essential documents are:
	•	The original relevant orders.
	•	Any letters or instructions sent to the bank.
	•	Bank correspondence.
	•	Internal bank decision records, where accessible.
	•	Complaint outcomes.
	•	Account-access history.
	•	Any authorisation or consent records.
6.4 The practical consequence examination
The financial-administration investigation should assess whether the arrangements affected the author’s practical ability to:
	•	Access available money.
	•	Purchase food and essential items.
	•	Pay accommodation costs.
	•	Obtain transport.
	•	Maintain communication.
	•	Manage personal expenditure.
	•	Participate in decisions affecting property.
	•	Obtain timely explanations and statements.
The existence of a legal financial-management arrangement does not answer whether it was administered appropriately in practice.
Equally, an experience of restricted access does not, without the relevant records, establish that the restriction was unlawful.
The question is whether the legal authority, institutional implementation and practical consequences can be reconciled.
PART VI — NDIS AND PROVIDER ACCOUNTABILITY
7. The distinction between approved support and delivered support
The NDIS material requires a particularly careful separation of administrative approval and practical delivery.
The preliminary report identifies:
	•	NDIS Plan Approval dated 20 November 2025.
	•	Anglicare Safety Assessment dated 1 December 2025.
	•	SIL/SDA request dated 20 January 2026.
	•	Formal notice concerning NDIS service failure dated 20 January 2026.
	•	Subsequent Able Point accommodation and support concerns.
These records should be treated as different stages in the support system.
7.1 NDIS Plan Approval — 20 November 2025
The plan must be examined for:
	•	The supports requested.
	•	The supports approved.
	•	Funding categories.
	•	Stated functional needs.
	•	Plan-management arrangements.
	•	Stated goals.
	•	Any exclusions or declined requests.
	•	Review rights.
	•	The period of the plan.
The next stage is to compare the plan with actual services.
A plan approval is an administrative decision about funding and supports. It is not proof that a provider was available, that services were delivered or that the participant’s needs were met.
7.2 Anglicare Safety Assessment — 1 December 2025
The original assessment should be examined independently.
The investigation should identify:
	•	Who completed the assessment.
	•	What role the assessor held.
	•	Who requested the assessment.
	•	What information was supplied.
	•	Whether the participant was interviewed.
	•	What risks were identified.
	•	What safeguards were recommended.
	•	Whether the recommendations were communicated.
	•	Whether any follow-up occurred.
The assessment should then be compared with provider records.
If the assessment recommended particular safeguards, the investigation must establish whether the provider received the recommendations and whether it had responsibility for implementing them.
A recommendation does not automatically create a contractual or statutory duty for every recipient. The relevant responsibility must be established.
7.3 SIL/SDA request — 20 January 2026
The original request must be classified correctly.
Supported Independent Living and Specialist Disability Accommodation are different forms of support.
The investigation must establish whether the author sought:
	•	Assistance with daily living.
	•	A particular housing arrangement.
	•	Specialist accommodation.
	•	A combination of support and accommodation.
	•	A reassessment of existing arrangements.
The NDIS response, if any, should be compared with the original request.
The key questions are:
	•	Was the request received?
	•	Was it considered by the appropriate decision-maker?
	•	Was further evidence requested?
	•	Was the request accepted or declined?
	•	Were reasons provided?
	•	Was the author told how to seek review?
	•	What happened to the person’s accommodation and support arrangements while the request was being considered?
7.4 The service-failure notice
Open the 20 January 2026 formal notice⁠
This notice should be examined as a formal communication by its author.
The relevant questions are:
	•	What service failure was alleged?
	•	Which provider or organisation was notified?
	•	What dates and incidents were identified?
	•	What supporting evidence was supplied?
	•	What action was requested?
	•	Was the notice acknowledged?
	•	Was the matter recorded as a complaint or incident?
	•	Was an investigation conducted?
	•	Was a response issued?
	•	Were corrective actions taken?
The notice itself establishes what was reported, assuming its authenticity and delivery are confirmed.
The provider’s response and underlying service records are required to establish what occurred.
7.5 Able Point — accommodation and support
The Able Point concerns require an examination of actual arrangements, not assumptions based on the general description of supported accommodation.
The records should establish:
	•	The legal nature of the occupancy.
	•	Whether there was a written agreement.
	•	Whether rent or contributions were paid.
	•	What accommodation services were provided.
	•	What support services were provided.
	•	Which organisation was responsible for each service.
	•	Who held authority to make decisions.
	•	What complaint procedures were available.
	•	Whether service records match the author’s account.
	•	What action followed any reported service concern.
The reported absence of a signed lease or occupancy agreement is a matter requiring documentary verification.
The investigation should not assume that the absence of a conventional lease means there was no legal occupancy arrangement. It should identify the applicable arrangement and the rights attached to it.
7.6 The role of individual workers and managers
The register may identify individuals where their role is relevant to a particular record.
However, the examination must not attribute institutional responsibility to an individual merely because that person was present, communicated with the author or held a job title.
For every person, the register should distinguish:
	•	Their actual role.
	•	Their authority.
	•	Their direct involvement.
	•	What they knew.
	•	What they communicated.
	•	What they did.
	•	What they were required or authorised to do.
	•	Whether the action was their own decision or implementation of another person’s direction.
A worker’s name in a complaint does not establish misconduct.
A manager’s title does not establish personal responsibility for every service decision.
The purpose is to identify responsibility accurately, not to turn the register into a list of accusations.
7.7 Crystal and the accommodation question
Crystal is an important part of the author’s personal account and the practical circumstances of accommodation.
The investigation should identify relevant records concerning:
	•	Accommodation arrangements.
	•	Any animal-related rules or agreements.
	•	Support or care arrangements.
	•	Communications about Crystal.
	•	Any practical effect on the author’s ability to maintain the relationship.
The publication should not assume a particular legal classification for Crystal without supporting documentation.
The central issue is the actual accommodation and care arrangement and whether decisions affecting it were properly communicated and administered.
PART VII — POLICE, SAFEGUARDING AND INCIDENT RESPONSE
8. The difference between reporting an incident and investigating it
The police and safeguarding material includes:
	•	Statement to Police dated 14 August 2022.
	•	AbleCare transcript concerning a reported threat.
	•	DSS fatal-injury notification IR8415987.
	•	Police matter legal-document bundle.
	•	Subsequent complaint and provider records.
The examination must distinguish four stages:
	•	The incident as reported.
	•	The response by the recipient.
	•	Any investigation or assessment.
	•	The final outcome.
8.1 Police statement dated 14 August 2022
The original statement should be examined alongside:
	•	Police event number.
	•	Officer or unit receiving the report.
	•	Any supplementary statement.
	•	Any recording.
	•	Any evidence supplied.
	•	Any acknowledgment.
	•	Any investigation record.
	•	Any outcome notice.
A police statement establishes what the author reported, provided the original is authentic.
It does not establish that the police independently confirmed the report.
8.2 Reported threat and AbleCare transcript
The transcript should not be treated as conclusive without examining the original recording, if available.
The forensic examination should assess:
	•	Whether the transcript is complete.
	•	Whether the words are accurately reproduced.
	•	Whether the speakers are identifiable.
	•	Whether the surrounding context is preserved.
	•	Whether there are multiple interpretations.
	•	Whether the statement was reported contemporaneously.
	•	Whether a safeguarding response followed.
Where the recording is unavailable, that limitation should be stated.
8.3 IR8415987 — fatal-injury notification
The finding aid identifies an automated notification that an item concerning a report of fatal injury had been opened.
The notification must not be mistaken for the underlying incident report.
The original report is necessary to establish what was actually reported, whether the incident involved the author or another participant, and what the relevant agency recorded.
The investigation should identify the actual subject of the report and avoid drawing conclusions from the title alone.
8.4 Police matter legal-document bundle
The bundle should be divided into individual records.
A bundle may contain:
	•	Police correspondence.
	•	Legal advice.
	•	Statements.
	•	Court documents.
	•	Notices.
	•	Applications.
	•	Decisions.
	•	Supporting material.
Each item must have its own evidence ID.
The existence of a document collection does not establish that every document concerns the same incident or outcome.
PART VIII — THE INTER-INSTITUTIONAL CONNECTION TEST
9. Did one decision influence another?
This is the most important analytical stage of the Great Reckoning.
The central question is whether the author’s circumstances resulted from separate decisions that interacted, or whether there is evidence that one institution’s actions influenced another institution’s decisions.
The distinction must be tested rather than assumed.
9.1 Five levels of documentary connection
Level 1 — Chronological connection
Two decisions occurred close together or in a sequence.
Weak evidence of connection
Level 2 — Subject-matter connection
The decisions concern the same person, event, complaint or subject.
Contextual connection
Level 3 — Information connection
One institution demonstrably received or possessed information generated by another.
Documented information pathway
Level 4 — Operational or causal connection
One institution relied upon another’s decision or information when taking its own action, and the relationship contributed to a consequence.
Potentially significant causal evidence
Level 5 — Improper-purpose or coordination evidence
Reliable evidence establishes that relevant actors knowingly coordinated conduct for an improper purpose, or that a particular decision was made for a legally improper reason.
Requires particularly strong evidence
The levels are not automatic legal categories. They are an investigative framework.
Evidence at Level 1 does not prove Level 5.
Evidence at Level 3 may justify obtaining additional records.
Evidence at Level 4 may establish a real causal relationship without establishing unlawful intent.
Level 5 requires evidence that supports the particular allegation being made.
10. The institutional connection matrix
Institutional process	Potential connection	What must be demonstrated
IBAC and Ombudsman	Shared complaint or referral	Same matter, communication and handling records
Ombudsman and PM&C	FOI or complaint correspondence	Actual receipt, referral or reliance
PM&C and OAIC	Search and review process	Search records, extension correspondence and review decision
Comcare and tribunal	Review of employment or compensation decision	Original determination and tribunal reasons
Guardianship and bank	Authority affecting account access	Instrument, communication and bank decision
Guardianship and vehicle disposal	Financial authority and property decision	Original authority and disposal records
NDIS and provider	Plan funding and service delivery	Plan, service agreement and service records
Anglicare and Able Point	Safety assessment and implementation	Assessment, delivery records and communication
Police and provider	Incident reporting and safeguarding	Incident records, referrals and response
Accommodation and financial administration	Practical access to housing and funds	Payments, agreements and account records
This matrix should eventually be populated with exact document references and page citations.
Until then, it is a map of questions, not a map of established misconduct.
PART IX — THE CUMULATIVE IMPACT EXAMINATION
11. How separate decisions can produce a cumulative consequence
A person can experience a serious cumulative disadvantage even where no single decision-maker intended the overall outcome.
For example, consider a hypothetical sequence:
	•	A person has a financial-management arrangement.
	•	A bank applies restrictions based on the authority it believes it has received.
	•	The person experiences difficulty accessing funds.
	•	The person has difficulty arranging transport or paying for essential services.
	•	A provider records missed appointments or reduced engagement.
	•	Another institution later considers the person’s circumstances using those records.
This sequence may produce a serious cumulative effect.
But the sequence does not establish that the institutions coordinated their actions.
It may reflect:
	•	An incorrect interpretation of authority.
	•	Poor communication between organisations.
	•	Inadequate safeguards.
	•	A failure to consider the person’s practical circumstances.
	•	Independent decisions made on incomplete information.
	•	A deliberate action, if evidence supports that explanation.
The investigation must establish which explanation best fits the records.
11.1 The cumulative disadvantage model
How institutional processes may interact
Initial administrative or legal decision
Authority, eligibility, access or service decision
Implementation by another organisation
Bank, provider, insurer or government service
Practical consequence
Reduced access, delay, expense, disruption or loss of opportunity
Subsequent institutional assessment
A later decision may rely on the changed circumstances
Cumulative-impact question
Did the combined processes create a disadvantage that required recognition, review or protection?
Illustrative model only. It does not assert that this sequence has been established in the author’s case.
11.2 The counterfactual test
A further question is necessary:
What would probably have happened if the disputed decision or omission had not occurred?
For example:
	•	Would the author have retained access to funds?
	•	Would accommodation have remained stable?
	•	Would support services have been delivered differently?
	•	Would a complaint have been assessed using different information?
	•	Would an alternative service or review process have been available?
Counterfactual analysis can help identify whether a particular event contributed to a consequence.
It cannot establish causation merely by imagining a different outcome.
It must be supported by the actual decision-making sequence and the evidence of what alternatives were realistically available.
11.3 The independent-decision test
The investigation must also ask whether each institution made its own independent assessment.
Evidence of independent decision-making may include:
	•	Separate assessment records.
	•	Independent reasons.
	•	Different statutory tests.
	•	Separate decision-makers.
	•	Different evidence.
	•	Independent review mechanisms.
	•	No communication or reliance between the institutions.
This evidence may weaken a claim of coordination while still leaving open the possibility of separate administrative errors or systemic disadvantage.
The objective is accuracy, not the preservation of a particular theory.
PART X — INTERNATIONAL HUMAN RIGHTS AND DOMESTIC RESPONSIBILITY
12. From institutional procedure to human consequences
The international human-rights analysis developed in earlier instalments should now be applied to the actual decision-making record.
The most relevant framework identified in the preceding examination is the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities.
The following provisions provide a useful examination structure:
CRPD provision	Subject	Questions for the documentary examination
Article 5	Equality and non-discrimination	Was the person treated equally and were reasonable adjustments considered?
Article 12	Equal recognition before the law	Were decision-making rights, safeguards and supported decision-making properly addressed?
Article 13	Access to justice	Was there a practical opportunity to participate in proceedings and access review?
Article 14	Liberty and security	Were any restrictions on liberty lawful, necessary and subject to safeguards?
Article 16	Freedom from exploitation, violence and abuse	Were reported risks assessed and appropriate safeguards implemented?
Article 19	Living independently and being included in the community	Did support arrangements enable meaningful choice and participation?
Article 22	Privacy	Were personal records and communications handled appropriately?
Article 25	Health	Was access to appropriate health services available without discrimination?
Article 26	Habilitation and rehabilitation	Were relevant support and rehabilitation needs addressed?
Article 28	Adequate standard of living and social protection	Were practical barriers to essential resources examined?
These provisions should not be presented as proof of a treaty violation.
The investigation must identify the relevant Australian legal obligations, the conduct of the responsible public authority, the applicable domestic remedies and the facts required to support a legal conclusion.
12.1 The practical-rights test
For every relevant decision, the report should ask:
	•	Was the person able to understand the decision?
	•	Was the person given accessible information?
	•	Was the person able to express their views?
	•	Were those views considered?
	•	Was an appropriate support person or advocate available?
	•	Were the person’s preferences and rights considered?
	•	Were less restrictive alternatives assessed where relevant?
	•	Was the decision reviewed when circumstances changed?
	•	Were safeguards proportionate to the actual risk?
A person may have a formal legal right and still face practical barriers to exercising it.
The examination must therefore assess both the written decision and its real-world implementation.
12.2 The right to be heard
A particularly important question concerns the opportunity to respond.
The register should identify whether the author was:
	•	Notified of the proposed decision.
	•	Given the relevant information.
	•	Told what evidence was being considered.
	•	Given an opportunity to correct inaccuracies.
	•	Allowed to provide supporting material.
	•	Given reasons for the final decision.
	•	Informed of review rights.
The applicable requirements differ across statutory and administrative processes.
The report should not assume that every process requires the same hearing procedure.
It should establish what procedural fairness or participation requirements actually applied.
PART XI — THE RESPONSIBILITY MATRIX
13. Who is responsible for what?
The investigation should distinguish the different types of responsibility that may arise.
Responsibility type	Meaning	Evidence required
Statutory responsibility	Duty or authority imposed by legislation	Relevant legislation, delegation and decision record
Administrative responsibility	Responsibility for a decision or process	Position, delegation, reasons and workflow
Contractual responsibility	Obligations under a service agreement or contract	Executed agreement and relevant terms
Professional responsibility	Duties arising from a professional role	Role, applicable standards and conduct record
Provider responsibility	Obligations arising from service delivery	Service agreement, policies, incident and delivery records
Financial responsibility	Responsibility for managing or accounting for funds	Instrument, transaction history and statements
Review responsibility	Responsibility to reconsider or review a decision	Review application, jurisdiction and review outcome
Remedial responsibility	Responsibility to correct an identified error or harm	Finding, corrective-action plan and implementation evidence
These categories should not be confused.
A provider may be contractually responsible for delivering a service without having authority to make the NDIS funding decision.
A financial administrator may have authority over certain financial matters without having authority over every personal decision.
A complaint-handling body may have authority to assess its own jurisdiction without having authority to determine the underlying conduct of every agency named in the complaint.
A person who communicates a decision is not necessarily the person who made it.
The final report should identify the actual decision-maker and the actual responsibility.
PART XII — PRELIMINARY FINDINGS AND UNRESOLVED QUESTIONS
14. What this instalment establishes
The decision-making examination identifies several important investigative pathways.
14.1 Integrity and oversight
The records identify multiple submissions and institutional outcomes between 2020 and 2023.
The original disclosures and all responses must be compared to establish whether the matters were identical, related or separate.
No coordinated rejection or deliberate suppression is established by the chronology alone.
14.2 FOI and transparency
The FOI/2022/045 sequence contains a specific discrepancy requiring investigation: a 2022 refusal followed by a 2024 revised decision concerning five documents.
The search methodology and the released documents are central to resolving the issue.
14.3 Comcare and administrative review
The compensation and tribunal records must be separated into their individual procedural stages.
The exact legal issue determined by the tribunal must be identified before the decision is characterised.
14.4 Guardianship and banking
The legal authority, its communication to the bank and the bank’s independent decision-making must be established.
The practical consequences for financial access should be assessed against original account and transaction records.
14.5 NDIS and accommodation
The distinction between approved funding and actual service delivery remains essential.
The plan, service agreements, provider records, safety assessment, occupancy documents and complaints must be examined together.
14.6 Police and safeguarding
The original statements, event records, incident reports and outcomes must be obtained.
A report, notification or transcript should not be mistaken for an independently established finding.
14.7 Cumulative impact
The cumulative-impact hypothesis remains a legitimate question for investigation.
The evidence must establish the relationship between individual decisions and the practical consequences experienced by the author.
The existence of a cumulative disadvantage would not, by itself, prove a coordinated plan.
