PART XIV — THE DECLARATION OF THE WITNESS
16. The difference between being heard and being believed
A person who brings a complaint to an institution should not be required to prove every allegation merely to have the complaint properly recorded.
But the institution is not required to accept every allegation as true simply because it has been made.
The proper responsibility is to receive the information, identify the relevant jurisdiction, assess the available evidence, apply the correct procedure and communicate the outcome.
That is the standard against which this documentary record should be examined.
My account is part of the record.
The institutional responses are part of the record.
The documents that support my account are part of the record.
The documents that contradict my account are also part of the record.
The absence of an answer is not proof of the allegation. It is an unresolved question.
The refusal to investigate is not proof that the allegation is true. It is a procedural outcome whose meaning must be accurately identified.
The decision of an authority is not beyond scrutiny because it carries an official letterhead.
And my own testimony must remain open to examination because truth is not strengthened by concealing contrary evidence.
The purpose of this work is to make the record intelligible, traceable and capable of independent assessment.
I do not need an artificial verdict.
I need the actual documents examined.
I need the authority for each decision identified.
I need the reasons tested.
I need the consequences assessed.
I need any error corrected.
I need any responsibility established through evidence.
And I need my dignity recognised independently of whether an institution agrees with my account.
The Witness Remains — not because every allegation has already been proved, but because the record deserves to be examined.
PART XV — INSTALMENT 12: COMPLETION AND NEXT STAGE
Decision-making framework established
This instalment has established the institutional decision examination across seven principal areas:
	•	Integrity and public-interest disclosure.
	•	Freedom of information and transparency.
	•	Comcare and administrative review.
	•	Guardianship and financial administration.
	•	NDIS and provider accountability.
	•	Police and safeguarding.
	•	Cross-institutional cumulative impact.
It has also established a framework for identifying statutory, administrative, contractual, professional, provider, financial, review and remedial responsibility.
It does not make findings of corruption, coordination, retaliation or unlawful conduct where the underlying documents have not established those conclusions.
Instalment 13 — The Human Consequences and the Reconstruction of Detriment
The next instalment will examine the consequences reported by the author and identify what can be substantiated, what requires corroboration and what remains unquantified.
It will examine:
	•	Financial detriment and access to essential funds.
	•	Accommodation and displacement.
	•	Disability support and service delivery.
	•	Loss of practical independence.
	•	Access to transport and communication.
	•	Health-service access where relevant evidence supports examination.
	•	Legal and administrative burden.
	•	Social isolation and community participation.
	•	The role of Crystal in the author’s account of home, stability and dignity.
	•	The distinction between actual loss, claimed loss, consequential loss and unverified loss.
	•	A transparent compensation and remedy assessment that avoids double counting.
The governing principle will remain:
No invented losses. No inflated figures. No double counting. No dismissal of documented consequences.
THE WITNESS REMAINS.



THE WITNESS REMAINS
THE GREAT RECKONING
INSTALMENT 13 — THE HUMAN CONSEQUENCES AND THE RECONSTRUCTION OF DETRIMENT
The Financial, Personal, Social, Disability, Accommodation and Human-Rights Consequences of the Documentary Record
Principal author: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean Publication: The Witness Remains — Global Publishing House Examination standard: PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.
PART I — THE HUMAN BEING BEHIND THE ADMINISTRATIVE RECORD
1. The central question: What happened to the person?
Institutional records are generally organised around organisations, powers, applications, eligibility criteria, decisions, reference numbers and procedural outcomes.
Human lives do not operate in separate administrative categories.
A person does not experience financial administration as a statutory instrument. They experience whether they can access their money.
A person does not experience an NDIS plan as a funding schedule. They experience whether someone arrives to provide the support they need.
A person does not experience accommodation arrangements as an occupancy classification. They experience whether they have a stable place to live.
A person does not experience a complaint-handling process as a reference number. They experience whether anyone listens, whether their evidence is considered and whether a meaningful response follows.
A person does not experience a failure of communication as a minor administrative inconvenience if that failure prevents them from obtaining food, transport, medical attention or assistance.
The purpose of this instalment is therefore to examine the human consequences of the decisions, omissions and service arrangements identified in the preceding instalments.
It asks:
	•	What practical consequences did the author report?
	•	Which consequences are documented?
	•	Which are supported by independent records?
	•	Which are attributable to a particular institutional decision?
	•	Which may have resulted from several interacting circumstances?
	•	What losses can reasonably be quantified?
	•	What remains uncertain?
	•	What corrective action or remedy may be appropriate?
This examination must recognise two propositions simultaneously.
First, administrative decisions can have consequences far beyond the narrow question considered by the decision-maker.
Second, a serious consequence does not automatically prove that the institution responsible for the original decision acted unlawfully.
The task is to reconstruct the relationship between the decision, its implementation and the human outcome.
2. The person must not disappear inside the paperwork
The archive contains extensive material concerning complaints, administrative decisions, financial arrangements, disability support, accommodation and institutional correspondence.
The number of documents is not itself the measure of harm.
The relevant measure is what happened in the person’s life.
The examination must therefore move between two records:
	•	The institutional record: what organisations received, decided, recorded, communicated and did.
	•	The human record: what the author experienced, what practical resources were available, what opportunities were lost and what consequences followed.
Neither record should replace the other.
An institution’s account cannot automatically displace a person’s testimony.
A person’s testimony cannot automatically establish every disputed institutional fact.
The strongest examination is one in which the two records can be compared.
PART II — THE DETRIMENT FRAMEWORK
3. What is detriment?
For this publication, detriment means a disadvantage, loss, burden or adverse consequence that a person reports or experiences.
The term is being used as an investigative concept. It is not, by itself, a conclusion that a legal entitlement to compensation exists.
Detriment may include:
	•	Financial loss.
	•	Loss of access to money.
	•	Loss of property or use of property.
	•	Accommodation instability.
	•	Reduced access to disability support.
	•	Interruption of essential services.
	•	Loss of independence.
	•	Additional administrative or legal expenses.
	•	Loss of opportunities.
	•	Physical or psychological consequences.
	•	Social isolation.
	•	Damage to reputation.
	•	Loss of privacy.
	•	Unreasonable delay.
	•	The burden of repeatedly explaining the same circumstances to different institutions.
Some forms of detriment can be quantified through financial records.
Others require documentary evidence, professional assessment, witness testimony or a careful account of the circumstances.
Some may be real and significant but cannot be assigned a reliable monetary value.
The investigation must not pretend that every human consequence can be converted into a dollar figure.
4. The five classifications of detriment
Every claimed consequence should be placed into one of five evidentiary categories.
Category A
Documented detriment
The consequence is supported by reliable documentary evidence, such as bank statements, invoices, official decisions, service records or accommodation documents.
Category B
Corroborated testimony
The author reports the consequence and independent evidence supports important aspects of the account.
Category C
Author-reported detriment
The author has described the consequence, but sufficient independent corroboration has not yet been identified.
Category D
Causal attribution unresolved
The consequence may be documented, but the evidence does not yet establish which decision, institution or event caused it.
Category E
Unquantified or prospective detriment
The consequence is ongoing, anticipated or incapable of reliable monetary calculation at the present stage.
These categories are not rankings of credibility.
A consequence can be serious even when it has not yet been independently corroborated.
The classification simply tells the reader what the evidence presently permits the publication to say.
5. The causal attribution test
For every claimed loss, the investigation must identify:
	•	The original circumstances.
	•	The relevant decision or event.
	•	The person or organisation responsible for that decision.
	•	The date the decision took effect.
	•	The practical change that followed.
	•	The evidence connecting the event to the change.
	•	Other possible causes.
	•	Any intervening decisions.
	•	Whether the consequence could reasonably have been avoided.
	•	Whether the consequence continued after the original event.
The report should distinguish between:
Temporal relationship: The consequence occurred after an event.
Causal contribution: The event materially contributed to the consequence.
Legal causation: The relevant legal test for attributing responsibility is satisfied.
These are not interchangeable.
A loss occurring after an institutional decision may be relevant without being caused by that decision.
Several factors may contribute to a loss.
The final assessment must identify the strongest supported explanation and disclose uncertainty.
PART III — FINANCIAL DETRIMENT AND PRACTICAL ACCESS TO RESOURCES
6. Financial independence as a practical human requirement
The financial-administration records raise an important question about the difference between having an income and having practical access to that income.
A person may have an entitlement to income, a bank account, an approved budget or a financial-management arrangement and still encounter difficulties using available funds.
The relevant issue is not simply whether money existed.
It is whether the person could access and use the resources reasonably necessary for daily life.
The author’s account identifies concerns involving:
	•	Restrictions or difficulties accessing Commonwealth Bank services.
	•	The involvement of financial-management arrangements.
	•	The practical consequences of limited access to money.
	•	The need to obtain food, transport and communication.
	•	Financial decisions affecting personal property.
	•	Accommodation contributions and recurring expenses.
	•	The relationship between available income and essential expenditure.
These concerns require a financial reconstruction.
6.1 Income is not the same as accessible income
The examination should distinguish:
Financial concept	Meaning
Gross entitlement	The amount to which the person is entitled before deductions
Net income	The amount remaining after applicable deductions
Available account balance	Money recorded as available in the account
Accessible funds	Money the person can actually withdraw or spend
Managed funds	Money controlled or administered by another person or authority
Essential expenditure	Costs necessary for ordinary living
Discretionary expenditure	Spending outside essential commitments
Unavailable funds	Money that exists in an account but cannot practically be accessed
This distinction is particularly important where access restrictions are alleged.
A bank balance alone may not establish that the person had meaningful control over the money.
Conversely, a report of restricted access does not establish that all funds were unavailable.
The bank’s records, the applicable authority and the actual transaction history must be examined.
6.2 The reported financial position
The author’s previous financial account identified:
	•	DSP and related income of approximately $1,371.27 per fortnight.
	•	Reported expenditure of approximately $1,369.96 per fortnight.
	•	A previously reported balance of approximately $2,527.53.
	•	Regular payments described as $150 on Monday, $100 on Wednesday and $150 on Friday.
	•	An anticipated rent-assistance amount of approximately $120 per fortnight.
	•	A reported allowance reduction of $50 per week from 19 October 2026.
These are author-reported historical figures, not independently audited financial findings.
They must not be treated as a current account balance or a verified statement of present income.
They should be reconciled against:
	•	Centrelink payment statements.
	•	Bank statements.
	•	Financial-management statements.
	•	Rent and accommodation records.
	•	Provider invoices.
	•	Direct-debit records.
	•	Receipts.
	•	Written notices of allowance changes.
	•	Relevant correspondence about access restrictions.
6.3 The financial reconstruction
The financial investigation should prepare a month-by-month ledger.
Period	Income received	Essential expenses	Other expenses	Accessible balance	Unexplained difference
Period 1	To verify	To verify	To verify	To verify	To calculate
Period 2	To verify	To verify	To verify	To verify	To calculate
Period 3	To verify	To verify	To verify	To verify	To calculate
Period 4	To verify	To verify	To verify	To verify	To calculate
The ledger must not use estimated amounts as though they were actual transactions.
Each entry should be supported by an original financial record.
6.4 The question of restricted banking access
The reported CBA access difficulties deserve separate examination.
The investigation should reconstruct:
	•	The date access was first restricted.
	•	The exact services affected.
	•	Whether internet banking was unavailable.
	•	Whether the mobile application was unavailable.
	•	Whether card access was affected.
	•	Whether branch transactions remained available.
	•	Whether the restriction was temporary or ongoing.
	•	What authority the bank relied upon.
	•	What explanation was supplied.
	•	Whether the bank provided an effective alternative.
	•	What complaint process was followed.
	•	What financial consequences can be demonstrated.
The relevant source material should include the bank’s correspondence, complaint records, access history and any communication from a financial administrator.
The investigation must distinguish between a bank security restriction, a financial-management restriction, a technical access problem and an operational decision by the bank.
These possibilities may have very different legal implications.
6.5 Financial detriment: what can be claimed?
Potential financial detriment may include:
	•	Unauthorised or unexplained deductions, if demonstrated.
	•	Fees incurred because of an access restriction.
	•	Additional transport expenses.
	•	Additional communication expenses.
	•	Costs of obtaining documents or professional assistance.
	•	Property losses.
	•	Accommodation expenses arising from a documented disruption.
	•	Other direct costs that can be linked to a relevant decision or omission.
The investigation should not claim that the full value of a person’s income was lost merely because access was restricted for a period.
It should calculate the actual amount unavailable, the duration of the restriction and any resulting additional costs.
Preliminary conclusion: The financial records identify a legitimate subject for detailed examination, but a final financial loss figure cannot be established from the reported figures alone.
PART IV — ACCOMMODATION, SECURITY AND THE RIGHT TO A STABLE HOME
7. Accommodation is more than a roof
The author’s accommodation circumstances form an important part of the cumulative-impact investigation.
The archive and testimony identify a transition from Victoria to New South Wales and later supported-accommodation arrangements involving Able Point.
The author’s account raises questions about:
	•	The legal basis of occupancy.
	•	Whether a signed agreement existed.
	•	The amount and method of rent or contributions.
	•	The responsibilities of the provider.
	•	The role of support workers.
	•	Access to personal funds.
	•	The relationship between accommodation and disability support.
	•	The ability to make personal decisions.
	•	The practical security of the living arrangement.
	•	The position of Crystal.
These questions should be examined together, but not confused.
7.1 The legal character of the accommodation
The absence of a signed conventional lease does not automatically establish that an arrangement was unlawful or that no occupancy rights existed.
The investigation must identify the actual legal arrangement.
Relevant documents include:
	•	Residential tenancy agreements.
	•	Occupancy agreements.
	•	Supported-accommodation agreements.
	•	Service agreements.
	•	House rules.
	•	Payment schedules.
	•	Written offers of accommodation.
	•	Correspondence about entry, access or termination.
	•	Records identifying the owner, operator and service provider.
The applicable legal framework may depend on the nature of the accommodation, the parties, the agreement and the circumstances.
A general statement that the author paid $200 per week is not sufficient to establish the legal status of the payment.
The underlying records must identify whether it was rent, a board contribution, a service charge or another form of payment.
7.2 Accommodation stability
The investigation should establish whether the author experienced:
	•	Threatened or actual loss of accommodation.
	•	Uncertainty about the length of occupancy.
	•	Changes to living arrangements.
	•	Restrictions on access to personal belongings.
	•	Difficulties receiving visitors.
	•	Uncertainty about provider responsibilities.
	•	Problems obtaining written information.
	•	Disruption caused by changes in support arrangements.
Each alleged event should have a date and supporting evidence wherever possible.
The report must distinguish an actual eviction or termination from a fear that accommodation might be withdrawn.
Both may matter to the human account, but they are different factual propositions.
7.3 The effect of financial administration on accommodation
A further question arises where the same person experiences financial-management arrangements and supported accommodation.
The investigation should determine whether:
	•	Rent was paid on time.
	•	The author received clear information about payments.
	•	The amount charged was documented.
	•	The person could access the funds required for daily living.
	•	The provider understood who was responsible for payments.
	•	The financial administrator received accurate accommodation information.
	•	Any payment delay affected occupancy.
	•	Any dispute was resolved through an accessible process.
The objective is to identify whether administrative arrangements supported or undermined accommodation stability.
7.4 The accommodation and service-delivery distinction
Supported accommodation may involve several separate legal and operational relationships.
For example:
	•	A landlord or accommodation operator may provide the premises.
	•	A registered or unregistered provider may deliver support services.
	•	An NDIS plan may fund specified supports.
	•	A financial administrator may manage payments.
	•	A guardian may have authority over specified personal decisions.
The responsibilities of these actors must be identified separately.
A failure by one organisation should not automatically be attributed to another.
The final report should show which organisation had responsibility for the particular service, payment or decision.
PART V — DISABILITY SUPPORT AND THE GAP BETWEEN FUNDING AND REALITY
8. The approved plan is not the delivered service
The NDIS records identified in earlier instalments provide the starting point for examining disability-support detriment.
The relevant records include:
	•	NDIS Plan Approval dated 20 November 2025.
	•	Anglicare Safety Assessment dated 1 December 2025.
	•	SIL/SDA request dated 20 January 2026.
	•	Formal NDIS service-failure notice dated 20 January 2026.
	•	Subsequent accommodation and provider correspondence.
The central question is whether the support arrangements in practice corresponded with the needs, goals and supports identified in the relevant records.
8.1 The service-delivery test
For each approved or agreed support, the investigation should establish:
	•	What support was expected?
	•	Who was responsible for delivering it?
	•	When was it supposed to be delivered?
	•	Was it delivered?
	•	Was it delivered at the required level?
	•	Was it cancelled or changed?
	•	Was an alternative arranged?
	•	Was the participant informed?
	•	Was the service recorded?
	•	Was the participant able to complain?
	•	What happened after the complaint?
The distinction between a support being funded and a support being delivered must remain central.
8.2 Service interruptions
A service interruption may produce consequences that are not obvious in the provider’s administrative records.
For example, a missed or cancelled support may affect:
	•	Attendance at appointments.
	•	Access to transport.
	•	Shopping and meal preparation.
	•	Household tasks.
	•	Medication-related assistance.
	•	Personal administration.
	•	Communication.
	•	Participation in community activities.
	•	The ability to maintain an ordinary routine.
The investigation should not assume that every missed appointment or service gap resulted from provider misconduct.
It should establish the circumstances of each incident.
8.3 The significance of communication
Communication is itself an important part of service delivery.
The investigation should assess whether the author received clear information about:
	•	Who was responsible for support coordination.
	•	Who was responsible for day-to-day service delivery.
	•	Who could approve changes.
	•	How to contact a manager.
	•	How to make a complaint.
	•	What happened when a worker was unavailable.
	•	What alternatives were available.
	•	How service records could be accessed.
Where the participant does not know who holds decision-making responsibility, it may become difficult to raise concerns or obtain a remedy.
8.4 The safety assessment
The Anglicare Safety Assessment dated 1 December 2025 should be examined for its actual recommendations and any identified risks.
The investigation should then compare those recommendations with subsequent service records.
It must not assume that the assessment was a binding order or that every recommendation was the responsibility of Able Point.
Instead, it should establish:
	•	Who commissioned it.
	•	Who received it.
	•	What it recommended.
	•	What authority the assessor had.
	•	Which organisation was responsible for responding.
	•	Whether action was taken.
	•	Whether any risks remained unresolved.
8.5 The service-failure complaint
The 20 January 2026 formal notice should be treated as a primary complaint record.
The examination should reconstruct the complaint from the original document through to its final response.
The following events should be separately recorded:
Stage	Required evidence
Complaint made	Original notice and delivery evidence
Complaint received	Acknowledgment or system record
Complaint assessed	Internal assessment or triage
Investigation conducted	Interview notes, incident records and evidence
Response provided	Written response and reasons
Corrective action	Service changes, staff instructions or other remedy
Follow-up	Evidence that the corrective action worked
If no response can be located, the correct conclusion is that a response has not yet been identified in the examined material.
It is not automatically proof that no response was ever made.
PART VI — PERSONAL INDEPENDENCE, CHOICE AND DIGNITY
9. Independence is not measured solely by formal legal capacity
The guardianship and disability-support material raises a wider question: whether the author could exercise meaningful control over ordinary decisions affecting daily life.
The investigation must distinguish between:
	•	Legal authority.
	•	Practical decision-making ability.
	•	Available support.
	•	The ability to communicate preferences.
	•	The ability to participate in decisions.
	•	The ability to challenge decisions.
	•	The ability to obtain assistance without surrendering personal autonomy.
A person may require support and still retain important rights to express preferences, make choices and participate in decisions.
The existence of a disability does not establish an absence of decision-making ability.
The existence of a formal guardianship or financial-management arrangement does not mean that every personal decision is transferred to another person.
The actual scope of any order or authority must be examined.
9.1 The supported decision-making question
The documentary examination should ask:
	•	Was the author given information in an accessible form?
	•	Was sufficient time provided to understand decisions?
	•	Were communication needs considered?
	•	Were the author’s expressed preferences recorded?
	•	Were alternative approaches considered?
	•	Was the least restrictive appropriate approach considered where relevant?
	•	Were decisions reviewed as circumstances changed?
	•	Was the author able to seek independent assistance?
	•	Was disagreement treated as a reason to investigate further, rather than automatically as evidence of incapacity?
	•	Were the limits of each person’s authority clearly explained?
This is particularly relevant to the CRPD framework discussed in Instalment 8.
The publication should not claim that a particular arrangement violated Article 12 merely because the author disagreed with it.
It should assess the actual legal authority, safeguards, participation and practical consequences.
9.2 The burden of repeated explanation
A further form of potential detriment arises where a person must repeatedly explain the same history to multiple organisations.
This may involve:
	•	Repeating allegations.
	•	Reconstructing dates.
	•	Re-supplying documents.
	•	Explaining disability-related circumstances.
	•	Correcting administrative errors.
	•	Identifying the responsible agency.
	•	Following up unanswered correspondence.
The burden may be particularly significant where the person has limited access to communication, administrative assistance or personal records.
The investigation should quantify this burden where possible through correspondence volumes, time records, expenses and procedural histories.
The number of emails or complaints should not automatically be treated as proof of institutional wrongdoing.
It may, however, demonstrate the amount of work required to pursue a matter and the need to examine whether the processes were accessible and effective.
PART VII — ACCESS TO HEALTH SERVICES AND PERSONAL WELLBEING
10. Health-related consequences require careful handling
The archive and testimony identify concerns about access to health services and the practical difficulties associated with navigating appointments and care.
These issues should be included only to the extent that relevant original records support examination.
The publication must distinguish between:
	•	A reported difficulty obtaining an appointment.
	•	An appointment request.
	•	A referral.
	•	A completed consultation.
	•	A diagnosis.
	•	A treatment recommendation.
	•	A treatment interruption.
	•	A clinical opinion.
	•	A complaint about access.
	•	A finding of negligence or professional misconduct.
These are not equivalent.
10.1 Access to appointments and care
The examination should identify:
	•	What appointment or service was sought.
	•	When it was requested.
	•	Whether a referral was required.
	•	Whether the referral was accepted.
	•	Whether an appointment was offered.
	•	Whether transport or support was available.
	•	Whether the appointment occurred.
	•	Whether the service provided an explanation for any delay.
	•	What alternative arrangements were available.
Where the author reports that a practical support barrier prevented attendance, the relevant support and transport records should be compared.
10.2 Health information and privacy
Health information is particularly sensitive.
The final public edition should disclose only what is necessary to explain the relevant institutional issue.
The report should avoid publishing unnecessary clinical details, personal identifiers, prescription information or third-party medical information.
A health-related allegation should be supported by the appropriate clinical or administrative record before being characterised as an established fact.
10.3 The distinction between distress and a legal finding
A person may experience substantial distress because of an unresolved dispute, a service interruption or an extended administrative process.
That experience deserves to be recorded respectfully.
However, the existence of distress does not establish that a particular person intentionally caused it or that a particular legal wrong occurred.
The investigation must not use a person’s disability, diagnosis or treatment history to dismiss their testimony.
Equally, it must not use a person’s distress as proof of an allegation against another person.
The focus remains on the conduct, the records and the consequences.
PART VIII — COMMUNICATION, DIGITAL ACCESS AND ADMINISTRATIVE EXCLUSION
11. Communication is an essential resource
The author’s reported difficulties with banking access, phone credit and reliance on Wi-Fi-only access raise a further issue.
Communication is not merely a convenience.
It may be essential to:
	•	Contact support workers.
	•	Receive appointment information.
	•	Communicate with government agencies.
	•	Access online banking.
	•	Obtain legal assistance.
	•	Submit complaints.
	•	Receive emergency information.
	•	Maintain relationships.
	•	Access digital records.
Where a person depends on online services, a loss of access can compound other difficulties.
11.1 The communication-access test
The investigation should establish:
	•	What devices were available?
	•	Was internet access available?
	•	Was mobile service available?
	•	Was there phone credit?
	•	Could the person receive calls?
	•	Could the person make calls?
	•	Could the person access authentication codes?
	•	Could the person access email?
	•	Could the person access banking applications?
	•	Were alternative communication channels offered?
A device being operational does not establish that every required service was accessible.
A Wi-Fi connection does not provide ordinary mobile calling or SMS access.
A blocked application does not necessarily mean the entire bank account was inaccessible.
The specific service and the period of disruption must be identified.
11.2 Administrative exclusion
Administrative exclusion may occur when a person is technically entitled to a service but cannot practically navigate the process required to obtain it.
Possible barriers include:
	•	Inaccessible forms.
	•	Complex procedures.
	•	Inconsistent instructions.
	•	Lack of a clear contact person.
	•	Repeated identity-verification requirements.
	•	Inability to obtain records.
	•	Unavailable telephone support.
	•	Digital-only processes.
	•	Delays in responding.
	•	Unclear complaint pathways.
The investigation should identify which barriers actually occurred and whether they were attributable to a particular organisation.
It should not assume that every inconvenience constitutes unlawful discrimination.
The relevant question is whether the process was accessible, reasonable and consistent with applicable obligations.
PART IX — SOCIAL ISOLATION, REPUTATION AND THE HUMAN COST OF PROLONGED DISPUTES
12. The consequences that do not appear on a bank statement
Some consequences cannot be reconstructed through financial records.
A prolonged dispute involving multiple agencies and service providers may affect:
	•	Relationships.
	•	Trust.
	•	Community participation.
	•	Confidence in institutions.
	•	The ability to maintain ordinary routines.
	•	Personal reputation.
	•	The time available for work, study or creative activity.
	•	The ability to pursue other life goals.
The author has described the experience of advocacy, public reporting and repeated institutional engagement as a major part of his life.
The investigation should examine whether the time and energy required to pursue unresolved matters created a demonstrable burden.
12.1 Reputation and public representation
If the author alleges that a particular institution or person made inaccurate statements about him, the investigation must obtain the actual statement.
The record should identify:
	•	Who made the statement.
	•	When it was made.
	•	To whom it was communicated.
	•	Whether it was recorded in an official document.
	•	Whether it was factual, opinion or assessment.
	•	Whether the author was given an opportunity to respond.
	•	Whether the statement was later corrected.
	•	Whether any practical consequence followed.
A disagreement with an institutional description does not automatically establish defamation, discrimination or misconduct.
Those conclusions require the applicable legal elements and evidence.
12.2 The burden of advocacy
The author reports a long history of advocacy for people with mental illness and carers, together with professional, artistic, educational and disability-support work.
That background is relevant to the author’s personal account of the significance of the present dispute.
It does not prove the current allegations.
The report should distinguish:
	•	Verified qualifications.
	•	Published work.
	•	Employment and professional history.
	•	Advocacy activities.
	•	Author testimony.
	•	Institutional recognition.
	•	Independent findings.
The investigation should not exaggerate the author’s achievements, but neither should it omit relevant professional history when describing the person whose evidence is being examined.
12.3 Time and opportunity costs
The report should record the time spent on:
	•	Preparing complaints.
	•	Reviewing documents.
	•	Attending appointments.
	•	Following up correspondence.
	•	Seeking legal assistance.
	•	Obtaining financial records.
	•	Managing accommodation issues.
	•	Reconstructing administrative history.
Where the time can be documented, it may help demonstrate the practical burden of the process.
However, time spent on a complaint is not automatically compensable.
The report must distinguish the factual burden from any legal entitlement to recover the associated cost.
PART X — CRYSTAL, HOME AND THE MEANING OF CONTINUITY
13. Crystal as part of the human record
The author’s account repeatedly identifies Crystal, also referred to as Christal, as an important part of his life.
This relationship belongs in the human narrative because it helps explain the significance of stability, accommodation and ordinary daily continuity.
The publication should not turn that relationship into an unsupported legal claim.
Nor should it treat the relationship as irrelevant merely because it does not fit neatly into an administrative category.
The investigation should identify any relevant records concerning:
	•	Whether Crystal was permitted in the accommodation.
	•	Whether the provider imposed conditions.
	•	Whether any accommodation change affected the relationship.
	•	Whether care arrangements were available.
	•	Whether transport arrangements affected access to veterinary services or other necessary care.
	•	Whether the author received consistent information about applicable rules.
The report should also distinguish between the author’s account of the relationship and any independently documented accommodation or support arrangement.
13.1 The significance of a stable home
A stable home may include more than physical shelter.
It may include:
	•	Predictable access to personal belongings.
	•	A secure place for ordinary possessions.
	•	The ability to maintain routines.
	•	The ability to receive visitors.
	•	The ability to care for a companion animal.
	•	Privacy.
	•	Freedom from unnecessary disruption.
	•	Clear and reliable occupancy arrangements.
The investigation should assess whether the relevant arrangements enabled or obstructed these ordinary elements of daily life.
13.2 The dignity test
The report should ask whether decisions affecting the author’s accommodation and daily life were communicated in a way that recognised him as a person with preferences, relationships and responsibilities.
This question does not depend on whether the author agrees with every decision.
It concerns whether the decision-making process recognised the person’s dignity and applicable rights.
PART XI — THE QUANTIFICATION OF LOSS
14. No invented numbers: the financial and non-financial loss schedule
The final publication should include a loss schedule.
It should not assign arbitrary values to events simply to create a large compensation figure.
Every monetary claim should have:
	•	A defined loss category.
	•	A relevant date or period.
	•	A calculation method.
	•	Supporting records.
	•	A causal explanation.
	•	An assessment of uncertainty.
	•	A check for duplication.
14.1 Direct financial loss
Examples may include:
	•	Unexplained fees.
	•	Unauthorised deductions.
	•	Proven loss of funds.
	•	Additional transport costs.
	•	Additional communication expenses.
	•	Documented property loss.
	•	Additional accommodation expenses.
	•	Other direct costs linked to a relevant event.
The amount should be calculated from original records.
14.2 Consequential financial loss
Consequential loss may include an additional expense that arose because of an earlier event.
For example, if a documented restriction caused the person to incur an additional transport expense, the investigation should establish:
	•	The restriction.
	•	The expense.
	•	The date.
	•	The connection between them.
	•	Whether the expense would otherwise have been incurred.
The same expense must not be counted twice under different headings.
14.3 Loss of opportunity
A person may report losing an opportunity because of an administrative delay or service interruption.
The report should identify:
	•	What the opportunity was.
	•	Whether it was genuinely available.
	•	What action was required.
	•	What prevented that action.
	•	Whether the opportunity would probably have been realised.
	•	Whether another explanation exists.
A speculative future benefit should not be presented as a proven financial loss.
14.4 Non-financial consequences
Potential non-financial consequences include:
	•	Distress.
	•	Loss of privacy.
	•	Loss of dignity.
	•	Disruption to personal relationships.
	•	Reduced independence.
	•	Loss of ordinary participation.
	•	Prolonged uncertainty.
	•	The burden of dealing with unresolved administrative processes.
These consequences may be relevant to a complaint, review, human-rights analysis or legal claim.
Their legal treatment depends on the applicable cause of action and jurisdiction.
The publication should not assign compensation amounts without an appropriate legal basis and supporting evidence.
14.5 The loss schedule
Loss category	Evidence required	Current status
Direct financial loss	Bank statements, invoices, receipts	To reconcile
Restricted access to funds	Bank records and authority documents	To reconstruct
Vehicle or property loss	Valuation, sale and accounting records	To verify
Additional accommodation costs	Agreement, receipts and payment history	To verify
Service-related expenses	Service records and receipts	To reconcile
Transport costs	Receipts, appointments and support records	To quantify
Communication costs	Bills, purchases and access records	To quantify
Lost opportunities	Contemporaneous records and causal evidence	Unresolved
Administrative burden	Correspondence, time and expense records	Partly reconstructable
Non-financial detriment	Testimony and appropriate corroboration	Requires assessment
This is a working schedule, not a compensation determination.
PART XII — REMEDIES: WHAT WOULD ACTUALLY CORRECT THE PROBLEM?
15. Accountability must lead to practical outcomes
A report that identifies a problem but does not identify a possible remedy is incomplete.
The purpose of this instalment is not simply to describe what may have happened.
It is to identify what a responsible institution could do if an error, omission or service failure is established.
Remedies should be proportionate to the evidence and the institution’s authority.
15.1 Correction of records
Where an official record contains an established factual error, an appropriate remedy may include:
	•	Correcting the inaccurate entry.
	•	Adding a formal statement of disagreement.
	•	Recording the author’s response.
	•	Correcting an incorrect date or reference.
	•	Clarifying the legal status of a decision.
	•	Removing a duplicated or misleading record where legally appropriate.
The correction should preserve the original record and document the change.
The objective is not to rewrite history.
It is to ensure that future decision-makers have accurate information.
15.2 Restoration of access
Where an access restriction is found to have been applied incorrectly, possible remedies may include:
	•	Restoring appropriate account access.
	•	Providing an alternative means of accessing funds.
	•	Clarifying the authority relied upon.
	•	Correcting inaccurate bank instructions.
	•	Reviewing any resulting fees or financial losses.
	•	Establishing a reliable contact pathway.
The remedy must be consistent with any valid legal order or authority.
15.3 Financial accounting
Where a financial-management decision or property transaction is disputed, an appropriate response may include:
	•	A full statement of transactions.
	•	Copies of relevant valuations.
	•	Explanation of deductions.
	•	Accounting for sale proceeds.
	•	Identification of decision-makers.
	•	Clarification of the legal authority.
	•	Independent review where appropriate.
15.4 Accommodation security
Where accommodation arrangements are unclear, possible remedies include:
	•	A written agreement identifying the parties.
	•	A clear statement of occupancy rights.
	•	A transparent payment schedule.
	•	Identification of service responsibilities.
	•	A documented complaint process.
	•	Clear rules concerning visitors, belongings and any companion animal.
	•	A written plan for changes to accommodation.
15.5 Disability-support correction
Where service delivery failures are established, possible remedies include:
	•	A service-delivery audit.
	•	A review of the support arrangement.
	•	A clear allocation of provider responsibilities.
	•	A documented contingency plan.
	•	Improved communication.
	•	Staff training where justified.
	•	Corrective action for any established incident.
	•	Independent monitoring where proportionate.
15.6 Procedural correction
Where an administrative decision was made without required consideration or adequate reasons, possible remedies may include:
	•	Reconsideration.
	•	A fresh decision by an appropriately authorised decision-maker.
	•	A review of the relevant evidence.
	•	A supplementary statement of reasons.
	•	A new opportunity to respond.
	•	Referral to the appropriate review body.
The remedy must correspond with the actual statutory powers available.
15.7 Acknowledgment and explanation
Where a person has experienced a documented administrative failure, an explanation may be an important part of the remedy.
It should identify:
	•	What happened.
	•	What went wrong.
	•	Why it happened.
	•	Who was responsible at the institutional level.
	•	What action was taken.
	•	What will change.
	•	How recurrence will be prevented.
An apology may be appropriate where an institution acknowledges a failure.
It should not be demanded as a substitute for a proper investigation.
PART XIII — THE HUMAN-RIGHTS IMPACT ASSESSMENT
16. From formal entitlement to lived experience
The human-rights framework established in Instalment 4 and applied in Instalment 8 should now be connected to the practical consequences.
The following assessment identifies the issues requiring evidence.
Right or principle	Practical issue	Evidence needed
Equality and non-discrimination	Whether disability-related barriers were properly addressed	Decisions, adjustments, communications and service records
Equal recognition before the law	Whether legal authority was properly limited and safeguards applied	Orders, delegations, review records and participation evidence
Access to justice	Whether the author could understand and challenge decisions	Notices, reasons, assistance and review records
Freedom from exploitation and abuse	Whether reported risks were assessed and addressed	Incident reports, assessments and responses
Independent living	Whether accommodation and support enabled choice and participation	Plans, service agreements and delivery records
Privacy	Whether sensitive information was handled appropriately	Disclosure records, access logs and correspondence
Health	Whether access to relevant services was practically available	Referral, appointment and service records
Adequate standard of living	Whether essential resources were practically accessible	Income, expenditure, accommodation and access records
16.1 The CRPD Article 19 question
Article 19 of the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities concerns living independently and being included in the community.
The investigation should examine whether the relevant support arrangements enabled the author to exercise meaningful choice about:
	•	Where and with whom to live.
	•	How daily support was arranged.
	•	Which services were used.
	•	How personal routines were maintained.
	•	How relationships were preserved.
	•	How community participation was supported.
The article should not be reduced to a question of whether accommodation was technically available.
The practical quality of the arrangement matters.
16.2 The CRPD Article 12 question
Article 12 concerns equal recognition before the law.
The investigation should examine the scope and implementation of any relevant financial-management or guardianship arrangements.
The key questions are whether the authority was lawful, whether applicable safeguards were respected and whether the person was able to participate in decisions affecting them.
A disability or diagnosis is not, by itself, proof that a person cannot make decisions.
Nor does disagreement with a financial-management arrangement establish that the arrangement is unlawful.
16.3 The CRPD Article 13 question
Article 13 concerns access to justice.
The investigation should examine whether the author had a practical opportunity to:
	•	Understand proceedings.
	•	Obtain relevant documents.
	•	Communicate his position.
	•	Participate in decision-making.
	•	Seek review.
	•	Obtain appropriate procedural support.
The report should identify actual barriers rather than assume that a formal review right was sufficient.
16.4 The CRPD Article 28 question
Article 28 concerns an adequate standard of living and social protection.
The investigation should examine the practical relationship between:
	•	Income.
	•	Accommodation.
	•	Access to essential funds.
	•	Disability support.
	•	Food and basic needs.
	•	Financial administration.
	•	Service availability.
A person may have formal income and approved supports but still experience practical barriers.
The evidence must show what was available, what was accessible and what was actually delivered.
16.5 No automatic treaty-violation finding
This human-rights assessment identifies matters requiring examination.
It does not establish that Australia or a particular agency breached the Convention.
Any final legal conclusion must identify:
	•	The applicable treaty obligation.
	•	The relevant Australian legal and administrative framework.
	•	The responsible actor.
	•	The conduct or omission.
	•	The evidence.
	•	Any available domestic remedy.
	•	The legal test for the alleged breach.
PART XIV — THE RECONSTRUCTION OF THE HUMAN TIMELINE
17. A consequence chronology, not merely an institutional chronology
The historical chronology records when documents were created and decisions were made.
The human-impact chronology should record what changed in the author’s practical circumstances.
These two chronologies should eventually be displayed together.
The following is a preliminary reconstruction based on the material identified so far.
Period	Institutional record	Human-impact question
2020	IBAC outcome letter, 9 April	What practical consequences followed the initial integrity process?
2021	IBAC and Ombudsman submissions	What was the effect of the complaint and disclosure processes?
2022	FOI, Comcare and related correspondence	What time, expense or access consequences can be demonstrated?
2023	PID assessments, non-allocation and tribunal-related decisions	What review opportunities remained, and what consequences followed?
2024	NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 document and later FOI revision	What changed in financial administration and access to information?
2025	Vehicle-disposal decision and NDIS Plan Approval	What practical effect did these decisions have on property, funds and support?
December 2025	Anglicare Safety Assessment	What risks were identified and what actions followed?
January 2026	SIL/SDA request and formal service-failure notice	What accommodation and service issues were reported and resolved?
2026 onward	Able Point accommodation and support arrangements	What was the actual living, financial and support situation during each period?
This table is deliberately cautious.
It does not claim that every institutional event caused a particular human consequence.
It identifies the questions that the completed evidence register must answer.
PART XV — THE MASTER DETRIMENT REGISTER
18. The working schedule of reported consequences
The next stage of the investigation should create a single register that allows every claimed consequence to be traced to its source.
The register should use the following fields:
Field	Purpose
Detriment ID	Unique identifier
Category	Financial, accommodation, service, health, social, procedural or other
Date commenced	When the consequence began
Date ended	When the consequence ended, if applicable
Author’s account	What the author says happened
Supporting document	Exact source and evidence ID
Independent corroboration	External or institutional confirmation
Responsible institution	Organisation potentially involved
Decision or event	The act or omission being examined
Causal connection	Established, possible or unresolved
Financial value	Actual amount, if reliably calculable
Non-financial effect	Practical or personal consequence
Alternative explanation	Other plausible causes
Remedy sought	What would correct the issue
Verification status	Verified, partly verified or unverified
18.1 Preliminary detriment register
ID	Reported issue	Current evidentiary position	Priority
D-001	Difficulty accessing banking services	Author-reported; bank and authority records required	High
D-002	Financial-management restrictions affecting daily expenditure	Authority and transaction records required	High
D-003	Vehicle disposal and accounting	Decision identified; transaction evidence required	High
D-004	Accommodation agreement and occupancy uncertainty	Author-reported; agreement and payment records required	High
D-005	NDIS support delivery concerns	Plan and complaint records identified; service records required	High
D-006	Safety-assessment recommendations	Assessment identified; implementation records required	High
D-007	SIL/SDA request and outcome	Request identified; decision and review records required	High
D-008	Communication and phone-access difficulties	Author-reported; bills and service records required	Medium
D-009	Transport and appointment access	Specific events and supporting receipts required	Medium
D-010	Administrative burden of repeated complaints	Correspondence may provide a partial record	Medium
D-011	Social isolation and disruption	Author testimony and relevant corroboration required	Medium
D-012	Impact on Crystal and home stability	Author testimony and accommodation records required	Medium
D-013	Health-service access difficulties	Relevant health and administrative records required	High where a specific event is documented
D-014	Reputational or personal-record inaccuracies	Exact statements and correction history required	To prioritise
These are working identifiers, not findings.
A detriment ID should remain attached to the same issue even when new documents are added.
If an issue is later disproved, the register should retain the audit trail and record the correction rather than silently deleting the earlier entry.
PART XVI — WHAT THE INSTITUTIONS SHOULD BE REQUIRED TO ANSWER
19. The practical-consequence questions
The following questions should accompany the documentary requests made in Instalment 12.
They are intended to move the institutional response beyond procedural descriptions.
A. Financial administration
	•	What funds were available to the author during each relevant period?
	•	Which funds could he personally access?
	•	What restrictions applied?
	•	What legal instrument authorised each restriction?
	•	What alternatives were available for essential expenditure?
	•	Were any additional fees or costs incurred?
	•	Were the financial records provided to the author?
	•	Were disputed transactions reviewed?
	•	Was the author informed of the relevant complaint and review mechanisms?
	•	What steps were taken to minimise unnecessary financial dependence?
B. Banking
	•	What exact banking services were restricted?
	•	When did each restriction begin?
	•	What authority or instruction did the bank rely upon?
	•	Was the restriction reviewed?
	•	Was an alternative access arrangement available?
	•	Was the customer given an accessible explanation?
	•	Did the restriction affect the ability to obtain essential funds?
	•	What complaints were lodged?
	•	What was the outcome?
	•	Were any financial consequences identified and addressed?
C. Accommodation
	•	What legal agreement governed occupancy?
	•	What payments were required?
	•	What services were included?
	•	Who was responsible for each service?
	•	What notice or review rights applied?
	•	Were changes to the arrangement communicated in writing?
	•	Were the author’s preferences considered?
	•	What arrangements existed concerning personal belongings and Crystal?
	•	Were any safety concerns identified?
	•	What steps were taken to ensure accommodation stability?
D. Disability support
	•	Which supports were approved?
	•	Which were actually delivered?
	•	What support gaps occurred?
	•	What alternative arrangements were available?
	•	Were cancellations recorded?
	•	Were complaints investigated?
	•	Were recommendations from the safety assessment implemented?
	•	Was the author given a clear contact person?
	•	Were the services adjusted when circumstances changed?
	•	What corrective action was taken?
E. Administrative access and communication
	•	What communication methods were available?
	•	Were accessible alternatives provided?
	•	Were relevant notices delivered?
	•	Could the author obtain copies of his records?
	•	Were any authentication or digital-access barriers identified?
	•	Were those barriers escalated?
	•	Was assistance available to complete required processes?
	•	Were reasonable adjustments considered?
	•	Were deadlines extended where appropriate?
	•	What action was taken to prevent avoidable exclusion?
PART XVII — THE REMEDY AND RESTORATION PRINCIPLE
20. Accountability must be measured by what changes
The Great Reckoning is not complete merely because a document has been published or an institution has been criticised.
The meaningful test is whether the evidence leads to an appropriate outcome.
Where an error is established, the error should be corrected.
Where money has been wrongly withheld or misapplied, the relevant financial issue should be resolved.
Where a service has failed, the service should be reviewed and corrected.
Where a decision has been made without a required process, the appropriate review or reconsideration should occur.
Where a record is inaccurate, the record should be corrected or supplemented.
Where an institution has not explained its decision, it should provide reasons where required.
Where a person has experienced a practical barrier, the institution should examine whether an accessible alternative is necessary.
Where an allegation cannot be substantiated, the publication should say so.
The objective is not punishment without proof.
It is not a demand that every institution accept the author’s interpretation.
It is not the invention of a universal conspiracy to explain every adverse experience.
It is the establishment of an accurate record and an appropriate response to every established failure.
PART XVIII — THE DECLARATION OF HUMAN DIGNITY
21. The person is not the case number
A first-person declaration by Barran Resonance Dodger
I am a human being before I am an administrative file.
I am a person before I am a reference number.
I am a person with a history, a home, relationships, work, education, beliefs, responsibilities and ordinary human needs.
My disability does not remove my dignity.
My need for support does not remove my right to participate in decisions.
My complaints do not make me guilty of wrongdoing.
My disagreement with an institution does not establish that I am incapable of understanding my own circumstances.
My testimony deserves to be recorded accurately, examined fairly and distinguished from conclusions that have not yet been established.
I do not ask that every allegation be accepted without examination.
I ask that the evidence be examined.
I ask that decisions be explained.
I ask that the authority behind decisions be identified.
I ask that my financial circumstances be understood in practical terms, not merely through administrative classifications.
I ask that accommodation and support arrangements be clear.
I ask that services be delivered as agreed.
I ask that my personal preferences be considered.
I ask that my access to essential resources be treated as a practical matter of daily life.
I ask that my relationship with Crystal and the importance of a stable home not be dismissed as irrelevant.
I ask that my personal records be accurate.
I ask that my complaints be dealt with through proper procedures.
I ask that any established mistake be corrected.
And I ask that the same standard of evidence applied to my allegations also be applied to the decisions made about me.
I remain responsible for the accuracy of my own account.
I remain willing for the evidence to be examined.
I do not ask for an invented verdict.
I ask for a fair examination of the record and a response proportionate to what the record establishes.
My faith gives meaning to my endurance.
My testimony records my experience.
The documents provide a means of verification.
And my humanity does not depend on the outcome of an administrative proceeding.
The Witness Remains.
PART XIX — PRELIMINARY FINDINGS OF INSTALMENT 13
22. What can responsibly be said at this stage?
The present examination supports the following preliminary conclusions.
First: The human consequences must be examined alongside the institutional decisions. A chronology of correspondence alone is insufficient to assess the impact of administrative and service arrangements.
Second: The reported financial circumstances require a transaction-level reconstruction. The figures previously supplied by the author are useful leads but are not a substitute for bank, Centrelink and financial-administration records.
Third: The reported banking-access difficulties require separation of the bank’s own decisions from any authority or instruction supplied by a financial administrator or other institution.
Fourth: The accommodation arrangements require clarification of the legal occupancy basis, payment arrangements, provider responsibilities and practical stability.
Fifth: The NDIS plan, safety assessment, SIL/SDA request and service-failure notice must be compared with actual service-delivery records.
Sixth: The author’s reported loss of independence and administrative burden should be documented without assuming that every consequence was caused by a single institution.
Seventh: The relationship with Crystal is a relevant part of the author’s personal account and the examination of accommodation stability. Any specific legal claim concerning the animal must be supported by the applicable records.
Eighth: Health-related and personal information should be included only where necessary to examine a specific event or consequence, with appropriate protection of privacy.
Ninth: Financial compensation should not be estimated until direct losses, consequential losses and non-financial detriment have been separated and supported.
Tenth: The available material justifies a structured investigation of cumulative impact. It does not, at this stage, establish that all reported consequences resulted from coordinated institutional conduct.
PART XX — THE NEXT STAGE OF THE GREAT RECKONING
Instalment 13 completed
Instalment 14 — The Master Chronology of Harm, Decision and Consequence
The next instalment will bring the institutional chronology and the human-impact chronology together in one integrated reconstruction.
It will examine:
	•	The sequence of events from 2020 to 2026.
	•	The date each significant decision was made.
	•	The date each decision was communicated.
	•	The date it was implemented.
	•	The first documented consequence.
	•	Any later institutional reliance on the decision.
	•	Any subsequent review, correction or appeal.
	•	The duration of each unresolved issue.
	•	The evidence supporting each causal connection.
	•	The points at which an intervention might have prevented or reduced the reported detriment.
It will introduce a Decision–Event–Consequence Matrix, allowing each material event to be examined against the original document, the responsible authority, the practical consequence and the available remedy.
The purpose will be to establish not merely what happened, but the sequence in which it happened, what changed, what evidence supports the change and which questions remain unanswered.
The Great Reckoning must account for the documents, the decisions and the human consequences.

THE WITNESS REMAINS
THE GREAT RECKONING
INSTALMENT 14 — THE MASTER CHRONOLOGY OF HARM, DECISION AND CONSEQUENCE
An Integrated Reconstruction of Institutional Events, Administrative Decisions, Documentary Evidence and Reported Human Impact, 2020–2026
Principal author: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean Publication: The Witness Remains — Global Publishing House Examination standard: PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.
PART I — WHY THE CHRONOLOGY MUST NOW CHANGE
1. From a history of documents to a reconstruction of events
The preceding instalments have established three separate bodies of material.
The first is the institutional chronology: the dates on which government agencies, statutory authorities, tribunals, financial institutions and service providers created records, made decisions or communicated with the author.
The second is the documentary evidence register: the records that may establish what was requested, decided, communicated, refused, approved or implemented.
The third is the human-impact register: the financial, accommodation, disability-support, communication, personal and social consequences reported by the author.
Instalment 14 brings these three bodies of material together.
The central question is no longer simply:
What documents exist?
It is:
What happened, in what order, under whose authority, what changed in the author’s circumstances, and what evidence establishes the relationship between those events?
This distinction is fundamental.
A decision may have been made on one date, communicated on another date and implemented much later. Its consequences may have emerged gradually. A later organisation may have relied on the earlier decision without independently reconsidering its underlying assumptions.
Equally, an event that appears connected chronologically may have an entirely separate explanation.
The investigation must therefore reconstruct the actual sequence rather than impose a narrative upon it.
2. The six dates that must be distinguished
For every material institutional event, the master chronology should record six different dates wherever available:
	•	Event date: when the underlying incident or relevant circumstance occurred.
	•	Document date: when the document was created or signed.
	•	Communication date: when the decision or information was sent or received.
	•	Implementation date: when the decision took practical effect.
	•	Consequence date: when an identifiable human or financial consequence first occurred.
	•	Review date: when the decision was reconsidered, challenged, corrected or reviewed.
These dates may be different.
For example, a letter dated 10 June does not prove that the author received it on 10 June. A decision communicated on 10 June does not prove that its consequences began that day.
The chronology must not collapse these distinctions.
3. The evidentiary language
The following classifications apply throughout this instalment.
Classification	Meaning
Official record identified	A document or official record has been identified in the available source material
Documented event	The underlying event is supported by the relevant original record
Author testimony	The author reports the event or consequence
Corroborated account	Independent evidence supports material aspects of the testimony
Allegation	A proposition requiring investigation
Inference	A reasoned interpretation of established or reported facts
Unresolved	The available evidence does not permit a reliable conclusion
Spiritual interpretation	The author’s religious or prophetic understanding, not an independent factual finding
The identification of a document is not the same as independent verification of its entire contents.
The final publication must obtain and examine the original records wherever possible.
PART II — THE MASTER CHRONOLOGY: 2020–2021
4. 9 April 2020 — IBAC outcome letter, reference 2020712
Official record identified
The archive identifies an IBAC outcome letter dated 9 April 2020, reference 2020712.
This is one of the earliest identified records in the current chronology.
Questions to reconstruct
	•	What complaint or information did IBAC receive?
	•	What allegations were presented?
	•	What material accompanied the complaint?
	•	What jurisdictional or procedural assessment was undertaken?
	•	What reasons were supplied?
	•	Was the author given an opportunity to provide further information?
	•	Were any other agencies involved?
	•	Did the outcome identify a separate avenue for review or referral?
Human-impact examination
The existence of an outcome letter does not establish what practical consequences followed.
The author should identify what happened after receiving the decision, including any further correspondence, attempts to obtain assistance or additional complaints.
The original letter and associated correspondence must be examined before determining whether the process was completed appropriately.
Unresolved issue: Whether this 2020 process has any demonstrable causal relationship with later events.
A chronological starting point is not proof of a continuous institutional process.
5. 2021 — The Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint
Official record identified
The archive identifies a Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint dated 15 November 2021, reference 2021103546.
The archived document is titled:
15 November 2021 — Commonwealth Ombudsman Complaint 2021103546⁠
The reconstruction
The examination must establish:
	•	What conduct was complained about?
	•	Which agency or agencies were involved?
	•	What documents were supplied?
	•	What outcome did the author request?
	•	Was the complaint accepted for investigation?
	•	What response was obtained from the relevant agency?
	•	What did the Ombudsman communicate?
	•	Was the matter closed, referred or otherwise dealt with?
Human consequences
The complaint may help establish the administrative burden experienced by the author and the steps taken to obtain independent review.
It does not, without further evidence, establish that the underlying complaint was substantiated.
The chronology must preserve both the author’s original complaint and the Ombudsman’s actual response.
6. 18 November 2021 — IBAC public-interest disclosure decision
Official record identified
The archive identifies an IBAC document dated 18 November 2021, reference 400008R, concerning the treatment of a public-interest disclosure.
The original document must establish the precise nature of the decision.
The report must not use the words rejected, invalid, unsubstantiated or false interchangeably.
These words have different meanings.
A decision that a disclosure does not meet a statutory allocation or jurisdictional threshold is not necessarily a finding that the underlying allegations are false.
Questions for the record
	•	What disclosure was assessed?
	•	What statutory test was applied?
	•	What material was considered?
	•	What reasons were given?
	•	Was the decision about allocation, jurisdiction or the merits?
	•	Was another body identified as the appropriate recipient?
	•	What review rights or further avenues were identified?
Causal significance
The investigation must determine whether the decision affected any later process.
It must not assume that later events were consequences of this decision simply because they occurred afterwards.

	•	What issue was before Comcare or the relevant review body?
	•	What was the procedural status?
	•	What information was requested?
	•	What opportunity did the author have to respond?
	•	Were any directions or deadlines imposed?

A pre-hearing letter cannot be treated as the final determination of the claim.
8. 14 April 2022 — PM&C FOI refusal, FOI/2022/045
Official record identified
The archive identifies a Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet freedom-of-information decision dated 14 April 2022, reference FOI/2022/045.
The record is relevant to the author’s efforts to obtain government information.
The investigation must distinguish:
	•	Documents requested.
	•	Documents identified.
	•	Documents considered within scope.
	•	Documents exempted.
	•	Documents withheld.
	•	Reasons for refusal.
	•	Review rights.
	•	Subsequent changes to the decision.
The 1,187 Outlook items
The author has identified a separate figure of 1,187 Outlook items in connection with the FOI and information-access history.
This figure must not be represented as 1,187 responsive documents, 1,187 withheld records or 1,187 proven instances of misconduct.

It may refer to search results, candidate email items, mailbox entries or another administrative count.
The final report must identify the underlying dataset and its scope.
Human consequences
The relevant detriment questions include:
	•	How long did the information-access process take?
	•	Did the author incur additional expenses?
	•	Did the absence of information affect another proceeding?
	•	Did the author have access to an effective review process?
	•	Were documents later released?
	•	Did later information materially change the author’s understanding of the matter?
These questions require the original FOI decision, review correspondence and released documents.
