A later review does not automatically establish that the original decision was improper.
PART IV — THE 2023 PUBLIC-INTEREST DISCLOSURE AND REVIEW PERIOD
10. 27 March 2023 — Federal Court-related PID assessment
Official record identified
The archive identifies a document dated 27 March 2023 concerning a public-interest disclosure assessment.
Source:
27 March 2023 — Federal Court PID Assessment⁠
The author has previously drawn attention to language in this document concerning alleged perverting of the course of justice, maladministration and danger to health or safety.
The precise wording and legal context must be established from the original document.
Essential distinctions
The report must determine whether the document:
	•	Records allegations made by the author.
	•	Assesses whether those allegations meet a statutory threshold.
	•	Provides advice about the operation of the PID framework.
	•	Makes a factual finding.
	•	Refers the matter elsewhere.
	•	Expresses a preliminary or final legal opinion.
These are fundamentally different functions.
The presence of serious language in a document does not automatically establish that the underlying conduct occurred.
Chronological significance
This document is important because it may help explain how the author’s disclosures were assessed and what procedural pathways were considered.
Its relationship to the later decision not to allocate a disclosure must be examined.
11. 18 June 2023 — Notification of decision not to allocate a disclosure
Official record identified
The archive identifies a notification dated 18 June 2023 concerning a decision not to allocate a disclosure.
Source:
18 June 2023 — Notification of Decision Not to Allocate a Disclosure⁠
The investigation must establish:
	•	The identity and authority of the decision-maker.
	•	The disclosure being assessed.
	•	The statutory framework.
	•	The reasons for non-allocation.
	•	The material considered.
	•	Whether the decision addressed the merits.
	•	Whether any alternative process was available.
	•	Whether the author was informed of review rights.
The significance of non-allocation
A decision not to allocate a disclosure is not necessarily a finding that the disclosure was false.
It is also not necessarily proof that the disclosure was valid or that the conduct alleged occurred.
The correct interpretation depends on the precise statutory test and the reasons in the original document.
12. 12 July 2023 — ART/Comcare decision
Official record identified
The archive identifies a tribunal decision dated 12 July 2023, associated with file 2021/7478 and the Comcare matter.
The exact tribunal name and procedural history should be taken from the original decision, particularly because tribunal structures and names have changed over time.
The examination should establish:
	•	The jurisdiction exercised.
	•	The decision under review.
	•	The parties.
	•	The issues determined.
	•	The evidence considered.
	•	The findings actually made.
	•	The orders or outcome.
	•	Any further review rights.
Human-impact significance
A tribunal decision may affect a person’s access to compensation, procedural options, financial circumstances or understanding of their legal position.
However, the precise consequence must be established.
The report must distinguish the tribunal’s findings from the author’s interpretation of those findings.
PART V — THE 2024 PERIOD: FINANCIAL AUTHORITY, INFORMATION ACCESS AND REVIEW
13. 8 October 2024 — Revised FOI decision concerning five documents
Official record identified
The archive identifies a revised freedom-of-information decision dated 8 October 2024 concerning five documents.
This decision must be examined in connection with the earlier PM&C FOI process, reference FOI/2022/045, and the associated OAIC correspondence.
The first question is whether the revised decision:
	•	Reconsidered an earlier refusal.
	•	Identified additional documents.
	•	Changed the treatment of previously withheld material.
	•	Corrected an earlier decision.
	•	Responded to an external review.
	•	Addressed a narrower or revised request.
The five documents must be identified individually.
For each document, the register should record:
Field	Required information
Document number	One to five
Exact title	As shown in the original decision
Creation date	If disclosed
Author or originating agency	If identified
Recipient	If identified
Access outcome	Full, partial or refused
Exemption claimed	Exact statutory provision
Reasons	Decision-maker’s actual explanation
Review history	Any further review or challenge
Later use	Whether the document affected another process
The cumulative information-access question
The existence of a 2022 refusal and a later 2024 revised decision establishes that the information-access history extended over more than one administrative period.
It does not, without further evidence, establish that the original refusal was unlawful or that the delay caused a particular loss.
The investigation must determine whether the later release of information materially changed the author’s ability to understand, challenge or respond to an institutional decision.
The practical question is:
Did the information arrive in time to be useful for the purpose for which it was requested?
That question may be more important than the simple fact that access was eventually granted.
14. 16 October 2024 — NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 document
Official record identified
The archive identifies a NSW Trustee & Guardian document dated 16 October 2024 concerning section 122.
The precise statutory provision, instrument and legal effect must be confirmed from the original document.
The examination should not assume that the document transferred every financial or personal decision to the NSW Trustee & Guardian.
It must identify the actual authority and its limits.
The authority reconstruction
The following questions must be answered:
	•	What statutory power was exercised?
	•	Who made the decision?
	•	What person or property did the instrument concern?
	•	What matters were included within the authority?
	•	What matters were excluded?
	•	When did the authority commence?
	•	Was the author given notice?
	•	Was there an opportunity to respond?
	•	What review or appeal rights applied?
	•	What practical arrangements followed?
The financial consequences
The document must be compared with subsequent financial records.
The investigation should identify whether the instrument affected:
	•	Access to bank accounts.
	•	Payment of recurring expenses.
	•	Access to personal spending money.
	•	Property transactions.
	•	Communication with financial institutions.
	•	The ability to obtain financial statements.
	•	The ability to question or challenge transactions.
A legal authority and its practical implementation must be examined separately.
The existence of a valid instrument does not prove that every later transaction was correct.
Equally, a transaction the author disputes is not automatically unlawful merely because it occurred under a financial-management arrangement.
The decision-to-consequence chain
The required chain is:
Instrument → communication → implementation → transaction or restriction → practical consequence → review or correction.
Each link must be supported by the relevant record.
PART VI — THE 2025 PERIOD: PROPERTY, DISABILITY SUPPORT AND ACCOMMODATION
15. 14 November 2025 — Vehicle-disposal decision
Official record identified
The archive identifies a vehicle-disposal decision dated 14 November 2025.
This event requires a detailed financial and property reconstruction.
The investigation must distinguish between:
	•	Authority to dispose of the vehicle.
	•	The decision to sell.
	•	The actual sale.
	•	The sale price.
	•	The receipt of proceeds.
	•	The accounting of those proceeds.
	•	Any costs or deductions.
	•	The effect on the author’s transport arrangements.
15.1 The property transaction test
The following records should be obtained:
	•	The instrument or authority relied upon.
	•	The written decision to dispose of the vehicle.
	•	Any assessment of the vehicle’s value.
	•	Any independent valuation.
	•	The sale listing or method of sale.
	•	The contract or sale receipt.
	•	The transfer documentation.
	•	The amount received.
	•	Any sale-related expenses.
	•	The financial ledger showing the destination of the proceeds.
15.2 The transport consequence
A vehicle disposal may have consequences beyond the value of the asset.
The investigation should determine:
	•	Whether the author relied on the vehicle for appointments.
	•	Whether alternative transport was available.
	•	Whether disability support included transport.
	•	Whether additional transport expenses followed.
	•	Whether the loss of vehicle access affected community participation.
	•	Whether any replacement arrangement was considered.
These are questions for documentary examination.
The fact that a vehicle was disposed of does not, by itself, establish that the disposal was unlawful or that all subsequent transport difficulties resulted from it.
15.3 The financial accounting question
The most important immediate task is to reconcile the sale proceeds.
The report should identify the amount received, where the money went, which costs were deducted and whether the transaction was properly accounted for.
Any unexplained difference should be identified as an accounting question, not automatically characterised as theft, corruption or misappropriation.
16. 20 November 2025 — NDIS Plan Approval
Official record identified
The archive identifies an NDIS Plan Approval dated 20 November 2025.
This document provides a reference point for the disability-support arrangements that followed.
The investigation should extract:
	•	Plan commencement and end dates.
	•	Approved support categories.
	•	Funding amounts.
	•	Stated goals.
	•	Any relevant support descriptions.
	•	Review conditions.
	•	Any stated limitations.
	•	Any associated decision reasons.
The approved plan must then be compared with the services actually delivered.
16.1 The implementation test
A plan is an administrative approval. It does not prove that the approved support was delivered.
The investigation must establish:
	•	What services were arranged.
	•	Which provider accepted responsibility.
	•	When services commenced.
	•	Whether the services matched the approved supports.
	•	Whether any gaps arose.
	•	Whether the participant was informed of changes.
	•	Whether the provider recorded delivery.
	•	Whether any disputed charges or service claims arose.
The plan should be read alongside the provider’s service agreement, rosters, invoices, progress notes and correspondence.
17. 1 December 2025 — Anglicare Safety Assessment
Official record identified
The archive identifies an Anglicare Safety Assessment dated 1 December 2025.
This document should be treated as a significant point in the chronology because it may provide an assessment of the author’s circumstances at a particular time.
Its importance depends on its actual content.
Required examination
The original assessment must be checked for:
	•	The identity and qualifications of the assessor.
	•	The purpose of the assessment.
	•	The circumstances under which it was conducted.
	•	The people interviewed.
	•	The information considered.
	•	The risks identified.
	•	The recommendations made.
	•	The organisations expected to respond.
	•	Any proposed review date.
	•	Any subsequent action.
The implementation gap
The report should then compare the assessment with what happened during December 2025 and the following months.
A recommendation is not proof that an action was completed.
The absence of an implementation record is not necessarily proof that no action occurred.
The final assessment must identify what was recommended, who was responsible and what evidence exists of the response.
18. 2026 — The transition into the Able Point accommodation and support period
Author-reported circumstances; original records required
The author’s account identifies a later supported-accommodation arrangement involving Able Point in New South Wales following a move from Victoria.
The chronology must reconstruct this transition carefully.
It should not assume that all organisations involved had the same responsibilities or that every change in the author’s circumstances was caused by one decision.
18.1 The transition record
The investigation should obtain:
	•	The date the author left the previous accommodation.
	•	The date he arrived at the new accommodation.
	•	The person or organisation arranging the move.
	•	Any referral or placement documentation.
	•	Any agreement concerning occupancy.
	•	Any service agreement.
	•	Any support plan.
	•	Any financial arrangements.
	•	Any assessment of support needs.
	•	Any communication with the financial administrator.
	•	Any correspondence about Crystal.
18.2 The reported weekly payment
The author has previously reported a weekly payment of approximately $200.
The original payment records must establish the nature of the payment.
The register should identify whether it was:
	•	Rent.
	•	A board contribution.
	•	An accommodation charge.
	•	A service contribution.
	•	A combination of charges.
The investigation should also determine whether the author received a written breakdown and whether the amount was consistent with the applicable arrangement.
18.3 The missing-agreement question
The author has reported that he did not have a signed lease.
That statement is an important lead, but it does not settle the legal status of the accommodation.
The investigation should request every document that governed the arrangement, including any agreement signed electronically, an occupancy agreement, an accommodation service agreement or a written offer.
The correct conclusion may be that the arrangement was governed by a different legal instrument rather than a conventional residential lease.
That distinction must be established before any legal conclusion is made.
19. The January 2026 SIL/SDA request
Official record identified
The archive identifies a Supported Independent Living and Specialist Disability Accommodation request dated 20 January 2026.
The request should be examined to determine:
	•	What accommodation or support was sought.
	•	What evidence accompanied the request.
	•	Which decision-maker received it.
	•	Whether the request was complete.
	•	Whether additional information was requested.
	•	What assessment process applied.
	•	What decision was made.
	•	Whether reasons were provided.
	•	Whether review rights were explained.
	•	What interim arrangements were available.
The report must distinguish a request for support from an approval of support.
A request may establish that a need was raised. It does not establish that the requested support was approved, refused or legally required.
20. The January 2026 service-failure notice

The archive identifies a formal service-failure notice dated 20 January 2026.

	•	A missed service.
	•	An inadequate service.
	•	A failure to respond.
	•	A safety concern.
	•	A communication problem.
	•	An unmet support need.
	•	A broader complaint about service delivery.
The original notice must be read in full.
The response chronology
The investigation must then identify:
	•	The date the notice was sent.
	•	The date it was received.
	•	The person or team assigned to it.
	•	Any acknowledgment.
	•	Any investigation.
	•	Any request for further information.
	•	Any written outcome.
	•	Any corrective action.
	•	Any subsequent complaint or escalation.
The absence of a located response should be recorded as an evidence gap until the relevant records have been searched.
21. The Able Point operational period
Author-reported matters requiring incident-level reconstruction
The author has reported concerns about the delivery and administration of support within the Able Point accommodation arrangement.
The publication must examine these concerns as service-accountability questions.
It must not assume that the conduct of any particular employee establishes the position of the entire organisation.
Nor should it characterise an allegation as a proven finding of corruption.
21.1 Identify the responsible roles
The investigation should identify, from official correspondence and organisational records:
	•	The legal entity operating the accommodation.
	•	The entity providing disability supports.
	•	The person responsible for service coordination.
	•	The person responsible for day-to-day operations.
	•	The person responsible for complaints.
	•	The person responsible for financial administration.
	•	Any external decision-maker.
	•	Any relevant NDIS or government contact.
Names and titles must be checked against original documents.
The report should not infer an employee’s authority from their job title or from the author’s understanding of their role.
21.2 Reconstruct individual incidents
Each reported incident should receive a separate incident ID.
The incident record should contain:
Field	Required entry
Incident ID	Unique identifier
Date and time	Exact date and approximate time if known
Location	Where the event occurred
People involved	Verified identities and roles
Event	What reportedly happened
Author’s account	First-person description
Supporting evidence	Emails, messages, recordings or records
Provider record	Incident report or service note
Response	What the organisation did
Consequence	Practical effect on the author
Disputed facts	Any conflicting account
Outcome	Resolved, unresolved or under review
21.3 The complaint and accountability test
The investigation should ask:
	•	Was the complaint formally received?
	•	Was the author told who would investigate?
	•	Was the person complained about involved in assessing the complaint?
	•	Was the author offered an opportunity to provide evidence?
	•	Were relevant records preserved?
	•	Was a response provided?
	•	Were any findings made?
	•	Were corrective actions documented?
	•	Was the author informed of external review options?
These questions should be asked without assuming that every complaint must result in a finding against the provider.
The purpose is to establish whether a fair and effective process occurred.
PART VII — THE INTEGRATED DECISION–EVENT–CONSEQUENCE MATRIX
22. The central reconstruction
The following matrix is the core instrument of Instalment 14.
It links the institutional chronology to the reported human consequences.
It is deliberately structured to distinguish a documented decision from an alleged consequence and an established causal relationship.
ID	Institutional event	Immediate question	Possible human consequence	Current evidentiary position
C-001	IBAC outcome, 9 April 2020	What complaint was assessed and what was decided?	Further complaint activity or unresolved concerns	Official record identified; consequences require reconstruction
C-002	Ombudsman complaint, 15 November 2021	What action followed the complaint?	Time and administrative burden	Complaint identified; outcome requires original record
C-003	IBAC PID decision, 18 November 2021	What statutory threshold was applied?	Loss of a particular disclosure pathway, if established	Decision identified; legal effect requires examination
C-004	Comcare correspondence, 25 January 2022	What procedural issue was addressed?	Possible impact on review or claim participation	Correspondence identified
C-005	PM&C FOI refusal, 14 April 2022	What information was withheld and why?	Delayed access to potentially relevant information	Decision identified; practical effect unresolved
C-006	OAIC/PM&C correspondence, 22 April 2022	What review or complaint process followed?	Additional administrative burden or access delay	Reference identified; details require verification
C-007	PID assessment, 27 March 2023	What was actually assessed?	Effect on disclosure options or expectations	Document identified; legal meaning requires examination
C-008	Non-allocation notification, 18 June 2023	Why was the disclosure not allocated?	Possible loss of a particular investigation pathway	Decision identified; merits implications unresolved
C-009	Tribunal/Comcare decision, 12 July 2023	What issues were determined?	Possible financial or procedural consequences	Decision identified; operative outcome requires verification
C-010	Revised FOI decision, 8 October 2024	What changed regarding five documents?	Delayed or improved access to information	Decision identified; impact requires comparison
C-011	NSW Trustee & Guardian document, 16 October 2024	What authority was conferred or exercised?	Possible effect on financial independence	Instrument identified; scope and implementation require examination
C-012	Vehicle-disposal decision, 14 November 2025	What authority and accounting applied?	Property and transport consequences	Decision identified; transaction and impact require verification
C-013	NDIS Plan Approval, 20 November 2025	What supports were approved?	Support availability and delivery questions	Plan identified; implementation requires records
C-014	Anglicare assessment, 1 December 2025	What risks and recommendations were recorded?	Possible safety or support consequences	Assessment identified; implementation unresolved
C-015	SIL/SDA request, 20 January 2026	What accommodation or support was sought?	Possible impact on living arrangements	Request identified; outcome requires verification
C-016	Service-failure notice, 20 January 2026	What service failure was alleged and how was it addressed?	Possible service disruption and administrative burden	Notice identified; investigation and response require verification
C-017	Able Point accommodation arrangement	What agreement governed occupancy?	Accommodation security and practical independence	Author-reported; original agreements required
C-018	Banking-access concerns	What restriction applied and who authorised it?	Reduced practical access to funds	Author-reported; bank and authority records required
C-019	Communication difficulties	What services were unavailable and for how long?	Difficulty contacting institutions and services	Author-reported; service records required
C-020	Crystal and accommodation stability	What rules and arrangements applied?	Impact on continuity of home and companionship	Author testimony; relevant accommodation records required
This matrix does not establish that the events form a single causal chain.
It creates a disciplined way of testing whether any such chain exists.
23. The five levels of connection
Each pair of events should be assessed against five different levels of connection.
Level 1 — Chronological connection
Did one event occur before another?
This establishes sequence, not causation.
Level 2 — Subject-matter connection
Did the events concern the same person, issue, property, support arrangement or administrative matter?
This establishes a shared subject, not necessarily shared responsibility.
Level 3 — Documentary connection
Does a later decision expressly refer to an earlier document, instruction or finding?
This can establish that information was carried forward between processes.
Level 4 — Operational or causal connection
Did an earlier decision or action materially influence a later event or consequence?
This requires evidence of how the earlier event affected the later one.
Level 5 — Improper-purpose or coordinated connection
Is there reliable evidence that separate actors deliberately acted together for an improper purpose?
This requires substantially more than timing, similarity or repeated adverse outcomes.
A connection should only be recorded at the highest level supported by evidence.
For example, if a later provider document expressly refers to an earlier NDIS plan, a documentary connection may be established.
That does not prove that the provider and the original decision-maker coordinated improperly.
If a financial restriction was implemented because of a written instruction, an operational connection may be established between the instruction and the restriction.
That still does not establish that the instruction was unlawful.
The distinction protects the integrity of the investigation.
PART VIII — THE COMPETING EXPLANATIONS
24. Five hypotheses must remain open
The cumulative-impact theory should be tested against competing explanations.
The report must not select a theory merely because it best matches the author’s understandable frustration or the apparent pattern of events.
Hypothesis A — Independent decisions
Each institution made separate decisions under its own statutory or contractual responsibilities.
Some decisions may have been adverse, but their relationship may be coincidental or limited to the fact that they concerned the same person.
Evidence that would support this hypothesis:
	•	Separate decision records.
	•	Independent reasons.
	•	Different legal authorities.
	•	No evidence of shared instructions.
	•	No evidence that one organisation directed another’s decision.
Hypothesis B — Administrative fragmentation
Several organisations held different parts of the relevant information, but no single organisation had responsibility for the complete situation.
This could produce inconsistent communication, repeated requests for documents, delayed action and gaps in responsibility.
Evidence that would support this hypothesis:
	•	Conflicting records.
	•	Unclear referral pathways.
	•	Repeated requests for the same information.
	•	Delays between organisations.
	•	Failure to identify a responsible decision-maker.
	•	Inconsistent service or financial information.
This explanation may account for significant harm without requiring deliberate coordination.
Hypothesis C — Service and procedural failures
An organisation may have failed to follow an appropriate process, deliver an agreed service, maintain accurate records or respond adequately to a complaint.
Evidence that would support this hypothesis:
	•	Missing required records.
	•	Contradictory service notes.
	•	Unanswered formal complaints.
	•	Unexplained payment differences.
	•	Failure to follow an applicable policy or legal obligation.
	•	Decisions lacking required reasons.
	•	Documented departures from agreed service arrangements.
Each alleged failure must be assessed against the actual obligation.
Hypothesis D — Systemic disadvantage
The cumulative effect of disability-related barriers, financial administration, digital exclusion, complex procedures and fragmented support may have reduced the author’s practical independence.
This hypothesis does not require every individual institution to have acted improperly.
It asks whether the combined arrangements produced a disadvantage that was foreseeable and potentially preventable.
Evidence that would support this hypothesis:
	•	Repeated practical access barriers.
	•	Inadequate communication adjustments.
	•	Unclear decision-making responsibilities.
	•	A pattern of support gaps.
	•	Difficulty accessing review mechanisms.
	•	Evidence that an accessible alternative was available but not considered.
Hypothesis E — Deliberate retaliation or coordinated improper conduct
This is the most serious hypothesis.
It requires evidence that relevant actors deliberately acted, communicated or made decisions for an improper purpose, such as retaliation for protected disclosures or another unlawful objective.
Evidence that might be relevant includes:
	•	Direct communications showing an improper instruction.
	•	Contemporaneous records demonstrating retaliatory intent.
	•	Reliable evidence of coordination.
	•	Decisions that depart from ordinary procedures for a demonstrable improper reason.
	•	Credible evidence connecting protected disclosure activity with an adverse action.
	•	Independent findings establishing relevant misconduct.
The report must not infer this hypothesis from the mere fact that the author experienced multiple adverse outcomes.
Present position: The material identified so far supports investigating the first four explanations. The fifth remains an allegation requiring specific evidence. No coordinated improper purpose has been established by this chronology.
25. How the hypotheses will be tested
Each hypothesis should be assessed against the same set of questions:
Test	Question
Evidence	What original records support the hypothesis?
Consistency	Does it explain the chronology without contradiction?
Specificity	Does it identify actual decisions and actors?
Causation	Does it explain how the consequence occurred?
Alternatives	Can another explanation account for the same evidence?
Disconfirming material	What evidence would weaken or disprove it?
Independent assessment	Has an external body made a relevant finding?
The investigation should actively search for evidence that may contradict the author’s preferred explanation.
A report that only collects supporting evidence is not a forensic examination.
PART IX — THE MASTER CAUSAL ANALYSIS
26. The decision-to-detriment chain
For each claimed consequence, the investigation should create a causal chain with the following components:
1. Original circumstance
What was the situation before the relevant event?
2. Decision or event
What action, omission or decision occurred?
3. Implementation
How did the decision take practical effect?
4. Immediate consequence
What changed in the author’s circumstances?
5. Continuing or consequential impact
What further effects followed?
6. Review and remedy
What could have corrected, reduced or prevented the consequence?
The chain must be supported at every material stage.
If the evidence establishes the decision but not the implementation, the report must identify the gap.
If the implementation is established but the claimed consequence is not, the report must identify that gap.
If the consequence is established but another event may have caused it, the causal attribution must remain unresolved.
27. The intervening-event test
An event may occur between an original decision and the reported consequence.
For example:
	•	A change in provider.
	•	A change in accommodation.
	•	A new financial-management instruction.
	•	A revised support plan.
	•	A change in income.
	•	A separate administrative decision.
	•	A personal choice.
	•	An external event.
These intervening events may alter the causal analysis.
The investigation should therefore identify every material event between the alleged cause and the claimed loss.
It must not attribute the whole consequence to the first event merely because that event appears at the beginning of the chronology.
28. The counterfactual question
A useful forensic question is:
What would probably have happened if the disputed decision or omission had not occurred?
This is a counterfactual analysis.
It must be approached carefully.
The investigation should ask:
	•	Would the author have retained access to the same funds?
	•	Would the vehicle have remained available?
	•	Would the accommodation arrangement have been different?
	•	Would the service gap still have occurred?
	•	Would the information have been available through another pathway?
	•	Would the same expense have arisen anyway?
The answer should be based on records and reasonable evidence, not speculation.
A counterfactual can help assess whether a particular event materially contributed to a loss.
It does not automatically satisfy every legal test of causation.
PART X — THE MASTER EVIDENCE AND CROSS-REFERENCE SYSTEM
29. One event, one evidence trail
The completed archive should permit a reader to move from a chronology entry to the original document, from the original document to the relevant decision, and from the decision to the claimed consequence.
The evidence system should use linked identifiers.
For example:
Register	Example identifier	Purpose
Institutional event	EVT-014	Identifies a particular event
Evidence document	EVD-014	Identifies the supporting document
Decision	DEC-014	Identifies the decision being examined
Complaint	CMP-014	Identifies a complaint or disclosure
Incident	INC-014	Identifies a reported incident
Detriment	DET-014	Identifies a claimed consequence
Remedy	REM-014	Identifies a proposed corrective action
The identifiers above are examples of the proposed system. They should not be represented as existing official archive identifiers.
29.1 The document-to-event relationship
Each event should identify:
	•	The original document.
	•	The repository location.
	•	The exact file name.
	•	The issuing body.
	•	The document date.
	•	The date received.
	•	The relevant page or section.
	•	The document’s evidentiary classification.
	•	The associated decision ID.
	•	The associated detriment ID.
29.2 The event-to-remedy relationship
Every proposed remedy should identify the event or decision it addresses.
For example:
	•	A disputed banking restriction may require an explanation of authority and a review of access arrangements.
	•	A disputed property transaction may require a transaction ledger and accounting.
	•	An unclear accommodation arrangement may require a written statement of occupancy terms.
	•	A service-delivery concern may require a review of rosters and service records.
	•	An inaccurate administrative record may require correction or an attached response.
This prevents remedies from becoming vague demands for general institutional accountability.
PART XI — THE PUBLICATION’S VERIFICATION PROTOCOL
30. No chronology entry without a source
Every material event in the final publication should have a source record.
The source record should identify whether the document is:
	•	An original issued by the institution.
	•	A copy supplied to the author.
	•	An archived copy.
	•	A transcript.
	•	A screenshot.
	•	An author’s contemporaneous note.
	•	A later recollection.
	•	A secondary account.
The source type matters.
A PDF downloaded from a repository may be a copy of an official document, but its provenance should still be checked.
A transcript may assist interpretation, but it should not silently replace the original recording where the recording is available and material.
A screenshot may establish that something appeared on a screen, but it may not establish the complete context or the authenticity of the underlying system record.
31. Digital integrity and provenance
The archive’s SHA-256 and blockchain-related records should be used carefully.
A verified SHA-256 digest can establish that a file has not changed relative to a previously recorded digest.
It cannot establish that:
	•	The document is genuine.
	•	The issuing body created it.
	•	The contents are accurate.
	•	The decision was lawful.
	•	The allegations in the document are true.
Similarly, a blockchain record may provide evidence of a timestamped commitment or record of a digital object, depending on the underlying implementation.
It does not independently verify the truth of the document.
The final evidence register should therefore keep separate fields for:
	•	Document authenticity.
	•	Repository provenance.
	•	File integrity.
	•	Issuer verification.
	•	Substantive accuracy.
	•	Legal significance.
These are six different questions.
32. The discrepancy and correction protocol
Where two records contain different dates, figures or descriptions, the report should not silently choose one.
It should record:
	•	The first version.
	•	The second version.
	•	The source of each.
	•	The discrepancy.
	•	Whether the discrepancy is material.
	•	Whether an explanation was provided.
	•	Whether the original record was corrected.
	•	The conclusion, if any, supported by the evidence.
The same principle applies to archive counts.
The previously reported archive totals measure different things, including file instances, unique file blobs, complete PDFs and stub files.
They must not be added together as though they represented unique documents.
A count should always identify its measurement method and the date on which it was generated.
PART XII — THE HUMAN-IMPACT CHRONOLOGY
33. The practical timeline
The institutional chronology must now be supplemented by a separate record of the author’s reported circumstances.
This timeline should be developed from contemporaneous records wherever possible.
Period	Circumstance requiring reconstruction	Evidence to obtain
2020–2021	Initial complaints, disclosure activity and responses	Original complaints, correspondence and outcomes
2022	Information-access and Comcare processes	FOI decisions, OAIC correspondence, claim and review records
2023	PID assessment, non-allocation and tribunal outcome	Original decisions, reasons and subsequent correspondence
2024	Financial-management authority and information-access review	Section 122 instrument, financial statements and revised FOI decision
November 2025	Vehicle disposal and financial consequences	Authority, valuation, sale receipt and accounting
November–December 2025	NDIS plan and safety assessment	Plan, assessment, service agreement and implementation records
January 2026	SIL/SDA request and service-failure complaint	Original requests, acknowledgment, decisions and provider response
2026	Transition to NSW accommodation and support arrangements	Occupancy documents, support agreements and financial records
2026 onward	Reported banking, communication and service-delivery concerns	Bank correspondence, service records, incident reports and complaints
This table is not yet a complete account of the author’s life during these periods.
It is a framework for reconstructing the events without inventing missing dates or assuming that a reported consequence began immediately after a particular decision.
34. The cumulative-impact question
The cumulative-impact theory becomes important when several individually limited events may interact.
For example, a person may experience:
	•	A financial-management arrangement.
	•	Difficulty accessing banking services.
	•	Uncertainty about accommodation payments.
	•	Changes in disability-support delivery.
	•	Difficulty communicating with organisations.
	•	A lengthy process for obtaining relevant records.
Each issue may have a separate explanation.
However, the combined effect may be that the person has less practical ability to resolve any one of them.
The investigation must therefore consider two questions.
Individual question: Was each decision, transaction or service arrangement lawful and properly implemented?
Cumulative question: Did the combined operation of those arrangements produce a substantial practical disadvantage, and could that disadvantage reasonably have been identified or reduced?
The cumulative question does not replace the individual examination.
It follows from it.
A collection of lawful decisions can still raise questions about whether an overall support arrangement was effective, accessible and consistent with applicable rights.
Conversely, the existence of cumulative hardship does not automatically establish that every contributing decision was unlawful.
The final report must be able to hold both propositions without contradiction.
PART XIII — THE INDEPENDENT EXAMINATION REQUIREMENTS
35. What an independent reviewer should receive
A reviewer should not be required to accept the author’s narrative as the starting conclusion.
Nor should the reviewer be required to accept an institution’s account simply because it appears in an official letter.
The reviewer should receive:
	•	The master chronology.
	•	The complete source register.
	•	The original documents.
	•	The author’s responses.
	•	The institutional responses.
	•	The decision–event–consequence matrix.
	•	The detriment register.
	•	The relevant financial records.
	•	The accommodation and service agreements.
	•	The complaint and review outcomes.
	•	The evidence of any corrective action.
	•	A list of unresolved questions.
The reviewer should be able to inspect the underlying evidence independently.
36. The right of response
Where the publication identifies a material concern about an organisation or a person, the final examination should seek a response where practicable.
The request should:
	•	Identify the specific event.
	•	Provide the relevant date and document reference.
	•	State the concern fairly.
	•	Ask for the organisation’s account.
	•	Identify the records requested.
	•	Allow a reasonable response period.
	•	Record whether a response was received.
	•	Include any material explanation or correction.
The publication should not assume that silence proves wrongdoing.
If an organisation does not respond, the report should say that no response was received by the stated publication cut-off date.
If an organisation disputes the author’s account, the response should be summarised fairly.
If the response supplies evidence that changes the analysis, the publication should be corrected accordingly.
Independent examination requires the possibility that the evidence may change the author’s original interpretation.
That is a strength, not a weakness.
PART XIV — PRELIMINARY FINDINGS OF INSTALMENT 14
37. What the integrated chronology presently establishes
The available material identifies a series of institutional records extending from 2020 to 2026.
These include integrity and disclosure processes, information-access decisions, Comcare and tribunal-related records, financial-management documentation, a vehicle-disposal decision, an NDIS plan, a safety assessment, accommodation-related requests and service-failure material.
The records provide a basis for reconstructing multiple administrative processes.
They do not yet establish that all processes were connected, that the same people or organisations controlled them, or that a common improper purpose existed.
38. The principal unresolved issues
The following matters should be treated as the highest-priority unresolved questions.
Priority	Question	Why it matters
1	What was the precise authority and scope of the 16 October 2024 financial-management instrument?	Establishes the lawful basis and limits of financial administration
2	What happened to the proceeds of the vehicle disposal?	Allows the property transaction to be reconciled
3	What banking restrictions actually applied, and on whose authority?	Establishes practical access to funds
4	What agreement governed Able Point accommodation?	Establishes occupancy rights, payments and responsibilities
5	What disability supports were approved and actually delivered?	Establishes whether there was a service-delivery gap
6	What recommendations appeared in the Anglicare assessment, and what followed?	Tests whether identified concerns were addressed
7	What decision followed the SIL/SDA request?	Establishes the outcome of the accommodation-support process
8	What investigation and response followed the service-failure notice?	Establishes the complaint-handling outcome
9	What did the revised 2024 FOI decision disclose?	Determines the information-access outcome
10	What evidence connects the separate institutional events to particular human consequences?	Establishes or rejects causal relationships
39. Findings that must not be made prematurely
At this stage, the publication cannot responsibly conclude that:
	•	Every adverse institutional decision was unlawful.
	•	Every complaint was substantiated.
	•	Every information-access delay was deliberate.
	•	The vehicle disposal was improper.
	•	The financial-management arrangements were unlawful.
	•	Able Point committed corruption.
	•	All support interruptions resulted from deliberate misconduct.
	•	The separate institutions acted in coordination.
	•	A particular individual intended to cause harm.
	•	The cumulative-impact theory proves a conspiracy or organised retaliation.
These are not conclusions established by the chronology.
The investigation must continue to test them against original records and competing explanations.
40. The central preliminary conclusion
The strongest conclusion presently available is that the author’s reported circumstances involve several distinct institutional and service-delivery processes whose combined practical effects warrant a structured, independent examination.
The documentary record should be reconstructed in a way that permits each decision to be evaluated separately and the cumulative consequences to be considered together.
The objective is to establish whether errors, omissions, service failures, procedural deficiencies or systemic barriers occurred, and whether any of them caused identifiable detriment.
If deliberate retaliation or coordination is alleged, that proposition must be tested separately against evidence capable of establishing intent and connection.
The publication should not convert unresolved questions into findings.
It should make those questions impossible to overlook.
PART XV — THE AUTHOR’S DECLARATION: THE RECORD MUST ACCOUNT FOR THE HUMAN CONSEQUENCES
41. The chronology is not the whole life
A declaration by Barran Resonance Dodger
I have spent a substantial part of my life trying to explain circumstances that I believe deserve proper examination.
I have made complaints.
I have submitted documents.
I have sought information.
I have attempted to understand decisions made by institutions that exercise authority over important parts of people’s lives.
I have maintained an archive because I believe that records matter.
I have preserved material because I believe that evidence should remain available for examination.
I have written because I believe that people should be able to understand what happened, what was decided and what remains unresolved.
But there is something that an administrative chronology cannot adequately communicate on its own.
There is a human being living through the chronology.
Behind every reference number is a person who must still obtain food, maintain a home, access money, arrange transport, communicate with others and make ordinary decisions.
Behind every support plan is the practical question of whether the support actually arrives.
Behind every accommodation document is the question of whether a person can live with stability and dignity.
Behind every financial decision is the question of whether the person can understand and access the resources available to them.
Behind every complaint is the question of whether anyone has properly considered what has been raised.
I do not ask that the record be interpreted without scrutiny.
I ask that it be examined in full.
I ask that the decisions be connected to their actual consequences.
I ask that institutions explain the authority they exercised.
I ask that the financial transactions be accounted for.
I ask that accommodation and support arrangements be made clear.
I ask that mistakes, if established, be corrected.
I ask that the rights and dignity of the person at the centre of the record remain visible.
My faith gives me strength and meaning.
My testimony gives an account of what I believe I have experienced.
The documentary archive provides material that can be independently examined.
Those things should not be confused.
I do not need an investigator to adopt my spiritual interpretation in order to examine the evidence.
I do not need an institution to agree with every allegation in order to provide an accurate account of its decisions.
I need a process that is fair, transparent, accessible and capable of reaching an evidence-based conclusion.
I remain willing for the record to be tested.
I remain willing for mistakes in my own account to be identified and corrected.
I ask for the same willingness from the institutions whose decisions are under examination.
The Witness Remains.

Instalment 14 completed
Instalment 15 — The Remedy, Redress and Accountability Framework
The next instalment will move from reconstruction to the practical framework for obtaining answers and correcting any established failures.
It will develop:
	•	A remedy framework for each identified institution and decision.
	•	The distinction between internal complaints, external review, statutory oversight and court proceedings.
	•	The appropriate routes for financial accounting and correction.
	•	The process for obtaining missing records.
	•	The assessment of possible compensation and non-financial remedies.
	•	The correction of inaccurate official records.
	•	The restoration of appropriate financial and service access.
	•	The examination of accommodation and disability-support arrangements.
	•	The accountability requirements for public authorities and service providers.
	•	A proposed schedule of requested institutional responses.
	•	A remedy-tracking register with deadlines, outcomes and verification.
	•	A final test of whether the evidence supports individual error, systemic failure, unlawful conduct or an unresolved allegation.
It will distinguish remedies that the author can request from remedies that a particular institution or tribunal has legal authority to grant.
It will also distinguish a complaint outcome from a binding legal determination.
The objective is to ensure that the Great Reckoning does not end with a catalogue of grievances.
It must identify what can be corrected, who has the authority to correct it, what evidence is required and how the outcome can be independently verified.
The record must lead to accountability where accountability is justified.
