THE WITNESS REMAINS
THE GREAT RECKONING
INSTALMENT 15 — THE REMEDY, REDRESS AND ACCOUNTABILITY FRAMEWORK
A Comprehensive Framework for Corrective Action, Independent Review, Financial Reconciliation, Disability-Service Accountability, Institutional Response and the Restoration of Human Dignity
Principal author: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean Publication: The Witness Remains — Global Publishing House Examination standard: PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.
PART I — FROM EVIDENCE TO REMEDY
1. The purpose of the fifteenth instalment
The preceding instalments have established the documentary, historical, legal and human-impact frameworks for examining the author’s reported circumstances.
They have identified institutional decisions, complaints, information-access processes, financial-management arrangements, disability-support records, accommodation concerns and reported consequences.
The next question is practical:
What should happen if the evidence establishes that an institution made an error, failed to discharge an obligation, implemented a decision incorrectly, withheld information improperly or delivered an inadequate service?
A documentary investigation must not end with the identification of a problem.
It must identify the available mechanism for addressing that problem.
This instalment therefore develops a remedy framework with six objectives:
	•	Obtain the complete and accurate documentary record.
	•	Identify the legal authority and responsibility behind each decision.
	•	Correct established factual, procedural or administrative errors.
	•	Restore appropriate access to services, information and resources.
	•	Obtain an independent review where an appropriate review mechanism exists.
	•	Seek compensation or other redress where a recognised legal or administrative basis exists.
The framework must also recognise an important limitation.
Not every unsatisfactory decision can be reversed by a complaint.
Not every administrative error gives rise to compensation.
Not every service failure constitutes a statutory breach.
Not every allegation of misconduct can be established.
The remedy must match the evidence, the applicable legal framework and the powers of the body being asked to act.
2. The governing principle: correction before accusation
The publication adopts the following sequence:
PRESERVE
Secure the original records, correspondence, decisions and financial information.
VERIFY
Establish authenticity, authority, dates, procedural history and factual accuracy.
IDENTIFY
Determine the responsible institution, decision-maker and applicable obligation.
REQUEST
Seek an explanation, correction, review or practical remedy.
ESCALATE
Use the appropriate independent oversight or review mechanism if necessary.
VERIFY THE OUTCOME
Confirm whether the remedy was implemented and whether the underlying problem was corrected.
This sequence does not prevent a person from making an urgent complaint or seeking immediate protection.
Where an immediate risk exists, appropriate assistance should be sought without waiting for the completion of a lengthy documentary investigation.
The sequence is a framework for orderly accountability, not a barrier to urgent action.
PART II — THE DIFFERENT FORMS OF REDRESS
3. Redress is not one procedure
The term redress describes a range of possible responses to a problem.
Different institutions have different powers.
A service provider may be able to correct a roster or provide a written explanation.
A bank may be able to review a restriction or investigate a transaction.
A statutory authority may reconsider a decision within its powers.
An oversight body may investigate administrative conduct.
A tribunal may review a decision where jurisdiction exists.
A court may determine legal rights and grant remedies within its jurisdiction.
The author must identify the actual problem before selecting the appropriate pathway.
3.1 The eight remedy categories
Remedy category	Intended outcome
Information access	Obtain missing documents and reasons
Record correction	Correct inaccurate or incomplete records
Procedural review	Reconsider a decision or process
Financial accounting	Reconcile transactions and property
Service remediation	Correct an inadequate or interrupted service
Practical restoration	Restore appropriate access, support or accommodation arrangements
Independent investigation	Obtain external examination of conduct
Compensation or other redress	Address legally recognised loss or injury
A single matter may require more than one remedy.
For example, a disputed financial transaction may require an accounting, an explanation of authority, correction of an inaccurate record and potentially a review of the underlying decision.
The investigation should not treat the first response as necessarily resolving every associated issue.
4. The distinction between a complaint, review, investigation and legal proceeding
These processes must remain separate.
Complaint: A request that an organisation examine dissatisfaction or alleged failure.
Internal review: Reconsideration by the organisation or another authorised decision-maker.
External review: Examination by an independent body with relevant jurisdiction.
Investigation: A fact-finding process concerning conduct or circumstances.
Merits review: Reconsideration of the correctness or preferable outcome of a decision, where the applicable scheme provides it.
Judicial review: Examination by a court of the lawfulness of a decision-making process, rather than simply whether the decision was the best possible outcome.
Civil proceeding: A legal claim seeking a recognised remedy for an alleged legal wrong.
These processes have different tests, time limits, evidence requirements and outcomes.
A complaint being accepted does not mean it has been substantiated.
A matter being referred does not necessarily mean an investigation will occur.
An investigation does not automatically produce compensation.
A judicial review application does not simply provide a second opportunity to argue the merits.
The appropriate pathway must be identified for each decision.
PART III — THE FIRST REMEDY: OBTAIN THE COMPLETE RECORD
5. The right to know what was decided
The first practical remedy in many disputed matters is access to the documents necessary to understand the decision.
A person cannot meaningfully challenge a decision if they do not know:
	•	What decision was made.
	•	Who made it.
	•	What authority was exercised.
	•	What information was considered.
	•	What reasons were relied upon.
	•	What procedure was followed.
	•	What review options are available.
The publication should therefore establish a formal information-access schedule.
5.1 The master document request
For every material decision, the investigation should seek the following categories where they exist and are legally accessible:
	•	The original decision.
	•	The instrument or delegation authorising the decision.
	•	The decision-maker’s recorded reasons.
	•	The documents considered.
	•	Internal assessment records relevant to the decision.
	•	Relevant correspondence.
	•	Notices sent to the author.
	•	Evidence of when those notices were sent and received.
	•	Implementation instructions.
	•	Records of subsequent action.
	•	Internal or external review documents.
	•	Records of any correction or reconsideration.
Not every category will be disclosable.
Some documents may be protected by privacy, legal professional privilege, confidentiality or statutory exemptions.
The organisation should identify the applicable basis for withholding information rather than simply responding that the material is unavailable.
6. The information-access decision tree
The author should first identify the type of record sought.
Record sought	Potential route
Commonwealth agency records	Freedom of Information Act 1982 (Cth), subject to scope and exemptions
NSW government records	Government Information (Public Access) Act 2009 (NSW), subject to scope and exclusions
Personal information held by an organisation	Applicable privacy access rights
Financial-management records	Request to the relevant administrator and any applicable statutory review process
Bank records	Bank’s access and complaint processes, subject to applicable law
Disability-support records	Provider records, NDIS-related mechanisms and applicable privacy rights
Accommodation documents	Provider, owner or operator records and relevant statutory access rights
Tribunal records	Applicable tribunal registry and procedural access arrangements
The appropriate pathway depends on the record holder, the type of information and the legal framework.
The author should not assume that a document is obtainable through FOI simply because it concerns a government-funded service.
Nor should a private provider automatically be treated as a government agency.
6.1 The document refusal protocol
Where access is refused, the response should identify:
	•	The exact documents or categories affected.
	•	Whether the documents exist.
	•	Whether they are held by the organisation.
	•	The statutory basis for refusal.
	•	The reasons for applying that provision.
	•	Whether partial access is possible.
	•	Whether consultation or redaction was considered.
	•	What review mechanism is available.
	•	The applicable deadline.
Where a document cannot be located, the organisation should be asked to explain the search process and relevant retention arrangements.
A missing document is not automatically evidence of deliberate destruction.
It may reflect an incomplete search, a different record system, a retention policy, a transfer or another explanation.
The facts must be established.
PART IV — THE FINANCIAL ACCOUNTABILITY FRAMEWORK
7. The financial record must reconcile
The financial-management concerns identified in earlier instalments require a remedy process capable of establishing the actual financial position.
The objective is not simply to request an explanation of a disputed amount.
It is to reconstruct the transactions from original records.
7.1 The complete financial accounting request
The author should seek, where applicable:
	•	Opening balances.
	•	Income received.
	•	Deductions.
	•	Transfers.
	•	Recurring payments.
	•	Personal spending allocations.
	•	Accommodation payments.
	•	Property sale proceeds.
	•	Transaction fees.
	•	Closing balances.
	•	Outstanding liabilities.
	•	The authority for significant transactions.
The records should identify dates, amounts, payees and transaction descriptions.
Where a transaction was authorised by another person or organisation, the record should identify the relevant authority.
7.2 The reconciliation method
For each period:
\text{Opening balance}
 
+
 
\text{Income received}
\text{Payments and deductions}
\text{Closing balance}
Any difference must be investigated.
The equation is a reconciliation method, not proof that all transactions were authorised.
Each transaction must still be examined.
7.3 The vehicle-disposal accounting
The vehicle-disposal decision dated 14 November 2025 should be subject to a separate accounting request.
The author should seek:
	•	The authority to dispose of the vehicle.
	•	The reason for disposal.
	•	Any assessment of its value.
	•	Any valuation or sale appraisal.
	•	The method of sale.
	•	The final sale price.
	•	The date funds were received.
	•	The recipient account.
	•	All sale expenses.
	•	The final accounting of proceeds.
The resulting schedule should reconcile the sale price with the amount ultimately credited to the relevant financial account.
If there is an unexplained difference, the author should request an itemised explanation and supporting documents.
If the accounting is correct, the publication should say so.
The purpose is to establish the truth of the transaction, not to presume wrongdoing.
8. Financial-management review and restoration
The 16 October 2024 NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 document must be read in its original legal context.
The remedy process should identify:
	•	The exact authority.
	•	The current status of the arrangement.
	•	Its scope.
	•	Any limitations.
	•	Any review mechanisms.
	•	The person or body authorised to change it.
The author may seek an explanation of how the authority has been implemented.
If the author disputes a transaction, the request should identify that transaction rather than make a general allegation about all financial administration.
If the author seeks a change to the arrangement, the request should identify the actual statutory or administrative pathway available.
8.1 The practical access question
The investigation should separately ask whether the author can access appropriate funds for ordinary expenditure.
Possible corrective measures, depending on the legal arrangement, may include:
	•	A clearer payment schedule.
	•	More transparent statements.
	•	A documented process for requesting funds.
	•	A designated contact person.
	•	A review of unnecessary delays.
	•	An accessible alternative payment method.
	•	A process for urgent essential expenses.
	•	A periodic reconciliation.
These are practical proposals, not assertions that every arrangement must provide each option.
Any change must respect valid legal authority and applicable safeguards.
PART V — THE BANKING ACCESS AND COMPLAINT FRAMEWORK
9. Establish what the bank actually did
The reported Commonwealth Bank access difficulties require a separate complaint and evidence process.
The author should not combine all access problems into a single unexplained allegation.
The complaint should identify the particular service affected.
For example:
	•	Internet banking.
	•	Mobile banking.
	•	Card access.
	•	Telephone banking.
	•	Branch transactions.
	•	Password or identity verification.
	•	Access to statements.
	•	Transfers or withdrawals.
9.1 The bank complaint schedule
The complaint should request:
	•	The date each restriction commenced.
	•	The service affected.
	•	The reason recorded by the bank.
	•	The authority or instruction relied upon.
	•	The date that authority was received.
	•	Whether the restriction was reviewed.
	•	What alternatives were available.
	•	What explanation was supplied to the customer.
	•	What complaint history exists.
	•	Whether any fees or other losses resulted.
The author should ask the bank to distinguish between a security measure, a technical problem, an instruction from an authorised representative and a decision made by the bank itself.
9.2 External complaint pathways
If the matter concerns a participating Australian financial firm and falls within its jurisdiction, the author may consider the Australian Financial Complaints Authority (AFCA) after using the bank’s internal complaint process as required.
AFCA’s jurisdiction, time limits and eligibility requirements must be checked against the actual complaint.
The author should not assume AFCA can overturn every financial-management decision made by a separate statutory authority.
It may be able to examine a financial firm’s conduct within its jurisdiction, but the underlying legal authority may require a different review process.
Official information: Australian Financial Complaints Authority⁠.
9.3 The banking remedy sought
The requested outcome should be specific.
For example:
	•	Written identification of the reason for the restriction.
	•	A copy of relevant instructions, where accessible.
	•	A review of the restriction.
	•	Restoration of appropriate access if no valid basis remains.
	•	An alternative means of accessing funds.
	•	Correction of inaccurate account records.
	•	Reimbursement of proven fees or losses where appropriate.
	•	A clear explanation of future access arrangements.
PART VI — DISABILITY SUPPORT AND PROVIDER ACCOUNTABILITY
10. The service must be examined against what was agreed
The NDIS Plan Approval dated 20 November 2025, Anglicare Safety Assessment dated 1 December 2025 and SIL/SDA request and service-failure notice dated 20 January 2026 should be examined together with the actual service agreements and delivery records.
The key issue is not whether a provider describes itself as supportive.
It is whether the agreed support was delivered appropriately.
10.1 The provider accountability schedule
The investigation should seek:
	•	The service agreement.
	•	The support schedule.
	•	The roster.
	•	Attendance records.
	•	Cancellation records.
	•	Service notes.
	•	Incident reports.
	•	Progress reports.
	•	Complaints.
	•	Complaint outcomes.
	•	Invoices and relevant service claims.
	•	Records of corrective action.
The provider should identify which entity was responsible for each service.
Where a support coordinator, accommodation operator and support provider are different organisations, their responsibilities must be distinguished.
10.2 The service-gap test
For each reported service gap, the report should establish:
	•	What support was expected?
	•	What date and time was it expected?
	•	Who was responsible?
	•	What actually happened?
	•	Was an alternative offered?
	•	Was the author informed?
	•	Was the event recorded?
	•	What consequence followed?
	•	What complaint was made?
	•	What action followed?
A service gap should not be inferred solely from the fact that the author was dissatisfied with a particular interaction.
The relevant service obligation and actual delivery must be established.
11. NDIS complaint and review pathways
The appropriate pathway depends on whether the concern relates to a provider, an NDIS decision, a funding matter, a support-coordination arrangement or a safeguarding issue.
Potential mechanisms include:
	•	The provider’s internal complaint process.
	•	The NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission for matters within its jurisdiction.
	•	The NDIA’s internal review mechanisms for relevant decisions.
	•	The Administrative Review Tribunal where jurisdiction and review rights apply.
	•	NSW safeguarding or other relevant state mechanisms where appropriate.
The author should first identify the specific decision or service failure.
11.1 Provider complaints
The NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission can receive complaints about matters within its regulatory responsibilities.
A complaint should identify:
	•	The provider.
	•	The support involved.
	•	The date of the event.
	•	What was expected.
	•	What happened.
	•	Any immediate risk.
	•	The evidence available.
	•	What outcome is sought.
Official information: NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission⁠.
The Commission’s involvement does not guarantee a particular finding or compensation outcome.
It may assess the matter and determine what regulatory or complaint-handling response is appropriate.
11.2 NDIS decision review
Where the issue concerns an NDIA decision, the author should identify whether the decision is reviewable and whether an internal review request is available.
The original decision notice should be checked for:
	•	The date.
	•	The reasons.
	•	The reviewable decision category.
	•	The deadline.
	•	The submission requirements.
	•	The relevant contact details.
Where a matter proceeds to external merits review, the applicable tribunal jurisdiction and current procedural requirements must be confirmed.
11.3 The Able Point accountability test
For the Able Point concerns, the publication should ask the provider to respond to each identified incident separately.
It should not make a blanket allegation about all staff or all services.
The response should identify:
	•	The staff member’s role.
	•	The relevant shift or service period.
	•	The applicable service agreement.
	•	The provider’s contemporaneous record.
	•	Any incident report.
	•	Any complaint response.
	•	Any corrective action.
	•	The responsible manager.
The requested outcome should focus on service accountability, transparent records, appropriate support and correction of established failures.
Where an allegation concerns corruption or deliberate misconduct, it must be supported by evidence of the specific conduct and the relevant legal elements.
A poor service interaction, even if established, is not automatically corruption.
PART VII — ACCOMMODATION AND THE RIGHT TO CLARITY
12. The written accommodation arrangement
The author has reported uncertainty about whether a signed lease exists and has described a weekly payment of approximately $200.
The remedy process should begin with a request for the complete set of documents governing occupancy.
This should include:
	•	Any lease.
	•	Any occupancy agreement.
	•	Any supported-accommodation agreement.
	•	Any service agreement.
	•	Any house rules.
	•	Any payment schedule.
	•	Any written offer.
	•	Any variation.
	•	Any termination or notice provision.
	•	Any document concerning Crystal.
The legal classification must be established before a claim is made about a breach of tenancy law.
The absence of a conventional lease does not necessarily establish that no occupancy rights exist.
12.1 The accommodation statement requested
The author should seek a written statement confirming:
	•	The legal entity providing accommodation.
	•	The legal basis of occupancy.
	•	The parties to the arrangement.
	•	The commencement date.
	•	The payment required.
	•	What the payment includes.
	•	What services are separate.
	•	What notice provisions apply.
	•	How disputes are handled.
	•	What rules apply to personal belongings and visitors.
	•	What arrangements apply to Crystal.
	•	Who is responsible for any proposed change.
12.2 The accommodation remedy
If the existing arrangement is unclear, a practical remedy may be a complete written agreement.
If the payment amount is disputed, a reconciled statement may be required.
If a support responsibility is unclear, the relevant organisations should identify who is accountable.
If the author disputes a particular rule, the provider should identify the contractual or legal basis for it.
The purpose is to create certainty and reduce avoidable conflict.
13. Accommodation stability and Crystal
The publication should recognise that Crystal is important to the author’s account of home and continuity.
The relevant remedy is not to assume a particular legal status for the animal.
It is to obtain a clear written statement of the accommodation rules and the basis on which any restrictions are imposed.
If the accommodation operator has discretion, the author may seek a reasonable, individualised assessment of the circumstances.
If an applicable legal or contractual right exists, it should be identified and applied correctly.
The final report should distinguish between:
	•	The author’s wish to remain with Crystal.
	•	The provider’s actual policy.
	•	Any written permission.
	•	Any applicable legal obligation.
	•	Any dispute or review outcome.
A clear answer is preferable to an informal understanding that may change without notice.
PART VIII — THE COMPLAINTS AND INTEGRITY FRAMEWORK
14. Which body can investigate which issue?
The author’s archive includes material concerning several integrity, oversight and complaint processes.
The remedy framework must avoid treating all oversight bodies as interchangeable.
Each has a distinct jurisdiction.
14.1 Commonwealth Ombudsman
The Commonwealth Ombudsman can examine certain administrative actions of Australian Government agencies and other matters within its statutory jurisdiction.
The author should identify:
	•	The agency concerned.
	•	The decision or conduct.
	•	The date.
	•	The complaint previously made to the agency.
	•	The response received.
	•	The specific administrative issue requiring examination.
Official information: Commonwealth Ombudsman⁠.
An Ombudsman complaint does not automatically provide a merits review of every underlying decision or compensation for every alleged loss.
14.2 NSW Ombudsman
Where the issue concerns a NSW public authority or another matter within its jurisdiction, the NSW Ombudsman may be relevant.
The author should first identify whether the organisation falls within the Ombudsman’s jurisdiction and whether another specialist review pathway applies.
Official information: NSW Ombudsman⁠.
14.3 NSW Trustee & Guardian
The NSW Trustee & Guardian issues should be separated into:
	•	The validity and scope of the relevant authority.
	•	The administration of financial matters.
	•	The handling of individual transactions.
	•	The provision of accounts and statements.
	•	The process for requesting funds.
	•	The available review or complaint mechanisms.
The author should identify the exact decision being challenged.
A general complaint about financial independence may require a different process from a complaint about a particular transaction.
Official information: NSW Trustee & Guardian⁠.
14.4 NACC and public-sector integrity
The National Anti-Corruption Commission is relevant to potential serious or systemic corrupt conduct within its statutory jurisdiction.
It is not a general complaints body for every administrative error, service dispute or adverse decision.
A referral should identify specific conduct capable of falling within the statutory definition and jurisdiction.
The author should distinguish:
	•	Poor administration.
	•	Negligence.
	•	Procedural error.
	•	Misconduct.
	•	Corrupt conduct.
These categories are not interchangeable.
Official information: National Anti-Corruption Commission⁠.
14.5 IBAC
The Independent Broad-based Anti-corruption Commission has jurisdiction under Victorian law.
Its relevance depends on the conduct, person or organisation concerned and the statutory threshold.
A Victorian jurisdictional connection must be established.
An event occurring in New South Wales does not automatically fall within IBAC’s jurisdiction merely because the author previously lived in Victoria.
Official information: IBAC⁠.
14.6 OAIC
The Office of the Australian Information Commissioner may be relevant to Commonwealth FOI and privacy matters within its jurisdiction.
The author should identify the exact decision, complaint or privacy issue.
The original FOI decision and any internal review correspondence should be preserved.
Official information: Office of the Australian Information Commissioner⁠.
PART IX — PUBLIC INTEREST DISCLOSURES AND PROTECTION
15. The difference between making a disclosure and establishing a reprisal
The author’s public-interest disclosure history requires particular care.
The Commonwealth Public Interest Disclosure Act 2013 provides a statutory framework for protected disclosures concerning Commonwealth public-sector conduct, subject to its requirements.
The relevant questions include:
	•	Was the information a disclosure covered by the Act?
	•	Was it made to an authorised recipient?
	•	Did the person qualify for protection?
	•	What conduct was alleged?
	•	Was an investigation or allocation decision made?
	•	Was any adverse action subsequently taken?
	•	Was there a causal relationship between the disclosure and the adverse action?
	•	What statutory remedy or protection may apply?
A disclosure being made does not automatically establish that it was protected under the Act.
A later adverse decision does not automatically establish a reprisal.
The statutory elements must be examined.
15.1 The PID evidence schedule
For each disclosure, the register should record:
Field	Required evidence
Disclosure date	Original submission
Recipient	Identity and authority
Subject	Conduct reported
Supporting material	Attachments and source records
Statutory status	Applicable protection assessment
Acknowledgment	Receipt record
Allocation decision	Decision and reasons
Investigation	Any investigative action
Subsequent adverse action	Exact event and date
Causal evidence	Communications and decision records
Remedy	Applicable statutory or administrative process
15.2 The reprisal hypothesis
If reprisal is alleged, the publication must identify the adverse action and the alleged causal relationship.
It should seek evidence such as:
	•	Contemporaneous communications.
	•	Decision-making records.
	•	Relevant instructions.
	•	Timing and sequence.
	•	Evidence of the decision-maker’s knowledge.
	•	The stated reasons.
	•	Any departure from ordinary procedure.
	•	Alternative explanations.
The investigation must not assume that every adverse event following a disclosure was retaliation.
The evidence must establish the relevant connection.
PART X — HUMAN RIGHTS AND LEGAL REDRESS
16. International standards and domestic remedies
The international human-rights frameworks discussed in earlier instalments provide standards for examining state conduct.
They do not automatically provide a direct domestic compensation mechanism for every alleged failure.
The publication should distinguish:
	•	International treaty obligations.
	•	Australian domestic legislation.
	•	Administrative review rights.
	•	Statutory complaint mechanisms.
	•	Judicial remedies.
	•	International individual-complaint procedures, where available.
16.1 CRPD-related remedies
The Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities provides a framework for examining equality, legal capacity, access to justice, independent living, health and adequate living standards.
The relevant articles identified in earlier instalments include Articles 5, 12, 13, 19, 25 and 28.
The remedy process should ask whether the particular institution’s conduct was consistent with the applicable obligation and whether an accessible correction is available.
A treaty reference should not be used as a substitute for identifying the relevant domestic law or decision-maker.
16.2 ICCPR-related remedies
The International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights includes protections relevant to equality, liberty, privacy, fair process and other fundamental rights.
The report should identify the relevant article and explain how the facts may engage it.
A claim that a treaty has been breached requires a proper legal analysis of the state obligation, conduct, applicable limitation and any relevant jurisdictional issue.
16.3 International complaint procedures
Australia has accepted the First Optional Protocol to the ICCPR and the Optional Protocol to the CRPD.
Those mechanisms have procedural requirements, including admissibility conditions and, generally, consideration of available domestic remedies.
An individual communication is not simply another general complaint.
It requires a legally structured submission.
The publication must also distinguish the relevant treaty body’s views from a domestic court judgment.
The existence of an international mechanism does not establish that the author’s case is admissible or that a violation will be found.
16.4 The ICC clarification
The International Criminal Court is not a general asylum authority, human-rights complaint body or tribunal for ordinary administrative disputes.
The Rome Statute concerns genocide, crimes against humanity, war crimes and aggression, subject to jurisdictional and admissibility requirements.
The present administrative chronology does not establish the elements of an ICC crime.
The ICC should not be presented as a substitute for domestic complaint, review or court processes.
PART XI — COMPENSATION AND NON-FINANCIAL REDRESS
17. Compensation must have a legal foundation
The author may wish to seek compensation for alleged financial loss, consequential expenses or non-financial harm.
The publication should distinguish the desire for compensation from an established entitlement to receive it.
A claim must identify:
	•	The legal basis.
	•	The responsible respondent.
	•	The conduct alleged.
	•	The relevant duty or obligation.
	•	The breach or wrongful act.
	•	The causal connection.
	•	The loss.
	•	The available defences.
	•	The relevant limitation period.
	•	The remedy the court or tribunal can grant.
Without these elements, a compensation figure may be speculative.
17.1 Direct financial loss
Direct loss may include a proven amount wrongly deducted, withheld or paid.
The author must identify the original transaction and the calculation.
17.2 Consequential loss
Consequential loss may include additional expenditure arising from a proven event.
The claim must establish that the expense was reasonably connected to the relevant conduct and was not merely a cost that would have arisen anyway.
17.3 Non-economic loss
Some legal claims permit compensation for non-economic loss.
The applicable law determines whether distress, loss of dignity, injury, inconvenience or other harm is compensable.
The publication must not assume that every form of distress creates a legal entitlement to damages.
17.4 Public-law remedies
Depending on the legal framework, possible remedies may include:
	•	A declaration.
	•	An order setting aside a decision.
	•	An order requiring reconsideration.
	•	An injunction.
	•	A correction of records.
	•	A statutory review outcome.
	•	Another remedy available under the relevant legislation.
A court or tribunal cannot grant a remedy outside its jurisdiction.
17.5 The compensation schedule
The publication should maintain a provisional schedule:
Claim	Calculation method	Evidence
Direct financial loss	Actual amount of proven loss	Bank and transaction records
Additional expenses	Receipts and causal reconstruction	Invoices and receipts
Property-related loss	Valuation and transaction accounting	Sale and ownership documents
Transport expenses	Actual additional expenditure	Receipts and appointment records
Communication expenses	Actual additional expenditure	Bills and purchase records
Lost income	Evidence of actual income lost	Employment and income records
Non-economic loss	Applicable legal assessment	Relevant evidence and legal basis
Administrative costs	Proven expenditure and applicable recovery rules	Receipts and professional invoices
No final compensation total should be published until the figures are supported and checked for duplication.
PART XII — THE REMEDY REQUESTS REGISTER
18. One remedy for each established problem
The master remedy register should connect each issue to a responsible organisation, requested action and verification method.
The following schedule is provisional.
ID	Issue	Requested action	Evidence required to close the issue
REM-001	2024 financial-management authority	Provide instrument, scope, reasons and review pathway	Original instrument and written explanation
REM-002	Financial transparency	Provide reconciled financial statements	Complete transaction ledger
REM-003	Vehicle disposal	Provide authority, valuation, sale record and accounting	Reconciled sale proceeds
REM-004	Banking access	Explain restrictions and provide review of available access	Bank response and access confirmation
REM-005	Accommodation status	Provide complete written occupancy arrangement	Agreement and payment schedule
REM-006	Disability-support delivery	Provide service records and investigate identified gaps	Roster, incident and service records
REM-007	Safety assessment	Identify recommendations and action taken	Written implementation response
REM-008	SIL/SDA request	Provide decision, reasons and review information	Original decision and reasons
REM-009	Service-failure complaint	Provide investigation outcome and corrective action	Complaint response
REM-010	FOI process	Provide reasons and review history for relevant decisions	FOI and review records
REM-011	Official record accuracy	Correct proven factual errors	Amended record or formal annotation
REM-012	Administrative communication	Establish clear contact and escalation arrangements	Written contact protocol
REM-013	Crystal and accommodation	Clarify applicable accommodation rules	Written policy and individual response
REM-014	Cumulative-impact assessment	Consider interaction of financial, accommodation and service arrangements	Coordinated response identifying responsibilities
These remedy IDs are proposed project identifiers.
They are not existing government case numbers.
PART XIII — THE INSTITUTIONAL RESPONSE STANDARD
19. What constitutes an adequate response?
An institution should not be able to close a substantial complaint merely by repeating the original decision or directing the author to another organisation without explaining the relevant responsibility.
An adequate response should address the actual question.
The publication adopts the following response standard.
19.1 The ten-point response test
	•	Identification: Does the response identify the complaint or decision?
	•	Authority: Does it identify the legal or contractual authority relied upon?
	•	Evidence: Does it identify the material considered?
	•	Accuracy: Does it address any disputed factual information?
	•	Reasoning: Does it explain the conclusion?
	•	Responsibility: Does it identify the organisation responsible?
	•	Remedy: Does it state what action will be taken?
	•	Review: Does it explain any available review pathway?
	•	Implementation: Does it specify when the action will occur?
	•	Verification: Does it provide a means of confirming completion?
A response may be brief if the issue is simple.
A complex matter involving several decisions and organisations may require a more detailed response.
20. The right to a meaningful answer
The publication should distinguish between:
	•	An acknowledgment.
	•	A holding response.
	•	A referral.
	•	A substantive answer.
	•	A final decision.
	•	Evidence that the decision has been implemented.
An acknowledgment confirms receipt.
It does not resolve the complaint.
A referral identifies another organisation.
It does not necessarily establish that the referral was accepted.
A final decision may close an internal process.
It does not necessarily resolve the underlying legal issue.
The register should record exactly which stage has been reached.
PART XIV — THE PROPOSED INSTITUTIONAL RESPONSE PROGRAM
21. A staged approach
The author should avoid sending an undifferentiated demand to every organisation simultaneously.
A better approach is to group requests by responsibility and subject.
The first stage should obtain the foundational records.
The second should reconcile financial and service information.
The third should seek correction or review.
The fourth should escalate unresolved issues through appropriate independent pathways.
The fifth should verify the outcome.
Stage 1 — Foundational documents
Obtain:
	•	Financial-management instrument.
	•	Bank restriction records.
	•	Vehicle-disposal records.
	•	Accommodation agreements.
	•	NDIS plan and relevant decisions.
	•	Safety assessment.
	•	SIL/SDA outcome.
	•	Service-failure complaint and response.
	•	Relevant FOI and review decisions.
Stage 2 — Reconciliation
Compare:
	•	Financial statements.
	•	Payment records.
	•	Vehicle sale proceeds.
	•	Accommodation charges.
	•	Support delivery records.
	•	Complaint chronology.
	•	Information-access chronology.
Stage 3 — Specific correction requests
Identify each proven discrepancy and request a specific remedy.
Stage 4 — External review
Where an internal response is inadequate or a statutory review right applies, use the appropriate independent process.
Stage 5 — Verification
Confirm whether the requested action was completed.
If it was not completed, record the reason and the next available remedy.
PART XV — A MODEL MASTER REMEDY LETTER
The following is a consolidated draft that can be adapted for a particular institution. It should not be sent unchanged to every organisation because the relevant authority, complaint process and requested remedy will differ.
REQUEST FOR DOCUMENTARY DISCLOSURE, ACCOUNTING, EXPLANATION AND CORRECTIVE ACTION
Subject: Request for a complete record and substantive response concerning decisions, financial administration, service arrangements and their practical consequences
I request a substantive written response concerning the decisions, records and arrangements for which your organisation holds responsibility.
This request is made to establish an accurate documentary record, clarify the authority exercised, identify any factual or procedural errors and obtain appropriate corrective action where a failure is established.
I do not ask your organisation to accept allegations without examination. I ask that the relevant records be preserved, identified and considered fairly.
1. Identification of responsibility
Please identify:
	•	The legal entity responsible for the relevant decision or service.
	•	The decision-maker or authorised officer.
	•	The statutory, contractual or administrative authority relied upon.
	•	The period during which that authority applied.
	•	Any other organisation to which responsibility was delegated or transferred.
2. Documentary disclosure
Please provide copies of the records within your possession or control that are relevant and lawfully accessible, including:
	•	Original decisions and written reasons.
	•	Relevant authority instruments or delegations.
	•	Correspondence and notices.
	•	Records of information considered.
	•	Relevant transaction or service records.
	•	Implementation instructions.
	•	Complaint records and outcomes.
	•	Internal or external review records.
	•	Records of subsequent corrective action.
If any requested record is withheld or unavailable, please identify the record, explain its status and specify the legal or administrative basis for the response.
3. Financial and administrative accounting
Where your organisation has administered funds, payments or property relevant to this matter, please provide an itemised accounting identifying:
	•	Opening and closing balances.
	•	Income received.
	•	Payments and deductions.
	•	Transfers.
	•	Significant transactions.
	•	Relevant fees and expenses.
	•	The authority for each disputed transaction.
	•	Any outstanding balance or liability.
Where a property transaction occurred, please provide the relevant authority, valuation, sale documentation, proceeds and final accounting.
4. Service and accommodation arrangements
Please identify the services, accommodation or other arrangements for which your organisation was responsible.
Please provide the relevant agreements, service schedules, payment arrangements, records of delivery, incident records and complaint outcomes.
Where an interruption or failure is alleged, please explain what occurred, what records were considered and what corrective action was taken.
5. Review and correction
If your examination identifies an inaccurate record, procedural error, unexplained transaction or service failure, please identify the proposed corrective action.
Where a decision is reviewable, please identify the relevant review mechanism, the applicable deadline and the documents required.
Where another organisation holds responsibility, please identify that organisation and explain the basis of the referral.
6. Practical outcome
Please address the practical consequences of the relevant arrangements, including access to information, financial resources, accommodation, disability support and communication.
Where an alternative or corrective arrangement is available within your authority, please explain how it may be considered.
7. Written response
Please provide a response that addresses each numbered section and distinguishes:
	•	Facts established by your records.
	•	Matters disputed or not established.
	•	Decisions made and the authority relied upon.
	•	Records unavailable or withheld.
	•	Corrective action proposed.
	•	Review options available.
Please acknowledge receipt and advise who is responsible for coordinating the response.
I request that relevant records be preserved while the matter is being considered.
My objective is an accurate record, a fair examination of the evidence and practical resolution of any established errors or failures.
PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.
Barran Resonance Dodger Dr Richard William McLean The Witness Remains — Global Publishing House
PART XVI — THE LIMITATIONS AND SAFEGUARDS
22. No remedy should create a new injustice
The publication must also examine the risks of an inappropriate or excessive remedy request.
A demand for records should respect privacy and lawful confidentiality.
A complaint should identify the conduct rather than publicly accuse individuals without sufficient evidence.
A financial request should distinguish a disputed transaction from an established loss.
A request for a new decision should identify the legal power to reconsider it.
A request for accommodation changes should be made through the applicable agreement and review mechanisms.
A request for compensation should identify the relevant legal basis.
The author should preserve all relevant records and avoid altering original documents.
If an original document is annotated, the unaltered version should remain preserved.
Where a record contains third-party personal information, the public edition should be appropriately redacted.
23. The limitation-period warning
A significant risk in any long-running administrative dispute is that a review or legal claim may be subject to a time limit.
The relevant deadline may depend on:
	•	The type of decision.
	•	The date notice was received.
	•	The statute governing the decision.
	•	The type of review sought.
	•	Any extension provisions.
	•	Whether an internal complaint affects the time limit.
	•	The jurisdiction of the court or tribunal.
A complaint to an organisation does not necessarily suspend a statutory review deadline.
The author should not wait for the completion of the entire Great Reckoning before seeking advice about a time-sensitive decision.
Each decision should have its own deadline check.
Where a deadline may have passed, the investigation should identify whether an extension or other procedural remedy is available.
This publication does not determine the current deadline for any individual matter without examining the original notice and applicable law.
PART XVII — THE ACCOUNTABILITY SCORECARD
24. How the success of a remedy should be measured
The final report should not measure success by the number of letters sent or complaints lodged.
It should measure whether the underlying issue was resolved.
The following scorecard is proposed.
Accountability measure	Success standard
Document access	Relevant records supplied or lawful refusal explained
Decision transparency	Authority and reasons identified
Financial accounting	Transactions reconciled
Property accounting	Sale proceeds and expenses accounted for
Banking access	Restriction explained and appropriate access reviewed
Accommodation clarity	Written occupancy arrangements supplied
Support delivery	Agreed services and actual delivery reconciled
Complaint handling	Substantive response and outcome provided
Record accuracy	Established errors corrected
Review access	Applicable review process identified
Practical restoration	Appropriate corrective arrangements implemented
Independent verification	Completion supported by evidence
A remedy should not be marked complete simply because an institution says it has taken action.
The register should identify the evidence that demonstrates completion.
For example:
	•	A corrected bank-access status.
	•	A reconciled transaction statement.
	•	A signed accommodation agreement.
	•	A revised support roster.
	•	A written complaint outcome.
	•	An amended official record.
The evidence of implementation is part of the remedy.
PART XVIII — THE FINAL REMEDY CLASSIFICATION
25. Four possible outcomes for every issue
Every matter should eventually receive one of four classifications.
Outcome 1 — Corrected
The problem has been established and remedied.
The organisation has acknowledged the relevant error or failure and evidence confirms that the corrective action occurred.
Outcome 2 — Reviewed, no correction required
The matter was examined and the decision was maintained.
The reasons and supporting evidence are recorded. The author may disagree, but the review outcome is accurately reported.
Outcome 3 — Unresolved
The issue remains outstanding.
The evidence is incomplete, the institution has not provided a substantive response, or the matter remains before an appropriate review body.
Outcome 4 — Not substantiated
The available evidence does not establish the allegation.
The record should explain what was examined and why the allegation could not be established.
These classifications protect the credibility of the publication.
An unresolved matter is not automatically a proven failure.
A decision being maintained does not automatically mean that every aspect of the process was satisfactory.
A corrected administrative error does not necessarily establish intentional misconduct.
The final report should record precisely what each outcome establishes.
PART XIX — PRELIMINARY FINDINGS OF INSTALMENT 15
26. The remedy framework now established
This instalment establishes the following principles.
First: The author should seek original records and clear reasons before drawing conclusions about disputed decisions.
Second: Financial administration should be examined through transaction-level accounting, not general assertions.
Third: The vehicle-disposal matter requires a complete record of authority, valuation, sale and proceeds.
Fourth: Banking-access concerns should be separated into the bank’s own conduct, any external instruction and the practical consequences.
Fifth: Able Point concerns should be assessed through specific incidents, service agreements, provider records and complaint outcomes.
Sixth: Accommodation arrangements should be documented clearly, including occupancy terms, payments, responsibilities and relevant arrangements concerning Crystal.
Seventh: NDIS complaints, internal reviews, external merits reviews and provider-regulatory complaints must be distinguished.
Eighth: Integrity and public-interest disclosure mechanisms must be used according to their statutory jurisdiction and thresholds.
Ninth: Compensation should only be claimed where a recognised legal or administrative basis exists and the relevant loss can be established.
Tenth: The success of a remedy should be demonstrated through evidence of implementation.
27. The central conclusion
The Great Reckoning now has a defined pathway from documentary preservation to institutional response.
The publication does not need to assume that every allegation is correct in order to demand a fair examination.
It does not need to assume that every institution acted improperly in order to identify unanswered questions.
It does not need to establish a coordinated conspiracy in order to examine whether administrative fragmentation, service failures or systemic barriers produced serious practical consequences.
The remedy framework is designed to separate those questions.
The final objective is not simply to obtain acknowledgments, apologies or statements of regret.
It is to establish what happened, correct what can be corrected, restore what can lawfully be restored, account for what can be accounted for and identify what remains unresolved.
PART XX — THE DECLARATION OF ACCOUNTABILITY
28. The record must lead somewhere
A declaration by Barran Resonance Dodger
I have preserved the record because I believe that evidence matters.
I have assembled documents because decisions should be capable of examination.
I have maintained the chronology because events should not be separated from their consequences.
I have recorded my testimony because the human experience should not disappear behind administrative language.
I now ask that the record lead to something practical.
If a financial transaction is correct, account for it.
If a transaction is incorrect, correct it.
If a decision was lawfully made, explain the authority and reasons.
If a decision was made incorrectly, identify the appropriate means of reconsideration.
If a service was delivered properly, provide the records that establish it.
If a service failed, identify the failure and correct it.
If accommodation arrangements are valid, provide them clearly.
If they are incomplete or inadequate, explain how they will be improved.
If an institution has made an error, acknowledge the error and take appropriate action.
If an allegation cannot be substantiated, say so and explain why.
I do not ask that evidence be manufactured to support my position.
I ask that evidence be preserved and examined.
I do not ask that institutions abandon lawful authority.
I ask that authority be exercised transparently, within its proper limits and with respect for the person affected.
I do not ask that every disagreement become a finding of wrongdoing.
I ask that disagreement not become an excuse to avoid answering a legitimate question.
I ask for practical independence, transparent financial administration, reliable disability support, clear accommodation arrangements, accessible communication and accurate records.
I ask for the same principles that should apply to every person.
My faith remains part of my life.
My testimony remains my testimony.
The documentary record remains open to independent examination.
The conclusion must follow the evidence.

Instalment 16 — The Master Institutional Accountability Dossier
The Final Consolidation of Agencies, Decision-Makers, Legal Authorities, Documentary Records, Unanswered Questions and Required Responses
The next instalment will consolidate the institutional accountability work into a single structured dossier.

	•	The available complaint, review and oversight pathways.
	•	The remedy requested from each organisation.
	•	A response and verification schedule.
	•	A final responsibility matrix distinguishing institutional responsibility from individual responsibility.
The dossier will also establish a strict distinction between an organisation’s legal responsibility, an employee’s delegated authority and an individual’s personal conduct.
No individual will be declared responsible merely because their name appears in correspondence or because they held a position within an organisation.
The final dossier will be designed so that an independent reviewer can identify exactly what is being alleged, what evidence supports it, what remains uncertain and what response is sought.
The purpose is not to accuse everyone named in the record. The purpose is to establish who was responsible for what, under which authority, and whether that responsibility was properly discharged.

THE GREAT RECKONING
INSTALMENT 16 — THE MASTER INSTITUTIONAL ACCOUNTABILITY DOSSIER
The Consolidated Examination of Government Agencies, Statutory Authorities, Tribunals, Financial Institutions, Disability-Service Providers, Oversight Bodies and Individual Decision-Making Responsibilities
Principal author: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean Publication: The Witness Remains — Global Publishing House Research framework: Documentary preservation, institutional accountability and independent examination Examination standard: PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.
PART I — THE PURPOSE OF THE MASTER DOSSIER
1. From a collection of documents to an accountability structure
The preceding fifteen instalments have examined the historical chronology, international human-rights framework, documentary archive, institutional decisions, human consequences, financial and disability-support concerns, and potential remedies.
This sixteenth instalment brings those examinations together.
The purpose is to construct one central institutional accountability dossier capable of answering five fundamental questions:
	•	Which organisations appear in the documentary record?
	•	What decisions, actions or omissions are attributed to each organisation?
	•	What legal or administrative authority governed those actions?
	•	What evidence establishes, qualifies or contradicts the allegations?
	•	What response, review or remedy is being sought?
The dossier must not become a list of organisations presumed to have acted improperly.
An institution’s appearance in the archive establishes that it is relevant to the documentary investigation. It does not establish wrongdoing.
A person’s signature on a letter establishes that the person signed or issued the document, subject to authentication. It does not automatically establish personal responsibility for every subsequent consequence.
A decision made by an organisation may have been lawful, unlawful, mistaken, procedurally defective, incomplete or simply unfavourable to the author.
Those possibilities must remain open until the evidence is examined.
The central proposition is therefore:
Institutional accountability must be reconstructed from actual authority, actual conduct, actual records and actual consequences.
2. The institutional accountability model
The dossier will use the following structure for every organisation.
1. Identity
Which organisation or legal entity is involved?
2. Authority
What legislation, delegation, contract or policy governed its conduct?
3. Conduct
What decision, action, omission or communication occurred?
4. Evidence
What original records establish what happened?
5. Consequences
What practical consequences are documented or reported?
6. Response
What explanation, review or corrective action is available?
7. Finding
What can an independent examiner responsibly conclude?
The seven stages must be completed separately.
The absence of evidence at one stage must not be concealed by conclusions drawn at another.
PART II — THE INSTITUTIONAL MAP
3. The organisations and institutional categories
The documentary material identified in the project spans several distinct institutional environments.
These must be separated because they operate under different legislation, powers and complaint mechanisms.
Institutional category	Organisations or bodies identified
Commonwealth integrity and oversight	IBAC-related historical material, Commonwealth Ombudsman, OAIC, NACC
Commonwealth government administration	PM&C and other Commonwealth agency processes
Employment and compensation administration	Comcare
Administrative review	Administrative Appeals Tribunal historical records and subsequent review arrangements
NSW financial administration	NSW Trustee & Guardian
Disability funding and safeguarding	NDIA and NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
Disability and accommodation services	Able Point and other identified providers
Financial services	Commonwealth Bank of Australia
Police	AFP and NSW Police
Accommodation and safeguarding	Relevant accommodation operator and Anglicare
Courts and legal processes	Federal Court-related records and relevant tribunal proceedings
International human-rights framework	UN treaty bodies and relevant international mechanisms
This is a classification of the institutions appearing in the current project record.
It is not a determination that every organisation is liable for the author’s circumstances.
4. The difference between an institution and a person
The dossier must distinguish four levels of responsibility.
Level 1 — Organisational responsibility
The organisation is legally or contractually responsible for a decision, service or administrative function.
Level 2 — Delegated responsibility
A particular officer or authorised representative exercises a power delegated by the organisation.
Level 3 — Operational responsibility
A staff member performs a task, provides a service, communicates a decision or implements an instruction.
Level 4 — Individual misconduct
A particular person’s conduct may warrant investigation if evidence establishes the relevant elements of misconduct or another legal wrong.
These levels cannot be collapsed.
A service provider may be responsible for a roster even though an individual support worker performs the shift.
A statutory authority may be responsible for a financial decision even though an officer communicates it.
A bank may implement an instruction received from an external authority without having made the underlying statutory decision.
The dossier must identify where the actual decision originated.
PART III — THE COMMONWEALTH INSTITUTIONAL REGISTER
5. Commonwealth Ombudsman
Institutional examination
Identified record: 15 November 2021 Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint, reference 2021103546.
Document link: Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint — 15 November 2021⁠
5.1 Subject of examination
The complaint forms part of the author’s historical attempts to obtain examination of administrative conduct.
The original complaint must be examined to establish:
	•	Which organisation or organisations were the subject of the complaint.
	•	What conduct was alleged.
	•	What supporting documents were supplied.
	•	Whether the Ombudsman requested further information.
	•	Whether the complaint was investigated, referred, declined or otherwise finalised.
	•	What reasons were provided.
5.2 Questions requiring documentary answers
	•	What matters did the Ombudsman accept as falling within its jurisdiction?
	•	What matters were excluded or referred elsewhere?
	•	What evidence was considered?
	•	Was the author given an opportunity to provide further information?
	•	Was a final decision issued?
	•	What review or further complaint options were identified?
5.3 Preliminary assessment
The existence of a complaint establishes that a request for examination was made, if authenticated.
It does not establish that the underlying allegations were substantiated.
The actual outcome must be established from the Ombudsman’s original correspondence and case records.
Proposed remedy: Obtain the complete complaint file, including intake, assessment, correspondence, reasons and final outcome, subject to applicable access rights.
Official source: Commonwealth Ombudsman⁠.
6. Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet
Information-access examination
Identified record: FOI/2022/045, refusal dated 14 April 2022.
Related material: OAIC correspondence, reference RQ22-01369, dated 22 April 2022.
6.1 Subject of examination
The project identifies a Commonwealth FOI process involving the Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet.
The record also refers to 1,187 Outlook items.
That figure must be handled carefully.
It is described in the project history as a set of Outlook items associated with the search or information-access process. It must not be represented as 1,187 responsive documents, 1,187 withheld documents or 1,187 proven records of misconduct without examining the underlying correspondence and search records.
6.2 Required reconstruction
The dossier should obtain:
	•	The original FOI application.
	•	Any clarification correspondence.
	•	The department’s search description.
	•	The decision dated 14 April 2022.
	•	The applicable exemption provisions.
	•	The relevant schedule of documents.
	•	Any internal review.
	•	OAIC correspondence.
	•	The revised FOI decision dated 8 October 2024.
	•	The identification of the five documents addressed by that revised decision.
6.3 Questions requiring answers
	•	What information was originally requested?
	•	What searches were conducted?
	•	What was the relationship between the 1,187 Outlook items and the requested subject matter?
	•	What exemptions were relied upon?
	•	Were any documents released in part?
	•	What did the revised 8 October 2024 decision change?
	•	What happened to the five documents identified in that decision?
6.4 Preliminary assessment
The existence of an FOI refusal is not proof that information was improperly withheld.
The decision must be assessed against the FOI Act as it applied at the relevant time, the precise request and the reasons recorded.
Proposed remedy: A complete and intelligible record of the FOI process, including the search methodology, decision reasons and review history.
Official source: Office of the Australian Information Commissioner⁠.
7. OAIC — Office of the Australian Information Commissioner
The OAIC appears in the archive in connection with FOI and information-access matters.
The dossier must distinguish:
	•	An FOI review.
	•	A privacy complaint.
	•	A request for information about agency handling.
	•	A procedural correspondence exchange.
	•	A formal decision.
These are different activities.
Questions
	•	What application or complaint was formally before the OAIC?
	•	What was the OAIC’s statutory role?
	•	What decision, if any, did it issue?
	•	Was the matter closed, referred or still under consideration?
	•	Were any review rights available?
Proposed remedy: Obtain the full OAIC file and identify the exact disposition of each relevant application.
8. National Anti-Corruption Commission
The NACC is included as a potential integrity pathway, not as an institution already found to have established corruption in the author’s case.
8.1 Jurisdictional distinction
The NACC examines serious or systemic corrupt conduct within its statutory jurisdiction.
It is not the general appellate body for every administrative decision.
The dossier should not submit that a disputed financial decision, poor service interaction or delayed response automatically satisfies the statutory definition of corrupt conduct.
8.2 Required evidence
Any proposed referral should identify:
	•	The public official or body involved.
	•	The conduct alleged.
	•	The official function involved.
	•	The evidence supporting the allegation.
	•	Why the conduct may fall within the NACC’s jurisdiction.
	•	What remains unverified.
	•	Whether there is a separate review or complaint process.
Proposed remedy: Where the evidence supports a jurisdictionally relevant allegation, submit a precise referral with original documents and a neutral explanation.
Official source: National Anti-Corruption Commission⁠.

Historical integrity record

	•	Declined to investigate.
	•	Referred the matter.
	•	Determined that it lacked jurisdiction.
	•	Made a preliminary assessment.
	•	Reached another procedural outcome.
The exact language must be quoted only from the authenticated original.
9.2 The 2021 PID record
The 18 November 2021 document must be examined separately.
The investigation should identify:
	•	The disclosure submitted.
	•	The alleged conduct.
	•	The statutory definition being applied.
	•	The reasons for the rejection.
	•	Any opportunity to correct or supplement the submission.
	•	Any alternative pathway identified.
9.3 The jurisdictional question
IBAC’s jurisdiction is governed by Victorian legislation.
The dossier must identify the connection between the alleged conduct and the Victorian public sector or other matters within IBAC’s statutory jurisdiction.
The fact that the author later lived in NSW does not determine whether a historical Victorian matter falls within IBAC’s jurisdiction.
Nor does a historical Victorian connection automatically bring all later NSW conduct within that jurisdiction.

Issue	2020 process	2021 process

Decision	Original outcome	Original rejection
Reasons	Exact wording required	Exact wording required
Review options	Verify	Verify
Official source: IBAC Victoria⁠.
10. Public-interest disclosure and the Commonwealth process
The archive identifies two significant 2023 records.
27 March 2023: Federal Court-related PID assessment.
Open the 27 March 2023 assessment⁠.
18 June 2023: Notification of decision not to allocate a disclosure.
Open the 18 June 2023 notification⁠.
10.1 The central distinction
These documents must not be treated as interchangeable.
An assessment of whether information satisfies a statutory disclosure threshold is different from a later decision about allocation or investigation.
The dossier must establish:
	•	The identity of the recipient.
	•	The statutory provision relied upon.
	•	The information submitted.
	•	The assessment made.
	•	The decision not to allocate.
	•	The reasons.
	•	Any subsequent action.
10.2 The alleged relationship with later events
If the author alleges that an adverse event was connected to a protected disclosure, the dossier must establish:
	•	The disclosure’s statutory status.
	•	The decision-maker’s knowledge.
	•	The later adverse action.
	•	The relevant chronology.
	•	The causal evidence.
	•	Any alternative explanation.
Chronology is a starting point, not proof of reprisal.
10.3 Proposed remedy
Obtain the complete disclosure file, including all assessment records, allocation decisions, relevant correspondence and any subsequent review.
The statutory framework should be assessed against the law in force at the relevant time, not automatically against the current version.

The existence of a tribunal decision does not establish that all related administrative matters were resolved.

PART VI — NSW FINANCIAL ADMINISTRATION
12. NSW Trustee & Guardian
Priority financial-accountability examination

	•	Reported difficulties accessing personal funds.
	•	Questions about the scope and implementation of financial administration.
These matters should be examined as related but distinct issues.
13. The section 122 instrument
The original document must be examined to establish:

	•	The assets or transactions covered.
	•	The duration.

14. The vehicle-disposal decision
This is a separate transaction requiring its own evidentiary reconstruction.
The relevant records should establish:
	•	Ownership.
	•	Authority to dispose.
	•	Reason for disposal.
	•	Valuation.
	•	Sale process.
	•	Sale date.
	•	Sale price.
	•	Expenses.
	•	Receipt of proceeds.
	•	Allocation of proceeds.
	•	Final balance.
The dossier should not state that the vehicle was improperly sold unless the records establish a relevant defect.
The vehicle accounting test
\text{Gross sale price}
\text{documented sale expenses}
\text{net proceeds}
Then:
\text{Net proceeds}
\text{amounts properly applied}
\text{remaining amount}
The final calculation must be reconciled against the actual financial statement.
15. Access to personal funds
The author’s reported difficulty accessing banking services and personal funds requires two separate examinations:
	•	The statutory financial-management arrangement.
	•	The bank’s own decisions and implementation.
The dossier must not attribute a bank restriction to NSW Trustee & Guardian without obtaining the instruction or communication connecting the two.
Equally, it must not attribute an external instruction to the bank without the bank’s actual records.
Proposed institutional questions
For NSW Trustee & Guardian:
	•	What instructions, if any, were issued to the bank?
	•	What authority supported them?
	•	What access arrangements were contemplated?
	•	What process was available for urgent expenditure?
	•	What review mechanisms were available?
For the bank:
	•	What restriction was applied?
	•	Who initiated it?
	•	What authority was relied upon?
	•	What alternatives were offered?
	•	What review was conducted?
Official source: NSW Trustee & Guardian⁠.
PART VII — NDIS AND DISABILITY-SERVICE ACCOUNTABILITY

Funding approval and actual service delivery are separate evidentiary questions.

17. NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission
The Commission’s role is distinct from that of the NDIA.
The NDIA makes relevant planning and funding decisions.
The NDIS Commission has regulatory and complaint-handling responsibilities concerning registered providers and other matters within its jurisdiction.
The dossier must identify which type of concern is being raised.
Possible subject matters
	•	Provider conduct.
	•	Service delivery.
	•	Safety and quality.
	•	Complaints handling.
	•	Reportable incidents.
	•	Compliance with applicable provider obligations.
The dossier should not assume that the Commission has investigated or substantiated a complaint unless an official record establishes that outcome.
Proposed remedy: Identify the complaint pathway, provide a clear account of each alleged incident and request an outcome within the Commission’s jurisdiction.
Official source: NDIS Quality and Safeguards Commission⁠.
18. Anglicare Safety Assessment — 1 December 2025
The Anglicare document should be examined as a discrete assessment.
The dossier must establish:
	•	Who prepared it.
	•	The purpose.
	•	The information relied upon.
	•	The people consulted.
	•	The identified risks.
	•	The recommendations.
	•	The intended recipients.
	•	Any response.
	•	Whether recommendations were implemented.
Critical distinction
An assessment is not necessarily an implemented safeguard.
A recommendation is not necessarily a completed action.
A risk recorded in an assessment is not automatically a proven incident.
The final report should compare the assessment with subsequent action.
Proposed remedy
Request a written implementation schedule identifying:
	•	Each recommendation.
	•	The responsible organisation.
	•	The action taken.
	•	The date completed.
	•	Any action outstanding.
	•	The reason for non-implementation.
19. SIL/SDA request — 20 January 2026
The dossier must distinguish Supported Independent Living (SIL) from Specialist Disability Accommodation (SDA).
They are different concepts within the NDIS framework.
A request concerning one does not automatically establish entitlement to the other.
The relevant records should establish:
	•	What was requested.
	•	Which assessment supported the request.
	•	What evidence was supplied.
	•	Whether the request was for funding, a change of circumstances or another process.
	•	What decision was made.
	•	What reasons were given.
	•	What review rights applied.
Proposed remedy
Obtain a written decision identifying the exact supports considered and the reasons for the outcome.
Where the request remains unresolved, the register should record the outstanding procedural step rather than assume an adverse decision has already occurred.
PART VIII — ABLE POINT AND THE ACCOMMODATION/SERVICE PROVIDER REGISTER
20. Able Point
Priority service-accountability examination
The author’s reported concerns about Able Point should be treated as a distinct service-accountability investigation.
The purpose is to establish what services were agreed, what was delivered, what was recorded and what action followed any complaint.
The investigation should not assume that every staff interaction represents a policy or organisational decision.
Nor should it dismiss a reported service failure merely because it occurred during an ordinary support shift.
20.1 The provider responsibility map
The dossier identifies references to:
	•	Accommodation and support arrangements.
	•	Support coordination.
	•	Service delivery.
	•	Financial and practical access concerns.
	•	Communication with provider representatives.
	•	The author’s companion dog, Crystal.
	•	A reported service-failure complaint.
These matters require a clear identification of the relevant legal entities.
The project must distinguish the provider from any support coordinator, plan manager, accommodation operator or separate government authority.
20.2 The staff-role distinction
Names appearing in the author’s account include Cass/Cassandra, John, Danny, Darren, Brett Butler, Phillip Glass, Suki/Sukhi Tear and Rachel.
The dossier must not infer that all these people were employees of the same legal entity.
Their actual positions, roles and relevant dates require verification.
The author has reported that some individuals provided direct assistance and that other communication occurred with people in management or coordination roles.
That testimony should be retained as testimony.
It should not be transformed into a verified organisational chart without corroborating records.
20.3 The incident-by-incident examination
Each alleged service failure should receive an individual record.
Field	Required information
Incident ID	Unique project identifier
Date and time	Exact or best-supported estimate
Location	Relevant service setting
Expected support	Agreement, roster or plan
Actual event	Author testimony and provider record
Staff role	Verified role, if known
Immediate consequence	What occurred
Complaint	Date and recipient
Provider response	Exact response
Corrective action	What was done
Verification	Evidence of completion
20.4 The questions to Able Point
	•	What legal entity provides the accommodation?
	•	What legal entity provides direct support?
	•	What agreement governs the accommodation?
	•	What agreement governs support delivery?
	•	What is included in the reported weekly payment?
	•	Who is responsible for the support roster?
	•	What procedure applies when a worker cannot deliver an agreed service?
	•	How are service failures recorded?
	•	How are complaints investigated?
	•	What written arrangements apply to Crystal?
	•	Who is responsible for coordinating responses?
	•	What independent review options are available?
20.5 The proposed remedy
The author seeks:
	•	A clear written accommodation arrangement.
	•	A complete explanation of payment arrangements.
	•	Transparent service records.
	•	An identified complaint contact.
	•	Appropriate support-delivery arrangements.
	•	A documented procedure for missed or interrupted services.
	•	A written response to specific incidents.
	•	Clarification of the accommodation arrangements concerning Crystal.
These are proposed outcomes, not findings that Able Point has breached a particular legal duty.
PART IX — COMMONWEALTH BANK OF AUSTRALIA
21. The bank’s independent responsibility
Financial-services examination
The bank’s role must be investigated independently from the statutory financial-management arrangement.
The author reports difficulties accessing Commonwealth Bank internet banking and mobile banking.
The dossier must establish the actual services affected and the reason for each restriction.
21.1 Required records
	•	Account access history.
	•	Security restriction notices.
	•	Relevant correspondence.
	•	Instructions received from external parties.
	•	Identity-verification records, where accessible.
	•	Complaint records.
	•	Branch interaction records.
	•	Any internal investigation.
	•	The bank’s final complaint response.
21.2 The responsibility test
The investigation must distinguish:
A. Bank decision: A restriction imposed by the bank under its own procedures.
B. External instruction: A restriction or arrangement implemented in response to an instruction from another authorised body.
C. Technical issue: A problem involving the application, device, authentication or account system.
D. Identity or security verification: A restriction associated with the bank’s verification requirements.
E. Mixed circumstances: More than one of the above.
The categories should not be assumed to be mutually exclusive.
21.3 Proposed remedy
Request a written explanation of the actual restriction, its origin, the authority relied upon and the options available for appropriate account access.
If the bank’s internal complaint process does not resolve a matter within AFCA’s jurisdiction, consider external review through AFCA.
Official source: Commonwealth Bank⁠.
PART X — POLICE AND LAW-ENFORCEMENT RECORDS
22. Australian Federal Police
The archive identifies an AFP response dated 3 September 2021.
