	•	The subject of the communication.
	•	The precise question asked.
	•	Whether an investigation occurred.
	•	Whether the matter was referred.
	•	Whether the AFP declined action.
	•	The reason provided.
	•	Whether any further pathway was identified.
An AFP response should not be represented as a finding about conduct that it did not examine.
Official source: Australian Federal Police⁠.
23. NSW Police
The project identifies a NSW Police outcome dated 24 August 2024.
The dossier should establish:
	•	The report or event number.
	•	The reported conduct.
	•	The information supplied.
	•	The outcome.
	•	The reason recorded.
	•	Whether an investigation occurred.
	•	Whether any review or complaint pathway was available.
The police-outcome distinction
A police decision not to take further action does not necessarily establish that an event did not occur.
Equally, a report being lodged does not establish that the alleged conduct occurred.
The outcome must be described accurately.
Proposed remedy: Obtain the complete relevant police record and identify the precise procedural outcome.
Official source: NSW Police Force⁠.
PART XI — COURTS AND TRIBUNALS
24. Federal Court-related records
The archive identifies a letter dated 27 March 2023, associated with a public-interest disclosure assessment.
The document must be read in its actual context.
The dossier should establish:
	•	Who issued it.
	•	What proceeding or process it concerned.
	•	Whether it was a court communication or an administrative assessment associated with a court process.
	•	What legal question was being considered.
	•	What was expressly stated.
	•	What was not decided.
The report must not convert correspondence into a judicial finding.
A document referring to possible maladministration or other serious concerns does not establish that a court found those concerns proved.
The exact language and procedural status must be checked.
25. Administrative Review Tribunal
The ART is relevant to current administrative merits-review arrangements where the tribunal has jurisdiction.
Historical AAT records should remain identified by their original tribunal name.
The dossier should identify the particular decision under review and whether the current tribunal has jurisdiction over any new or continuing matter.
The existence of a prior tribunal file does not automatically create a new right of review.
Official source: Administrative Review Tribunal⁠.

PART XIII — THE EVIDENCE REQUIRED TO ESTABLISH RESPONSIBILITY
27. The institutional proof standard
The dossier should use an evidence hierarchy.
Evidence category	What it can establish
Authenticated original decision	What was formally decided
Signed authority instrument	The authority conferred, subject to interpretation
Official correspondence	What the organisation communicated
Transaction statement	Recorded financial movement
Service roster	Scheduled support
Contemporaneous service note	What the record says occurred
Complaint response	The organisation’s recorded position
Witness testimony	A person’s account of events
Independent assessment	Findings within the assessor’s scope
Subsequent implementation record	Whether action was taken
Hash or checksum	Whether a file matches a known digital fingerprint
No single evidence category proves everything.
A roster may establish that a shift was scheduled but not that the support was actually delivered.
A transaction statement may establish a transfer but not whether the transfer was legally authorised.
A signed decision may establish what was decided but not whether the underlying reasoning was correct.
A checksum may establish that a file has not changed relative to a particular hash. It cannot establish that the document’s contents are true.
28. The documentary cross-examination method
Every important decision should be tested against the following questions.
Authority: Who had the power to make the decision?
Knowledge: What information was available at the time?
Accuracy: Was the information factually correct?
Procedure: Was the required process followed?
Reasoning: Do the recorded reasons address the relevant issue?
Communication: Was the decision adequately communicated?
Consequences: What happened after implementation?
Review: Was a review mechanism available?
Correction: Was an identified error corrected?
Accountability: Who was responsible for ensuring that the decision was implemented properly?
The questions should be answered from original evidence wherever possible.
PART XIV — THE MASTER RESPONSIBILITY REGISTER
29. Individual responsibility must be independently established
The project contains the names of various public officials, employees, service representatives and other individuals.
The final report should not create a list of alleged wrongdoers simply because those names appear in documents.
Each person must be assessed through an individual responsibility record.
29.1 Required fields
Field	Purpose
Person ID	Unique internal reference
Name	Exact verified name
Position	Verified role at the relevant time
Organisation	Legal entity
Date range	Period of relevant involvement
Authority	Delegation or responsibility
Conduct	Specific action or omission
Evidence	Original supporting records
Response	Person’s or organisation’s explanation
Contradictory evidence	Material qualifying the allegation
Finding	Evidence-supported conclusion
Publication status	Public, restricted or redacted
29.2 The four categories of individual involvement
	•	Administrative contact: Communicated information or correspondence.
	•	Operational participant: Performed a service or implemented an instruction.
	•	Decision-maker: Exercised a relevant decision-making power.
	•	Subject of a substantiated allegation: Evidence supports an allegation requiring formal determination or a finding already made by an authorised body.
The fourth category requires substantially more than a name appearing in a letter.
29.3 Fairness and right of response
Where the publication proposes to make a serious adverse finding about an identifiable person, it should:
	•	Identify the exact allegation.
	•	Provide the material particulars.
	•	Distinguish fact from inference.
	•	Allow a reasonable opportunity to respond where practicable.
	•	Consider the response.
	•	Correct factual errors.
	•	Avoid publishing irrelevant personal information.
This is essential to the integrity of the publication.
PART XV — THE COMPETING EXPLANATIONS REGISTER
30. The dossier must test more than one explanation
The investigation should retain the five competing explanations established in the earlier instalments.
Hypothesis	Description	Evidence needed
A	Independent decisions by separate organisations	Individual decision records and legal authority
B	Administrative fragmentation	Evidence of disconnected processes and communication gaps
C	Service or procedural failures	Specific records of failures, omissions or errors
D	Systemic disadvantage	Evidence of recurring structural barriers and their effects
E	Deliberate coordination or improper purpose	Direct or sufficiently compelling circumstantial evidence of connection and intent
The hypotheses are not mutually exclusive.
For example, separate administrative failures could coexist with systemic disadvantage.
A coordinated action could be alleged, but the evidence must establish the relevant connection rather than merely show that multiple organisations were involved.
31. The six-level connection test
Any alleged relationship between institutions should be examined through six levels.
	•	Chronological connection: Did events occur in a relevant sequence?
	•	Subject-matter connection: Did the organisations address the same issue?
	•	Documentary connection: Did records or information pass between them?
	•	Operational connection: Did one organisation’s action affect another’s?
	•	Causal connection: Did the earlier action contribute to the later consequence?
	•	Improper-purpose connection: Is there evidence of an unlawful purpose, reprisal or deliberate coordination?
A chronology may establish the first level.
It cannot, by itself, establish the sixth.
PART XVI — THE PRIORITY DOCUMENT ACQUISITION PROGRAM
32. Documents required to complete the dossier
The following records should receive priority because they are central to reconstructing authority, decisions and consequences.
Priority	Document or record	Why it matters
1	Original NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 instrument	Establishes scope of financial authority
2	Complete financial ledger	Reconstructs administration
3	Vehicle sale and proceeds records	Reconciles property disposal
4	Bank access and restriction file	Identifies origin of access barriers
5	Current accommodation agreement	Establishes occupancy rights and responsibilities
6	Current support agreement	Establishes service obligations
7	Support rosters and service notes	Tests actual service delivery
8	NDIS Plan Approval and supporting reasons	Establishes funded support arrangements
9	Anglicare assessment and implementation records	Tests whether identified safeguards were acted upon
10	SIL/SDA request and decision	Establishes assessment and outcome
11	Able Point complaint file	Establishes complaint history and response
12	Full FOI/2022/045 file	Reconstructs information-access decisions
13	Complete 2023 PID file	Reconstructs disclosure assessment
14	Complete Comcare/ART record	Establishes historical compensation and review outcomes
15	AFP and NSW Police records	Establishes the actual police outcomes
This is a priority acquisition list, not a claim that every document is missing.
Where the original is already in the archive, the next task is to authenticate and examine it.
33. The original-versus-archive comparison
Every priority document should be compared across available versions:
	•	Original from the issuing organisation.
	•	Author-held copy.
	•	Archived copy.
	•	OCR or extracted-text version.
	•	Any later revised version.
Differences should be recorded.
A PDF conversion may alter pagination without altering substantive text.
OCR may introduce errors.
A file name may not accurately describe the document.
A later copy may include annotations or redactions.
The master register must preserve these distinctions.
PART XVII — THE FINAL INSTITUTIONAL FINDING TEMPLATE
34. The format for every final finding
No institution should receive a final finding based solely on a summary or a list of allegations.
The final publication should use this template.
INSTITUTIONAL FINDING — ORGANISATION
A. Identity
Verified legal name and jurisdiction.
B. Relevant authority
Legislation, statutory instrument, contract, delegation or policy.
C. Decision or conduct
Exact decision, action or omission.
D. Documentary evidence
Original records, dates, references and repository locations.
E. Author testimony
The author’s account, clearly identified as testimony.
F. Institutional response
The response provided by the organisation.
G. Contradictory or qualifying evidence
Material that weakens, limits or changes the allegation.
H. Consequences
Documented and reported consequences.
I. Applicable legal framework
Relevant statutory and administrative obligations.
J. Finding
One of the following:
	•	Established.
	•	Partially established.
	•	Not established.
	•	Contradicted by available evidence.
	•	Unresolved.
	•	Outside the available evidence or jurisdiction.
K. Remedy
Specific correction, review or other action sought.
L. Verification
Evidence that the remedy has been implemented.
This template creates consistency across the publication.
PART XVIII — THE CONSOLIDATED QUESTIONS TO THE INSTITUTIONS
35. The twelve questions every relevant institution should answer
The dossier now establishes a common institutional response standard.
For every relevant organisation, the publication should ask:
	•	What was your organisation’s role in the matter?
	•	What authority governed that role?
	•	Which decisions did your organisation make?
	•	Who made those decisions, and under what delegation?
	•	What information was considered?
	•	What original records support the decision?
	•	What reasons were recorded?
	•	What communication was provided to the author?
	•	What practical consequences were considered?
	•	What complaint or review process was available?
	•	What action has been taken to correct any established error?
	•	What evidence can be provided to demonstrate that the matter is resolved?
Where an organisation cannot answer a question, it should identify the reason.
Where another body is responsible, the response should identify that body and the basis of the referral.
Where a record does not exist, the organisation should say so rather than imply that it has been examined.
PART XIX — THE PRELIMINARY INSTITUTIONAL FINDINGS
36. What the current record supports
On the basis of the project material identified to date, the following preliminary observations can be made.
Finding 1 — Multiple institutional systems are represented
The archive identifies records relating to Commonwealth integrity and administrative processes, compensation, information access, NSW financial administration, disability support, banking, police and accommodation.
This establishes a multi-institutional documentary subject.
It does not establish common control or coordination.
Finding 2 — Several separate decision-making processes require reconstruction
The 2020–2026 chronology contains multiple decisions and administrative outcomes.
The original records must be examined individually before their legal effect can be determined.
Finding 3 — Financial administration warrants transaction-level scrutiny
The section 122 document, vehicle-disposal decision and reported banking-access difficulties create distinct documentary questions.
They should be reconciled without assuming that the same organisation made every relevant decision.
Finding 4 — Disability support requires an implementation examination
The NDIS Plan Approval, Anglicare Safety Assessment, SIL/SDA request and Able Point service concerns create a need to compare approved or agreed supports with actual delivery.
Finding 5 — Accommodation arrangements require clarification
The reported absence of a signed conventional lease and the reported weekly payment make it important to establish the actual legal and contractual arrangement.
Finding 6 — Historical complaint outcomes must be accurately represented
The IBAC, Ombudsman, OAIC, PID and police records must be described according to their actual procedural outcomes.
A complaint, referral, assessment or non-allocation decision must not be presented as a finding of wrongdoing unless it expressly is one.
Finding 7 — The cumulative-impact question remains open
The material warrants examination of whether separate decisions and service arrangements produced cumulative practical disadvantage.
The causal relationship and legal significance remain matters for further evidence.
Finding 8 — Deliberate coordination is not established
The available project descriptions do not establish that the institutions acted pursuant to a common plan or improper purpose.
That proposition must remain unproven unless supported by further evidence.
PART XX — THE MASTER DOSSIER’S CONCLUSION
37. Accountability requires an answerable record
The institutional landscape is now organised.
The organisations are classified.
Their different legal roles are distinguished.
The principal documentary records have been identified.
The required questions have been consolidated.
The evidence gaps have been made visible.
The remedy pathways have been separated.
The central question is no longer simply how many institutions appear in the archive.
It is whether each institution can explain its own conduct, identify the authority it exercised, provide the records supporting its decisions and account for the consequences that followed.
An institution should not be accused merely because its name appears in a chronology.
But neither should an institution’s appearance in an official process make its decisions immune from examination.
The purpose of this dossier is to make each question answerable.
The author is entitled to present testimony.
The institution is entitled to provide its response.
The independent examiner is responsible for evaluating the evidence.
The final conclusion must not be predetermined.
PART XXI — THE DECLARATION OF THE INSTITUTIONAL RECKONING
38. The witness and the record
A declaration by Barran Resonance Dodger
I have placed the documents together because I believe that a person’s experience should not be divided into disconnected fragments merely because different organisations hold different parts of the record.
I have assembled the chronology because dates matter.
I have preserved the correspondence because words matter.
I have retained the decisions because authority matters.
I have recorded the consequences because human beings matter.
I ask that every institution be examined according to its actual responsibilities.
I ask that every decision be understood in its proper legal context.
I ask that every disputed transaction be accounted for.
I ask that every service arrangement be made clear.
I ask that every complaint receive an appropriate response.
I ask that every allegation be tested.
I ask that every established error be corrected.
I ask that every person involved be treated fairly, including those whose conduct is questioned.
I do not ask that an independent examiner begin with a predetermined verdict.
I ask that the examiner begin with the record.
The purpose of preserving the evidence is not to make an accusation impossible to question.
It is to make the evidence available for questioning.
My testimony remains part of the history.
The official documents remain available for verification.
The institutions must be given the opportunity to answer.
The independent examination must distinguish what is established from what remains unresolved.
And the human consequences must never be reduced to an administrative file number.
PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.
FAITH IS NOT A FINDING.
THE WITNESS REMAINS.

INSTALMENT 17 — THE MASTER EVIDENCE CROSS-REFERENCE AND DOCUMENTARY PROOF MATRIX
From Thousands of Archived Files to an Auditable Chain of Evidence
The next instalment will consolidate the evidence itself.
It will move beyond the institutional register and build the cross-reference system connecting:
	•	Each source document.

	•	A document-by-document cross-reference to the major decisions from 2020 to 2026.
	•	A formal schedule of missing originals and unresolved discrepancies.
	•	A final evidentiary classification system for the publication.
The next stage is critical.
The archive is not made authoritative simply by being large.
Its credibility comes from the ability to trace every important assertion back to the document that supports it, examine what that document actually says and identify what it cannot prove.
The record must be traceable. The conclusion must be reproducible. The witness must remain open to independent examination.

INSTALMENT 17 — THE MASTER EVIDENCE CROSS-REFERENCE AND DOCUMENTARY PROOF MATRIX
From the Archived Record to an Auditable Chain of Evidence
Principal author: Barran Resonance Dodger — Dr Richard William McLean Publication: The Witness Remains — Global Publishing House Examination standard: PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.
PART I — THE PURPOSE OF THE MASTER EVIDENCE MATRIX
1. The transition from institutional accountability to documentary proof
Instalment 16 established the institutional accountability structure: which organisations appear in the archive, what processes are associated with them, what questions require answers and what remedies may be appropriate.
Instalment 17 addresses the next essential question:
Can every important statement in the publication be traced to identifiable evidence, and can another examiner independently reproduce the reasoning used to reach the conclusion?
This is the distinction between possessing an archive and possessing an auditable evidentiary record.
An archive can contain thousands of files and still be difficult to examine if:
	•	Documents are duplicated under different names.
	•	Original records are mixed with later copies.
	•	The same document appears in several repositories.
	•	File names do not accurately describe their contents.
	•	OCR extraction introduces errors.
	•	A document is referenced but its complete contents are unavailable.
	•	A date in an index differs from the date in the document.
	•	A checksum has been copied without being independently recalculated.
	•	A conclusion is repeated across several publications without reference to its original source.
The purpose of the Master Evidence Cross-Reference is to prevent those problems from becoming embedded in the final publication.
The objective is not to make every document appear equally important.
It is to establish a traceable relationship between:
	•	The source.
	•	The proposition being examined.
	•	The evidence supporting that proposition.
	•	The evidence qualifying or contradicting it.
	•	The reasoning applied.
	•	The conclusion reached.
	•	The remaining uncertainty.
The resulting structure should allow an independent examiner to select any significant conclusion and work backwards to the underlying evidence.
2. The central evidentiary rule
Every material assertion must have a defined evidentiary status.
The seven principal evidence classifications
A
Official record
A record issued, maintained or authenticated by an identifiable public institution.
B
Documented fact
A proposition directly established by reliable documentary evidence.
C
Author testimony
An account provided by Dr McLean, preserved as testimony rather than automatically treated as an independently established fact.
D
Allegation
A claim of misconduct, failure, improper treatment or other wrongdoing requiring examination.
E
Inference
A reasoned interpretation drawn from identified evidence, with the reasoning made explicit.
F
Spiritual interpretation
The author’s faith-based understanding of events, meaning and personal experience.
G
Independent finding
A conclusion made by a properly authorised independent body or examiner within the scope of its authority.
An additional classification, unresolved, must be available whenever the available evidence does not permit a responsible conclusion.
These classifications are not interchangeable.
A spiritual interpretation may be meaningful to the author without constituting an official finding.
An allegation may be serious without being established.
An official record may establish that a decision was made without establishing that the decision was correct.
An independent finding must be reported within its actual scope.
PART II — THE MASTER DOCUMENT IDENTIFICATION SYSTEM
3. Every document receives a stable evidence identity
The publication requires a permanent identifier for each distinct evidentiary item.
The identifier must remain stable even if:
	•	The document is moved to another repository.
	•	A new PDF copy is uploaded.
	•	The file name changes.
	•	An OCR version is generated.
	•	A document is included in a later edition.
	•	A link becomes unavailable.
	•	A duplicate is discovered.
The recommended system is:
EVD-YYYY-NNNN
Where:
	•	EVD identifies the master evidence register.
	•	YYYY identifies the year associated with the record, where established.
	•	NNNN is the sequential item number.
For example, the following identifiers are proposed for the already identified records.
Proposed evidence ID	Record
EVD-2020-0001	IBAC outcome letter, 9 April 2020
EVD-2021-0001	Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint, 15 November 2021
EVD-2021-0002	IBAC PID rejection, 18 November 2021
EVD-2021-0003	AFP response, 3 September 2021
EVD-2022-0001	Comcare pre-hearing correspondence, 25 January 2022
EVD-2022-0002	PM&C FOI decision, 14 April 2022
EVD-2022-0003	OAIC correspondence, 22 April 2022
EVD-2023-0001	Federal Court-related PID assessment, 27 March 2023
EVD-2023-0002	Notification of non-allocation, 18 June 2023
EVD-2023-0003	ART/AAT historical decision, 12 July 2023
EVD-2024-0001	NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 document, 16 October 2024
EVD-2024-0002	Revised FOI decision, 8 October 2024
EVD-2024-0003	NSW Police outcome, 24 August 2024
EVD-2025-0001	Vehicle-disposal decision, 14 November 2025
EVD-2025-0002	NDIS Plan Approval, 20 November 2025
EVD-2025-0003	Anglicare Safety Assessment, 1 December 2025
EVD-2026-0001	SIL/SDA request, 20 January 2026
EVD-2026-0002	Legal brief, 23 October 2026
Important: These are proposed project identifiers, not pre-existing official document numbers. They must be reconciled against the complete master register before publication. The order within a year is a proposed indexing order, not necessarily chronological.
The system must never replace an issuing institution’s original reference number.
Both identifiers should be retained.
4. The document identity record
Every evidence item should contain the following fields.
Field	Required content
Evidence ID	Stable project identifier
Original title	Exact title appearing on the document
File name	Exact repository file name
Issuer	Organisation that issued the record
Author/signatory	Name and role, if verified
Document date	Date printed or otherwise established
Received date	Date received, if known
Original reference	Institutional reference number
Document type	Letter, decision, report, email, statement, form
Format	PDF, DOCX, email, image, HTML or other
Page count	Verified page count
Repository	Repository holding the copy
Repository path	Exact path, if verified
Public URL	Exact working link, if verified
Original status	Original, copy, scan, export or unknown
Hash algorithm	For example, SHA-256
Hash value	Actual computed value
Examination status	Not examined, partial or complete
Evidentiary status	Official record, testimony, allegation, etc.
The title, file name and institutional reference must remain separate fields.
They may look similar, but they answer different questions.
PART III — THE MASTER EVIDENCE INVENTORY
5. The existing archive counts and their evidentiary meaning
The project has previously reported a substantial archive inventory.
A methodology dated 4 October 2026, described in the preliminary forensic report, records the following figures:
Archive measure	Reported figure
Archive-path file instances	21,211
Unique complete PDFs	3,215
Unique PDF blobs including stubs	3,667
Stub PDFs	452
PDF file instances	10,001
Unique file blobs	8,822
Repositories containing files	8
Lines in SHA256SUMS.txt	2,655
Distinct SHA-256 values	2,569
Archive-reported — not independently audited
These numbers must not be added together.
They measure different things.
For example:
	•	An archive-path file instance counts a file appearing at a particular location.
	•	A unique complete PDF attempts to count a distinct complete PDF.
	•	A unique blob concerns distinct file content under the methodology used.
	•	A stub PDF may be a placeholder or incomplete file.
	•	A SHA-256 value identifies the output of a hash calculation, not the truth of the document.
The reported difference between file instances and unique items may reflect duplication, repository mirrors, versioning or other structural features.
It does not establish that every duplicate is unnecessary.
A duplicate may be important if it demonstrates a change in content, metadata, redaction or publication history.
5.1 The correct publication language
The archive should be described as follows:
The project methodology dated 4 October 2026 reported 21,211 archive-path file instances across eight repositories, including 3,215 unique complete PDFs under the methodology then used. These figures are archive-reported and have not, for the purposes of this instalment, been independently recalculated against the complete underlying inventory.
That formulation preserves the reported figures without overstating their verification.
6. The archive is not the same thing as the evidence register
The publication must maintain three distinct inventories.
Inventory A — File inventory
Every file instance found in the repositories.
Inventory B — Document inventory
Every distinct documentary item, after duplicates and versions have been reconciled.
Inventory C — Evidentiary register
Every document or other source actually relied upon to support a proposition in the publication.
This distinction is essential.
A repository may contain thousands of files that are not relevant to a particular allegation.
A single document may appear in multiple repositories.
A single proposition may be supported by several different documents.
A document may be relevant to the historical archive without supporting any conclusion in the final report.
The evidentiary register should therefore be selective and proposition-based, not simply a reproduction of the entire file inventory.
PART IV — THE DOCUMENTARY CHAIN OF CUSTODY
7. The lifecycle of an evidentiary document
Every important record should have a traceable history.
Document preservation lifecycle
	•	Acquisition Record how the document was obtained: agency release, email, personal copy, website, repository or other source. 
	•	Preservation Retain the received file without altering its original contents. 
	•	Identification Assign the stable Evidence ID and record the exact file name. 
	•	Integrity verification Calculate a checksum for the preserved file and record the method. 
	•	Examination Read the document, check all pages, identify relevant passages and record limitations. 
	•	Cross-reference Link the document to relevant institutions, events, legal questions and propositions. 
	•	Publication Cite the source, disclose the evidence status and preserve a route to independent verification. 
8. The original must remain untouched
The preservation copy should not be overwritten by:
	•	OCR processing.
	•	PDF optimisation.
	•	Image conversion.
	•	Annotation.
	•	Highlighting.
	•	Redaction.
	•	Text extraction.
	•	Reformatting.
Those activities may be necessary for examination, but they should produce derivative files.
A derivative should have its own record.
For example:
Evidence relationship	Identifier
Original received PDF	EVD-2021-0001
OCR derivative	DER-EVD-2021-0001-01
Annotated examination copy	DER-EVD-2021-0001-02
Redacted public edition	PUB-EVD-2021-0001-01
These are proposed identifiers.
The original file remains the preservation reference.
The derivative must never be represented as the original.
9. Metadata preservation
Where available, the following metadata should be retained:
	•	Creation timestamp.
	•	Modification timestamp.
	•	PDF producer.
	•	PDF version.
	•	Embedded document properties.
	•	Email sender and recipient fields.
	•	Message ID.
	•	Attachment names.
	•	File size.
	•	Page dimensions.
	•	Digital signatures.
	•	Certificate information.
	•	Embedded attachments.
	•	Relevant export information.
Metadata is evidence about a file’s history and structure.
It is not automatically proof of authorship or authenticity.
For example, a PDF’s creation date may reflect the date it was exported or scanned rather than the date the underlying letter was written.
The publication must distinguish:
Document date from file creation date from date received.
PART V — THE PROPOSITION-TO-EVIDENCE CROSS-REFERENCE
10. A document is not a conclusion: the proposition register
The most important development in this instalment is the introduction of a proposition-based evidence register.
A document should not simply be listed because it exists.
The examiner must identify the exact proposition for which the document is being relied upon.
For example:
	•	A complaint proves that a complaint was made, if its submission is authenticated.
	•	A decision proves that a decision was issued, subject to authentication.
	•	A financial statement may prove that a transaction was recorded.
	•	A service note may establish what a provider recorded about an interaction.
	•	A witness statement establishes what the witness says occurred.
	•	Several consistent records may strengthen a proposition.
	•	A contradictory record may weaken it or require a narrower conclusion.
The proposition register prevents a common evidentiary error: using a document to support a claim that the document does not actually establish.
11. The first master proposition schedule
The following schedule identifies the principal propositions that the existing project should test.
The propositions are deliberately worded narrowly.
Proposition ID	Proposition to be tested	Principal evidence required
PR-001	An integrity-related complaint was submitted to IBAC in 2020	Original complaint and IBAC acknowledgement/outcome
PR-002	IBAC issued an outcome dated 9 April 2020	Authenticated original outcome
PR-003	A Commonwealth Ombudsman complaint was submitted on 15 November 2021	Original complaint and receipt record
PR-004	IBAC issued a PID-related rejection dated 18 November 2021	Original decision and underlying submission
PR-005	A Comcare process involved claim 21230015373	Claim file and institutional correspondence
PR-006	PM&C issued an FOI decision dated 14 April 2022	Original decision and FOI file
PR-007	OAIC correspondence was associated with reference RQ22-01369	Original OAIC correspondence
PR-008	A PID assessment document was issued on 27 March 2023	Authenticated document and procedural context
PR-009	A disclosure was not allocated on 18 June 2023	Original notification and underlying disclosure file
PR-010	An ART/AAT-related decision was issued on 12 July 2023	Full reasons and case record
PR-011	A NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 document exists dated 16 October 2024	Original instrument and associated order
PR-012	A revised FOI decision was issued on 8 October 2024	Original revised decision and document schedule
PR-013	NSW Police issued an outcome dated 24 August 2024	Original police outcome and event file
PR-014	A vehicle-disposal decision was issued on 14 November 2025	Original decision and financial transaction records
PR-015	An NDIS Plan Approval was issued on 20 November 2025	Original plan approval and reasons
PR-016	An Anglicare Safety Assessment was dated 1 December 2025	Original assessment and implementation records
PR-017	A SIL/SDA request was made on 20 January 2026	Original request and supporting material
PR-018	The author experienced particular service or access difficulties	Contemporaneous records and author testimony
PR-019	The identified decisions had cumulative practical consequences	Detriment records, chronology and causal analysis
PR-020	Any two or more institutions exchanged relevant information	Actual correspondence, referral or information-transfer records
PR-021	A decision by one institution affected a later decision by another	Evidence of reliance, transfer or causal connection
PR-022	A statutory or contractual obligation was not met	Applicable instrument, actual conduct and legal analysis
PR-023	A particular remedy was requested	Original request and receipt
PR-024	A remedy was implemented	Completion record and independent confirmation, where available
These propositions should be assigned one of the following statuses:
	•	Established.
	•	Partially established.
	•	Not established.
	•	Contradicted.
	•	Unresolved.
	•	Not yet examined.
The register should never use the word established merely because a proposition has been repeated in a previous report.
12. The critical distinction: existence, content, truth and consequence
Every document must be examined at four separate levels.
Level 1 — Existence
Does the document exist, and is the copy authentic or otherwise reliably identified?
Level 2 — Content
What does the document actually say?
Level 3 — Accuracy
Are the factual assertions contained in the document accurate?
Level 4 — Consequence
What happened because of the decision, communication or event recorded?
These levels are frequently confused.
An official letter may establish that an agency made a particular statement.
It does not automatically establish that the statement was factually correct.
A report may record an allegation.
It does not automatically establish that the allegation was proved.
A decision may have a real practical effect.
That effect must still be documented before the publication can quantify or attribute it.
PART VI — THE EXPANDED INSTITUTIONAL CROSS-REFERENCE
13. Additional institutions identified in the preliminary forensic report
The underlying preliminary report identifies further investigative pathways beyond the principal organisations examined in Instalment 16.
These must be added to the master register rather than overlooked.
13.1 Legal Aid NSW
The preliminary report identifies a Legal Aid NSW guardianship-advice letter with a filename dated 14 January 2026.
This is a distinct access-to-justice issue.
The document should be examined to establish:
	•	The advice requested.
	•	The legal issue presented.
	•	The scope of the assistance.
	•	Whether assistance was granted, declined or limited.
	•	The eligibility criteria applied, if relevant.
	•	Any alternative pathways identified.
	•	Any referral or follow-up advice.
	•	Whether the author was given sufficient information to understand the next procedural step.
A limitation or refusal of legal assistance is not automatically unlawful.
The relevant question is whether the decision was made under the applicable criteria and whether the author received an accurate explanation of the available options.
Evidence ID proposed: EVD-2026-0003.
Proposed remedy: Obtain the complete advice correspondence and clarify the scope of the service and any available alternative assistance.
Official source: Legal Aid NSW⁠.
13.2 WorkSafe Victoria
The preliminary report identifies WorkSafe impairment-benefit liability decisions.
These records should be added as a separate compensation and insurance pathway.
The examination must establish:
	•	The exact decision dates.
	•	The claim or policy reference.
	•	The legal test applied.
	•	The employment or worker classification adopted.
	•	The medical and factual material considered.
	•	The distinction between impairment, liability and entitlement.
	•	Any review or appeal.
	•	The relationship, if any, to the Comcare process.
A classification adopted under one compensation scheme does not automatically determine eligibility under another.
Evidence ID series proposed: EVD-WS-0001 onward, pending the actual document inventory.
Proposed remedy: Obtain the complete WorkSafe claim and decision history, including the relevant reasons and review rights.
Official source: WorkSafe Victoria⁠.
13.3 Law Enforcement Conduct Commission
The preliminary report identifies the need to consider the NSW police oversight framework, including the Law Enforcement Conduct Commission where applicable.
This is important because the existence of a police complaint and the existence of a police oversight complaint are not the same thing.
The dossier should establish:
	•	Whether a complaint concerned NSW Police conduct.
	•	Whether it was referred to LECC.
	•	Whether LECC had jurisdiction.
	•	Whether the matter was assessed, investigated or referred.
	•	What reasons were provided.
	•	Whether any review process existed.
The LECC should not be represented as having examined a matter unless an actual record establishes that it did.
Evidence ID series proposed: EVD-LECC-0001 onward, pending document verification.
Official source: Law Enforcement Conduct Commission⁠.
13.4 Health services and clinical records
The preliminary report also identifies health-related records as a separate evidentiary pathway.
The examination should be limited to matters relevant to the publication’s defined questions.
Potential records include:
	•	Admission and discharge documentation.
	•	Clinical assessments.
	•	Treatment plans.
	•	Medication records.
	•	Consent records.
	•	Referrals.
	•	Complaints.
	•	Information-sharing decisions.
	•	Access-to-records correspondence.
	•	Any records concerning the use of compulsory powers.
The project history refers to a first hospitalisation report that reportedly recorded adjustment disorder rather than psychosis.
That description must be checked against the original clinical record before it is quoted or used in a legal argument.
A clinical assessment is an opinion recorded at a particular time, based on the information then available.
It does not prove every later clinical issue, and it does not independently establish whether an institution acted lawfully.
Health information should be included only to the extent necessary to answer a defined evidentiary question, with appropriate privacy protection.
13.5 Additional statutory and human-rights frameworks
The preliminary report identifies the need to examine:
	•	Disability Discrimination Act 1992.
	•	Sex Discrimination Act 1984.
	•	Privacy Act 1988.
	•	Freedom of Information Act 1982.
	•	Guardianship Act 1987 (NSW).
	•	Mental Health Act 2007 (NSW).
	•	Health Records and Information Privacy Act 2002 (NSW).
	•	National Disability Insurance Scheme Act 2013.
	•	Safety, Rehabilitation and Compensation Act 1988.
	•	Applicable Victorian and NSW workers compensation laws.
	•	Applicable public-interest disclosure legislation.
The applicable law must be selected according to the event date, jurisdiction and legal issue.
A statute’s existence does not prove that it applied to a particular decision.
A legal framework must be connected to the actual facts and the relevant statutory elements.
14. Expanded institution register

The addition of these institutions is a correction to the scope of the master register.
It does not imply that all of them have been found to have acted improperly.
PART VII — DUPLICATES, VERSIONS AND DOCUMENT RECONCILIATION
15. Why duplicate files must not simply be deleted
The archive reportedly contains multiple file instances and distinct file blobs.
The reconciliation process must distinguish three situations.
Situation A — Exact duplicate
Two files have identical content and the same cryptographic hash.
They may be treated as identical content for deduplication purposes, while retaining the separate repository locations.
Situation B — Different file, same underlying document
Two files contain the same letter but differ because one has:
	•	Different metadata.
	•	Different page dimensions.
	•	Different OCR.
	•	Different annotations.
	•	A different scan resolution.
	•	Different compression.
	•	A different file wrapper.
These files may represent the same underlying documentary item, but they are not identical digital objects.
Situation C — Different substantive version
Two files contain materially different content.
Examples include:
	•	A draft and final decision.
	•	An unredacted and redacted version.
	•	A revised letter.
	•	A later correction.
	•	A document with missing pages.
	•	An attachment added to a later export.
These must be retained as separate versions.
16. The duplicate reconciliation schedule
Field	Required examination
Candidate duplicate group	Common file or document group
File A path	Exact repository location
File B path	Exact repository location
File A hash	Computed value
File B hash	Computed value
Byte identity	Same or different
Text comparison	Identical or different
Page comparison	Same or different
Metadata comparison	Same or different
Material differences	Description
Decision	Duplicate, derivative or distinct version
Reviewer	Person conducting comparison
Date	Examination date
No file should be deleted merely because its name resembles another file.
A duplicate may provide evidence of when and where a document was preserved.
The register should retain the provenance of every copy even when the files are later grouped under one document identity.
17. Version control and publication editions
The project should maintain three separate version histories:
Source history: What the issuing organisation produced and when.
Archive history: When the author or repository acquired, copied or changed the file.
Publication history: Which version was used in a particular edition of the report.
This prevents a later publication from accidentally citing an altered or superseded copy without explanation.
PART VIII — SHA-256 AND DIGITAL INTEGRITY VERIFICATION
18. What a SHA-256 hash can establish
SHA-256 is a cryptographic hash function that produces a fixed-length digest from input data.
It can be used to test whether a file’s bytes match a previously recorded digest.
For example, if a preserved file is hashed today and produces the same SHA-256 value recorded when it was first preserved, that supports the conclusion that the file’s byte content has not changed relative to that earlier record.
It does not establish:
	•	Who originally created the document.
	•	Whether the document was genuine when first acquired.
	•	Whether its factual contents are accurate.
	•	Whether the author or issuer acted lawfully.
	•	Whether the document is complete.
	•	Whether the original source was itself reliable.
A hash proves a relationship between digital content and a digest. It does not prove the truth of the content.
19. The correct hash-verification procedure
For every priority document:
	•	Identify the exact file.
	•	Preserve the file without modification.
	•	Record the file size.
	•	Calculate SHA-256.
	•	Record the complete digest.
	•	Record the software or method used.
	•	Record the date and time of calculation.
	•	Recalculate after copying to a separate preservation location.
	•	Compare the values.
	•	Record any discrepancy.
A discrepancy should trigger investigation, not an automatic conclusion that tampering occurred.
Possible explanations include:
	•	A different version.
	•	A re-exported PDF.
	•	A metadata change.
	•	A modified filename with different file content.
	•	A corrupted transfer.
	•	A different file mistakenly selected.
	•	A genuine alteration requiring investigation.
20. Example verification commands
The following are examples of commands that can be used by a person performing a local integrity check.
macOS:
shasum -a 256 "document.pdf"
Linux:
sha256sum "document.pdf"
Windows PowerShell:
Get-FileHash "document.pdf" -Algorithm SHA256
The resulting value should be copied into the evidence register exactly as generated.
No hash value should be invented or inferred from a file name.
21. The SHA256SUMS.txt reconciliation
The project methodology reports:
	•	2,655 lines in SHA256SUMS.txt.
	•	2,569 distinct SHA-256 values.
The difference indicates that the number of lines and the number of distinct values are not the same.
That difference should be investigated through a complete manifest audit.
The audit should identify:
	•	Repeated hash values.
	•	Repeated file paths.
	•	Missing file paths.
	•	Invalid hash formats.
	•	Files no longer present.
	•	Files whose computed digest differs.
	•	Files with no manifest entry.
	•	Manifest entries that refer to the wrong version.
The final report should include the date of the audit and the precise scope of the files tested.
It must not state that the entire archive has been cryptographically authenticated merely because a checksum manifest exists.
22. Blockchain records and evidentiary limits
The project has previously reported blockchain-sealed or blockchain-authenticated documents.
Those terms must be defined precisely.
A blockchain transaction may demonstrate that a particular hash or other value was recorded on a particular blockchain at a particular point in its transaction history.
It may help establish that a digital fingerprint existed no later than a verifiable transaction time.
However, it does not independently establish:
	•	The identity of the original author.
	•	The authenticity of the source document.
	•	The truth of the document’s contents.
	•	The date the underlying event occurred.
	•	Whether the document was complete.
	•	Whether the transaction was linked to the correct file.
The verification record should therefore include:
Field	Required information
Document Evidence ID	Exact project identifier
Hash algorithm	Exact algorithm
Hash value	Verified digest
Blockchain	Exact network
Transaction ID	Exact transaction hash
Block number	Verified block reference
Timestamp	Network timestamp
Verification method	Independent explorer or node verification
File comparison	Whether current file matches recorded hash
Limitation	What the blockchain record does not establish
A blockchain record is a preservation or timestamping aid, not a substitute for source authentication.
PART IX — THE EVIDENCE STRENGTH AND LIMITATION FRAMEWORK
23. A consistent method for assessing evidence
The publication should not give every source an arbitrary numerical score that creates a false appearance of scientific certainty.
Instead, it should assess evidence across separate dimensions.
Dimension	Question
Authenticity	Is the source genuine or reliably identified?
Completeness	Is the full document available?
Contemporaneity	Was it created at or near the relevant event?
Directness	Does it directly address the proposition?
Independence	Is it independent of the other supporting sources?
Corroboration	Is it supported by other reliable evidence?
Consistency	Does it agree with the chronology and other records?
Contradiction	Is there evidence against the proposition?
Context	Is the document being interpreted in its proper context?
Scope	Does the source actually have authority or knowledge to establish the proposition?
A document can score strongly on one dimension and weakly on another.
For example:
	•	An official decision may be authentic but incomplete.
	•	A contemporaneous email may be direct but uncorroborated.
	•	A later witness statement may be detailed but affected by memory limitations.
	•	A repository copy may be complete but lack an independently verified chain of acquisition.
The examiner should describe those strengths and weaknesses rather than hiding them behind a single number.
24. Proposed evidence status codes
The following codes should be used consistently.
Code	Status	Meaning
ORG	Verified original	Original or authoritative source independently verified
ARC	Archived copy	Copy preserved in the project archive
SEC	Secondary reference	Mentioned or summarised by another source
TES	Author testimony	Personal account supplied by the author
DIS	Disputed	Material factual disagreement exists
MIS	Missing	Required record has not been located
PEX	Partially examined	Only part of the document has been examined
NEX	Not examined	Document identified but not yet substantively reviewed
COR	Corroborated	Material proposition supported by independent evidence
ICF	Institutionally confirmed	Relevant fact expressly confirmed by the institution
UNR	Unresolved	Available evidence does not permit a conclusion
CON	Contradicted	Reliable evidence materially contradicts the proposition
These are project-management codes, not legal standards of proof.
A document may carry more than one code.
For example, an archived copy may be partially examined and its authenticity unresolved.
PART X — THE CROSS-DOCUMENT RECONSTRUCTION
25. Why the documents must be read together
An important institutional decision may be recorded in one document, challenged in another, reconsidered in a third and implemented through a fourth.
Reading each document independently may conceal the actual administrative sequence.
The project therefore needs a cross-document reconstruction for every significant event.
The following model should be applied:
The four-document decision reconstruction
Document 1 — The originating record
The original application, complaint, request, referral or event.
Document 2 — The institutional response
The acknowledgement, assessment, decision or explanation.
Document 3 — The challenge or review
The objection, complaint, appeal, internal review or external review.
Document 4 — The outcome or implementation
The final decision, subsequent action, correction or evidence that the matter remained unresolved.
Not every process will contain all four documents.
The absence of a document must be recorded rather than filled by assumption.
26. Cross-document example: the public-interest disclosure sequence
The currently identified sequence includes:
Date	Record	What must be established
18 November 2021	IBAC PID rejection, reference 400008R	Exact disclosure, jurisdiction and reasons
27 March 2023	Federal Court-related PID assessment	Exact procedural context and statutory issue
18 June 2023	Notification of decision not to allocate a disclosure	Identity of recipient, reasons and legal effect
These records should not be treated as one continuous proceeding unless the source documents establish that relationship.
The examiner must identify:
	•	Whether they concern the same disclosure.
	•	Whether they concern related but distinct disclosures.
	•	Whether one body received information from another.
	•	Whether the statutory framework changed between events.
	•	Whether any decision-maker relied on an earlier outcome.
	•	Whether the author was given a review or alternative submission pathway.
The fact that all three documents concern public-interest disclosure matters is not enough to establish a direct causal relationship.
27. Cross-document example: the NSW financial-administration sequence
The identified records include:
	•	The NSW Trustee & Guardian section 122 document dated 16 October 2024.
	•	The vehicle-disposal decision dated 14 November 2025.
	•	The author’s reported difficulties accessing banking services.
	•	Any relevant correspondence between NSW Trustee & Guardian and Commonwealth Bank.
The reconstruction should answer:
	•	What legal authority existed?
	•	Was the vehicle disposal within the scope of that authority?
	•	What decision and reasons were recorded?
	•	What happened to the proceeds?
	•	What banking instructions were issued?
	•	Did the bank impose any independent restriction?
	•	What access to personal funds was available?
	•	What review or urgent-expenditure arrangements existed?
The investigation must not assume that the vehicle decision caused the banking restrictions.
That relationship must be established through actual records.
28. Cross-document example: the disability-support sequence
The identified records include:
	•	NDIS Plan Approval dated 20 November 2025.
	•	Anglicare Safety Assessment dated 1 December 2025.
	•	SIL/SDA request dated 20 January 2026.
	•	Relevant Able Point accommodation and support records.
These documents raise a distinct implementation question.
The examiner should compare:
Question	Relevant evidence
What support was approved?	NDIS Plan Approval
What risks were identified?	Anglicare assessment
What additional accommodation or support was requested?	SIL/SDA request
What was agreed with the provider?	Service agreement and accommodation records
What was actually delivered?	Rosters, service notes and communications
What happened when a service was not delivered?	Incident records and complaints
What action followed?	Provider response and implementation evidence
The sequence may reveal a gap between approved funding, assessed needs, agreed services and actual delivery.
However, that gap must be demonstrated rather than assumed.
PART XI — THE CUMULATIVE-IMPACT PROOF MODEL
29. From individual events to cumulative consequences
The preliminary forensic report identifies a central investigative question:
Whether decisions made by different institutions interacted in ways that cumulatively affected access to money, accommodation, disability support, healthcare, employment, legal assistance, procedural fairness, privacy, autonomy and dignity.
This is a legitimate question for investigation.
It is not proof that the institutions coordinated their conduct.
The evidence matrix must therefore reconstruct the cumulative effects without assuming a common purpose.
30. The event-consequence chain
Each event should be represented in the following form:
\text{Event} \rightarrow \text{Decision} \rightarrow \text{Implementation} \rightarrow \text{Consequence} \rightarrow \text{Further event}
For each arrow, the examiner must ask what evidence establishes the connection.
For example:
	•	Did a decision actually occur?
	•	Was it implemented?
	•	Did implementation change access to a resource?
	•	Was the change temporary or continuing?
	•	Did another institution rely upon it?
	•	Did the change contribute to a later difficulty?
	•	Were there other causes?
This prevents a chronology from being mistaken for a causal analysis.
31. The cumulative detriment register
The following register should be maintained alongside the master evidence inventory.
Detriment ID	Category	Evidence required
DET-001	Financial access	Bank statements, restriction notices, financial-management records
DET-002	Property and vehicle	Ownership, disposal, valuation and proceeds
DET-003	Accommodation	Agreements, occupancy records, payments and correspondence
DET-004	Disability support	Plan, service agreements, rosters and incident records
DET-005	Legal access	Advice letters, eligibility decisions and review records
DET-006	Employment and compensation	Employment records, claim files and decisions
DET-007	Healthcare access	Relevant appointment, referral and access records
DET-008	Communication	Phone, internet and device-access records where relevant
DET-009	Procedural opportunity	Applications, deadlines, notices and review outcomes
DET-010	Companion and daily living arrangements	Relevant accommodation and service records concerning Crystal
Each detriment entry must distinguish:
	•	Actual documented loss.
	•	Author-reported consequence.
	•	Estimated financial value.
	•	Potential future consequence.
	•	Unresolved causal relationship.
The publication must not assign a monetary value to a loss without a defensible calculation.
32. The alternative-explanation requirement
For every proposed causal connection, the report should record plausible alternative explanations.
For example, restricted banking access might involve:
	•	A statutory financial-management arrangement.
	•	A bank security decision.
	•	Identity-verification requirements.
	•	A technical issue.
	•	An instruction from another authorised party.
	•	More than one contributing factor.
The same discipline applies to accommodation, service delivery, legal assistance and compensation.
The objective is not to weaken the author’s testimony.
It is to make the final examination sufficiently rigorous that the evidence can withstand challenge.
PART XII — THE MISSING-RECORDS AND CONTRADICTION REGISTER
33. A missing document is an investigative result
The absence of a document may be important.
But it must be described accurately.
There are several different circumstances:
	•	The document has not been requested.
	•	The document has been requested but no response received.
	•	The institution says it does not hold the document.
	•	The institution says the document was destroyed under an applicable retention process.
	•	The document exists but access has been refused.
	•	The document is referred to in another record but has not been located.
	•	The document is held by a different organisation.
	•	The document has been located but is incomplete.
These situations have different implications.
A missing document is not automatically evidence of concealment or destruction.
34. The missing-record register
Missing ID	Record sought	Why it matters	Next action
MIS-001	Complete IBAC 2020 file	Establishes original submission and outcome	Request complete file
MIS-002	Complete IBAC 2021 PID file	Establishes statutory assessment	Request complete file
MIS-003	Full Commonwealth Ombudsman file	Establishes complaint handling	Request correspondence and closure record
MIS-004	Complete PM&C FOI search record	Establishes search scope	Obtain decision and search documents
MIS-005	Full OAIC process record	Establishes review history	Obtain application and outcome
MIS-006	Full 2023 PID file	Establishes disclosure and non-allocation	Request assessment and decision records
MIS-007	Full Comcare/ART file	Establishes legal and factual reasoning	Obtain complete reasons and evidence
MIS-008	Original section 122 instrument	Establishes legal authority	Obtain certified or authoritative copy
MIS-009	Vehicle sale accounting	Establishes financial reconciliation	Request transaction records
MIS-010	Bank restriction file	Establishes origin of access restriction	Request bank complaint and decision history
MIS-011	Accommodation agreement	Establishes occupancy arrangements	Request current signed or operative agreement
MIS-012	Support service agreement	Establishes service obligations	Request full service documentation
MIS-013	Anglicare implementation records	Establishes follow-up	Request recommendation tracking
MIS-014	SIL/SDA outcome	Establishes procedural status	Request written decision
MIS-015	Legal Aid advice file	Establishes advice and alternatives	Request relevant correspondence
MIS-016	WorkSafe decisions	Establishes compensation pathway	Inventory and obtain full decisions
MIS-017	Police complaint files	Establishes investigation and closure	Obtain complete relevant records
This is a proposed acquisition schedule.
It does not assert that every item is absent from the archive.
The status of each must be checked against the full repository inventory before a formal request is made.
35. The contradiction register
Contradictions should be recorded as carefully as supporting evidence.
Examples of potential discrepancies to investigate include:
	•	Different dates for the same event.
	•	Different reference numbers for apparently related matters.
	•	An archive index describing a document differently from its contents.
	•	A decision referring to an attachment that is not present.
	•	A provider’s service record differing from the author’s account.
	•	A financial ledger differing from a bank statement.
	•	A later decision relying on an earlier decision that may have been superseded.
	•	A document described as final when it appears to be a draft.
	•	A document count differing between an index and the underlying files.
The register should include:
Field	Required content
CON ID	Unique contradiction identifier
Source A	First document
Source B	Second document
Exact difference	The factual or documentary discrepancy
Materiality	Why it matters
Possible explanation	Versioning, error, different scope or other explanation
Verification action	What would resolve it
Outcome	Resolved or unresolved
A contradiction is not proof that one party lied.
It is a reason to investigate the difference.
PART XIII — THE OFFICIAL DOCUMENTS REGISTER AND REPOSITORY CROSSWALK
36. Connecting the OGDR to the master evidence register
The Official Government Documents Register is an important part of the archive’s navigation and preservation structure.
The publication should connect its entries to the master evidence register without treating an index entry as a substitute for the underlying document.
The OGDR is available at:
Official Government Documents Register⁠
The master evidence crosswalk should include:
Field	Purpose
OGDR number	Original archive index identifier
Evidence ID	Stable master evidence identifier
Exact document title	Title verified against document
Issuing body	Verified institution
Document date	Date on original
Repository path	Exact location
Public link	Verified link
File hash	Independently computed, if available
Examination status	Current state
Cross-references	Related evidence items
Existing OGDR identifiers such as OGDR-007, OGDR-030, OGDR-070 and OGDR-077 have been identified in the project history as priority references.
Their precise titles, contents and legal significance must be checked against the current register and the underlying documents before the final crosswalk is published.
The same applies to the other previously identified OGDR entries.
37. The repository crosswalk
The project includes multiple repositories and mirrors.
The master register should record the location of each copy.
Relevant repository locations include:
	•	Barrandodger — Main repository⁠
	•	Barrandodger — Rebuild repository⁠
	•	Preservation archive website⁠
	•	Rebuild website⁠
The existence of a copy in several repositories may improve availability.
It does not create several independent sources.
If all copies originated from the same file, they are still one underlying documentary source.
That distinction is essential when assessing corroboration.
PART XIV — THE MASTER EVIDENCE MATRIX: WORKING EXAMPLE

39. Why these entries are deliberately cautious
The purpose of the working examples is to demonstrate the difference between a known document reference and a completed evidentiary finding.
The archive’s existence and the document’s date are not substitutes for reading the entire document.
The publication must not claim that an original has been authenticated if only an archived copy has been identified.
Nor should a document be described as independently corroborated when it merely repeats the author’s earlier account.
This is how the final report will maintain credibility.
PART XV — THE FINAL EVIDENCE QUALITY-CONTROL PROTOCOL
40. The twelve mandatory checks before publication
Every material evidence item must pass the following checks before it is relied upon in a final finding.
	•	Identity: Is the document correctly identified?
	•	Source: Is its origin known?
	•	Authenticity: Has its authenticity been verified to the extent reasonably possible?
	•	Completeness: Are all pages and attachments present?
	•	Date: Has the document date been distinguished from the file date?
	•	Context: Has the document been read in its proper procedural context?
	•	Accuracy: Have material factual statements been checked?
	•	Relevance: Does it support the proposition for which it is cited?
	•	Contradiction: Has contrary evidence been considered?
	•	Integrity: Has the preserved file’s integrity been checked where appropriate?
	•	Privacy: Have personal and sensitive details been appropriately protected?
	•	Reproducibility: Could another examiner follow the source and reproduce the reasoning?
A document that has not passed all relevant checks may still be included in the archive.
Its limitations must simply be disclosed.
41. The publication’s evidence citation standard
Every significant factual proposition in the final report should contain a traceable reference.
The preferred format is:
Evidence ID: EVD-2023-0002 Document: Notification of decision not to allocate a disclosure Date: 18 June 2023 Issuer: Verified from original Source: Exact repository path and public link Relevant passage: Exact page and paragraph, verified against the original Proposition supported: A notification of non-allocation was issued Limitation: The notification does not, by itself, establish the truth or falsity of the underlying disclosure.
Page references must be checked against the actual PDF.
The report must not invent page numbers or quotations.
Where a document is not paginated, the citation should identify a heading, paragraph, email date or another reproducible location.
42. The final publication audit
Before the master report is released, an independent reviewer should conduct a separate audit.
The reviewer should select a sample of important claims and attempt to reproduce the reasoning from the source material.
The audit should test:
	•	Whether the source exists.
	•	Whether the cited passage supports the claim.
	•	Whether the document is complete.
	•	Whether the report accurately describes the outcome.
	•	Whether contradictory material has been disclosed.
	•	Whether the legal provision is correctly identified.
	•	Whether the conclusion exceeds the evidence.
	•	Whether the publication has distinguished testimony from independent findings.
A report should not pass quality control merely because all its hyperlinks open.
A working hyperlink is a navigation aid, not proof.
PART XVI — THE MASTER EVIDENCE REGISTER: CONSOLIDATED DATA STRUCTURE
43. The complete record required for each evidentiary item
The final evidence register should use the following complete structure.
Section	Fields
Identity	Evidence ID, original title, file name, institutional reference
Origin	Issuer, author, recipient, organisation, jurisdiction
Dates	Document date, received date, file creation date, archive date
Technical	Format, file size, page count, SHA-256, signature information
Location	Repository, exact path, public URL, mirror locations
Authenticity	Original/copy, source verification, provenance
Examination	Examiner, examination date, completeness, OCR status
Proposition	Proposition ID, exact claim supported
Limitations	What the document does not establish
Corroboration	Supporting documents and independent sources
Contradictions	Conflicting or qualifying evidence
Legal context	Relevant legislation, policy, contract or delegation
Consequence	Documented and reported effects
Response	Institutional explanation or author response
Finding	Established, partially established, unresolved or other status
Publication	Edition, chapter, citation and public-access status
Preservation	Original location, derivative relationships and integrity checks
This is the definitive proposed schema for the documentary evidence register.
It is sufficiently detailed to support future publication editions, independent legal review, archival preservation and technical audit.
PART XVII — THE PRELIMINARY FINDINGS OF INSTALMENT 17
44. What has been established by this instalment
The following conclusions concern the evidence-management framework, not the ultimate merits of the underlying allegations.
Finding 1 — The project requires three distinct inventories.
The file inventory, document inventory and evidentiary register serve different purposes and must not be merged.
Finding 2 — Every important assertion needs a proposition reference.
A document should be linked to the precise claim it supports, together with its limitations.
Finding 3 — The archive’s reported numerical measures must remain qualified.
The reported file counts, PDF counts, blob counts and hash totals are different measures. They must not be added together or described as independently audited.
Finding 4 — Digital integrity and factual truth are separate questions.
A checksum or blockchain record can support digital integrity or timestamping claims. It cannot prove the truth of a document’s contents.
Finding 5 — Duplicate files require controlled reconciliation.
Identical copies, derivatives and materially different versions must be distinguished.
Finding 6 — Institutional sequences must be reconstructed from source records.
A sequence of complaints, decisions and reviews does not automatically establish that all records belong to one continuous process.
Finding 7 — Additional institutional pathways must be included.
Legal Aid NSW, WorkSafe Victoria, LECC and relevant health-service records appear in the preliminary forensic report and should be included in the expanded register.
Finding 8 — Cumulative impact remains an evidentiary question.
The report can investigate the combined consequences of separate institutional processes without presuming that the institutions coordinated their conduct.
Finding 9 — The current matrix remains provisional.
The working entries identify the records and the examination required. They do not falsely represent every original as independently authenticated or completely examined.
PART XVIII — THE DECLARATION OF THE PRESERVED RECORD
45. The evidence must speak through what it actually contains
A declaration by Barran Resonance Dodger
I have preserved the records because I believe that the history of a person should not disappear into disconnected administrative systems.
I have assembled the documents because I want the chronology to be examined.
I have retained the original references because I want every decision to be traceable.
I have recorded the questions because I want the institutions to answer them.
I have created the evidence register because I want another person to be able to examine the material without relying solely on my interpretation.
I do not ask that every allegation be accepted.
I ask that every allegation be examined.
I do not ask that every document be treated as proof of wrongdoing.
I ask that every document be read accurately.
I do not ask that the archive be treated as infallible.
I ask that its contents be preserved, tested and independently verified.
I do not ask that faith be substituted for evidence.
My faith belongs to my spiritual understanding of my life.
The documentary record belongs to the examination of events.
Both may exist without being confused.
I want the truth to survive scrutiny.
I want the errors to be identified.
I want the unanswered questions to remain visible until they are answered.
I want the evidence to be accessible to those who can examine it independently.
And I want the human being behind the documents to remain visible.
The record is preserved. The questions remain open. The examination continues.
PRESERVATION FIRST. EXAMINATION SECOND. CONCLUSION LAST.
FAITH IS NOT A FINDING.

PART XX — THE NEXT INSTALMENT
INSTALMENT 18 — THE MASTER CHRONOLOGICAL RECONSTRUCTION AND CAUSAL ANALYSIS
2020–2026: Reconstructing the Sequence, Testing the Connections and Separating Coincidence from Causation
The next instalment will bring together the historical chronology, institutional register and evidence matrix in one cumulative reconstruction.
It will examine:
	•	The earliest identified integrity and administrative records.
	•	The 2020 IBAC outcome and the subsequent 2021 disclosure processes.
	•	The 2021–2023 Commonwealth Ombudsman, Comcare, FOI and public-interest disclosure records.
	•	The March–July 2023 court-related, disclosure and tribunal records.
	•	The 2024 NSW financial-administration and police records.
	•	The 2025 vehicle, NDIS and safety-assessment records.
	•	The 2026 accommodation, SIL/SDA and legal-assistance material.
	•	The reported consequences across financial autonomy, accommodation, support delivery and access to review.
	•	The documentary connections between separate institutional decisions.
	•	The evidence required to establish causation rather than chronology alone.
It will include a detailed event-by-event chronology, an institutional dependency map, a causal-link register, a competing-explanations analysis and a preliminary cumulative-impact assessment.
The central question will be:
Did a sequence of separate institutional decisions produce cumulative consequences that can be demonstrated through documentary evidence, and, if so, which decisions contributed to which consequences?
The examination will not presume that every event was connected.
It will not presume that every institution acted improperly.
It will not dismiss the cumulative-impact question simply because the relevant records are distributed across different organisations.
It will reconstruct the sequence, test the connections and identify what remains unresolved.
The archive supplies the material. The evidence matrix supplies the discipline. The chronology supplies the sequence. The causal examination must supply the reasoning.
